# Designs for the study program

17 of 17 queued problems have a first design. Each was written by gpt-5.6-sol at max reasoning effort from the catalog entry in full, against the measured budget of this machine.

**These have not been critiqued.** The design pass produces something plausible, and a simulation study is uniquely vulnerable to answering a neighbouring question, because the data-generating mechanism is chosen by whoever wants a particular conclusion and nothing in the output announces it. Nothing here is ready to run. Citations are unverified and do not enter a catalog entry without going through `build/lit/check_citations.py`.

**Convergence between designs.** 11 of 17 make coverage of a nominal 95 percent interval the primary measure, and 13 of 17 land on exactly 24 scenarios. Each design was written independently and saw only its own catalog entry, so that is either the right measure for most of these questions or one model reaching for one template. Nothing here decides which.

## 1. CNF-01: Exposure separation, effect dynamics, and horizon dependence in baseline-initiation target trials

*Catalog entry: Convergence of treatment in the comparator arm attenuates the observational intention-to-treat analogue with follow-up length*

**Question.** Across 36 observational target-trial scenarios, by how many risk-difference percentage points and by what fraction does natural later initiation after assignment to no initiation move the marginal initiate-now versus do-not-initiate-now effect at 6, 12, 24, 36, 60, and 120 months relative to an always-treated versus never-treated benchmark? Can a cumulative treated-month separation threshold of 0.70 identify horizons retaining at least 75% of that benchmark with sensitivity and specificity of at least 0.80?

**Aims.** Estimate the treatment-policy risk difference, its additive movement from the sustained-strategy risk difference, and its signed retained-effect fraction at six horizons. Determine whether equal 60-month comparator initiation risks produce materially different movements when switching is prognosis-dependent, temporally concentrated, or combined with heterogeneous and time-dependent treatment effects. Compare current-treatment separation with cumulative treated-month separation as candidate diagnostics. Evaluate a prespecified horizon rule without selecting its threshold from the simulation results.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 4000 independent individuals followed for 120 monthly cycles. Generate X1 from N(0,1), X2 from Bernoulli(0.35), X3 from N(0,1), and L0 = 0.50*X1 + 0.40*X2 + 0.30*X3 + epsilon0, where epsilon0 is N(0,0.75^2). For t=1,...,120, generate Lt = 0.70*L(t-1) + 0.15*X1 + 0.20*X2 + 0.10*X3 + epsilon_t, with independent epsilon_t from N(0,0.70^2). Baseline initiation is observational: A0 is Bernoulli with probability expit(-0.80 + 0.55*X1 + 0.75*X2 + 0.40*X3 + 0.35*L0). This creates moderate measured baseline confounding and approximately 40% baseline initiation. All variables in the assignment model are observed. Draw an independent ZS from N(0,1). For a switching-selection parameter rho, define U = Phi(-rho*X1 + sqrt(1-rho^2)*ZS), which is marginally uniform but gives earlier initiation to higher-risk individuals when rho=0.70. For a chosen 60-month initiation probability p60 and Weibull shape k, define F(t) = 1 - exp(log(1-p60)*(t/60)^k). The later-initiation month is T = min{t: U <= F(t)}; T is greater than 120 if no threshold is crossed. Baseline initiators remain treated. Those with A0=0 have At=1 from month T onward and At=0 before T. No treatment discontinuation occurs.
- Outcome model: The outcome is an absorbing event. Let Y0=0. At month t, among individuals with Y(t-1)=0, generate an event with probability expit(-5.70 + 0.35*X1 + 0.55*X2 + 0.25*X3 + 0.30*Lt + 0.005*t + eta_t). Set Yt=1 after the first event and retain Yt=1 thereafter. Coefficients are on the log odds scale of the monthly discrete-time hazard. The untreated mechanism produces a moderate chronic-disease risk over five to ten years rather than a rare-event approximation. There is administrative follow-up only, with no loss to follow-up or competing event.
- Treatment effect: Let d_t be completed treated months through month t, with d_t=0 while untreated. Four causal profiles are used. P0 has eta_t=0. P1 has an immediate homogeneous benefit, eta_t=log(0.70)*I(d_t>0). P2 has the same immediate benefit with effect modification, eta_t=log(0.70)*m(X1)*I(d_t>0). P3 has initiation toxicity followed by benefit: eta_t=log(1.50)*m(X1) during the first three treated months and eta_t=log(0.70)*m(X1) thereafter. Thus later initiation can dilute, preserve, amplify, or temporarily reverse a baseline-assignment contrast. The treatment values are methodological stress parameters and are not presented as estimates for a particular drug.
- Effect modification: For profiles P2 and P3, define M=I(X1>0) and m(X1)=0.60+0.80*M. The monthly odds ratio corresponding to a base odds ratio of 0.70 is about 0.81 for X1<=0 and 0.61 for X1>0. The initiation-toxicity odds ratios are about 1.28 and 1.76, respectively. P1 is homogeneous, and P0 is null. Comparing P1 with P2 isolates effect modification while holding immediate effect dynamics fixed. Because X1 also predicts the outcome and, in prognosis-dependent scenarios, initiation timing, the identity of switchers can matter even when the marginal initiation curve is unchanged.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 initiation levels by 3 switching mechanisms by 4 causal profiles. Baseline exchangeability given X1, X2, X3, and L0, consistency, positivity, correct measurement, and no informative censoring are true. The propensity-score model used by the adjusted estimators exactly matches the baseline assignment mechanism. Later treatment is part of the treatment-policy estimand and is never adjusted away. The null, homogeneous, heterogeneous, and initiation-toxicity profiles prevent monotone attenuation from being imposed in every scenario.
- Cannot detect: The design says nothing about irregular visits, treatment discontinuation, repeated stopping and restarting, treatment measurement error, competing mortality, informative censoring, unmeasured confounding, postbaseline clinical triggers not represented by the latent switching model, or healthcare systems outside the specified initiation trajectories. Results concern marginal risk differences and need not hold on risk-ratio, odds-ratio, or hazard-ratio scales. The study cannot establish a universal clinical horizon because its treatment effects and prescribing mechanisms remain stylized.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Comparator cumulative initiation by 60 months | p60=0.10, p60=0.40, p60=0.70 | These levels represent low, moderate, and high convergence and span the requested 10% to 70% range. The cited literature establishes that switching is context-dependent but does not supply a universal empirical rate, so these are explicit stress levels rather than claimed literature estimates. |
| Later-initiation mechanism | M1: memoryless and nonselective, k=1.00 and rho=0, M2: memoryless and prognosis-dependent, k=1.00 and rho=0.70, M3: accelerating and prognosis-dependent, k=2.00 and rho=0.70 | M1 versus M2 isolates who switches while preserving the marginal trajectory. M2 versus M3 changes the trajectory shape while preserving strong prognosis-based selection. At 120 months, M1 and M2 produce initiation probabilities 0.19, 0.64, and 0.91 for the three p60 levels. M3 produces approximately 0.344, 0.870, and 0.992, providing a deliberate long-horizon stress test. |
| Causal treatment-effect profile | P0: null, P1: immediate homogeneous benefit with monthly odds ratio 0.70, P2: immediate effect-modified benefit with base monthly odds ratio 0.70, P3: three months of initiation toxicity with base odds ratio 1.50 followed by effect-modified benefit with base odds ratio 0.70 | The null profile is a negative control. P1 tests the conventional dilution mechanism. P2 tests selective switching among people with different effects. P3 tests the auditor-supported possibility that treatment timing and effect dynamics prevent guaranteed attenuation toward zero. |

**Estimands.**

- **Marginal treatment-policy risk difference**: For h in {6,12,24,36,60,120}, RD_TP(h) = Pr(Y_h under do(A0=1) and natural subsequent treatment =1) - Pr(Y_h under do(A0=0) and natural subsequent treatment =1). Later initiation after do(A0=0) follows the scenario-specific structural equation. The contrast is marginal for the DGM superpopulation. Negative values favor initiation now. *Truth: For every scenario, simulate 2,000,000 new superpopulation individuals in chunks of 50,000. Use common baseline values, longitudinal innovations, switching variables, and outcome uniforms under both baseline interventions. Retain only cumulative summaries. Estimate each arm risk and their paired difference. Estimate Monte Carlo uncertainty from the 40 chunk estimates and extend to 4,000,000 if any risk-difference truth has Monte Carlo SE greater than 0.0005.*
- **Marginal sustained-strategy risk difference**: RD_S(h) = Pr(Y_h under treatment during every month =1) - Pr(Y_h under no treatment during any month =1). This is a distinct sustained-strategy estimand used only as an efficacy-separation benchmark. It is not described as a corrected treatment-policy effect. *Truth: Use the same 2,000,000-person common-random-number truth run under always-treated and never-treated interventions. The always-treated world equals the A0=1 treatment-policy world because discontinuation is absent. Apply the same 0.0005 Monte Carlo SE requirement.*
- **Movement and signed retained-effect fraction**: Define additive movement G(h)=RD_TP(h)-RD_S(h) and r(h)=RD_TP(h)/RD_S(h). Calculate r only when abs(RD_S(h))>=0.005 and the causal profile is non-null. Values between 0 and 1 denote same-direction attenuation, values above 1 denote amplification, and values below 0 denote sign reversal. Reporting G and both raw risk differences prevents an unstable ratio from determining the conclusion. *Truth: Calculate G and r from the paired large-sample truths. Propagate the chunk-level paired estimates to obtain Monte Carlo SEs and confidence intervals for G and r.*
- **Marginal treatment-trajectory diagnostics**: At each horizon h, estimate comparator cumulative initiation F0(h), current-treatment separation S_current(h)=Pr(A_h=1 under A0=1)-Pr(A_h=1 under A0=0), and cumulative treated-month separation S_cum(h)=h^(-1)*sum from t=1 to h of [Pr(A_t=1 under A0=1)-Pr(A_t=1 under A0=0)]. The prespecified horizon rule labels a horizon as adequately separated when S_cum(h)>=0.70. Its reference label is same-direction retention of at least 75%, defined as r(h)>=0.75. *Truth: The marginal trajectory truths are analytic because U is uniform: F0(t)=F(t), S_current(t)=1-F(t), and S_cum(h)=h^(-1)*sum from t=1 to h of [1-F(t)]. Evaluate the conceptual sensitivity and specificity of the horizon rule across all non-null scenario-horizon cells. Evaluate operational performance separately using the replicate-level estimated trajectory.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Crude baseline-assignment contrast | At each horizon, subtract the observed cumulative event proportion among A0=0 from that among A0=1. Do not condition on postbaseline treatment. | Use R1*(1-R1)/n1 + R0*(1-R0)/n0, where Ra and na are the observed risk and sample size in baseline arm a. | no | Declare failure if either baseline arm is empty or the estimate or SE is nonfinite. Otherwise retain the estimate, including misleading estimates caused by baseline confounding. |
| Baseline IPTW treatment-policy estimator without trajectory reporting | Fit glm(A0 ~ X1 + X2 + X3 + L0, family=binomial). At each horizon, estimate arm risks with normalized inverse-probability weights A0/e and (1-A0)/(1-e), then subtract the weighted A0=0 risk from the weighted A0=1 risk. Do not censor or adjust for later treatment. | Use an empirical stacked-equation sandwich for the propensity coefficients and the two normalized weighted risks. Obtain the risk-difference variance using the full covariance matrix, including propensity-score estimation. | yes | Declare failure if the propensity glm does not converge, its model matrix is rank deficient, any propensity is not finite or lies outside [0.000001,0.999999], the estimating-equation Jacobian is singular, or the estimate or SE is nonfinite. Record maximum weight, effective sample size, and propensity range as diagnostics without otherwise truncating weights. |
| AIPW treatment-policy estimator with standardized trajectory report | At each horizon fit glm(Y_h ~ A0*(X1 + I(X1>0) + X2 + X3 + L0) + I(X1^2) + I(L0^2) + X2:X3, family=binomial). Combine its predictions with the fitted baseline propensity using the augmented inverse-probability score: mean[m1-m0 + A0*(Y_h-m1)/e - (1-A0)*(Y_h-m0)/(1-e)]. Postbaseline treatment is deliberately excluded because it mediates the treatment-policy effect. Estimate each comparator trajectory quantity by the normalized weighted mean among A0=0 using weights 1/(1-e); the treated-arm trajectory is one throughout by construction. | For the risk difference, use the empirical efficient-influence-function SE, sd(phi)/sqrt(n), retaining covariance between arm risks. For weighted trajectory quantities, use a stacked-equation sandwich including propensity estimation. Scale the risk-difference SE by abs(RD_S truth) only for simulation summaries of r; do not present that ratio as observable in an applied dataset. | no | Declare the risk-difference component failed if the propensity model or the horizon-specific outcome model does not converge, a model matrix is rank deficient, any coefficient or prediction is nonfinite, a required propensity lies outside [0.000001,0.999999], or the influence-function SE is nonfinite. Declare the trajectory component failed only when its propensity or sandwich calculation fails. Report component-specific failures rather than discarding the entire replicate. |

**Performance.** Primary: Cellwise coverage of the nominal 95% AIPW treatment-policy risk-difference interval at each of the six horizons. Report every cell, plus the minimum and median coverage across cells; do not average away a failing scenario.. Secondary: Bias, empirical SE, mean model SE, relative error in model SE, MSE, bias-eliminated coverage, rejection rate, and convergence for every risk-difference estimator using the existing ADEMP functions, Type I error in the nine null scenarios at every horizon, Bias and coverage for F0(h), S_current(h), and S_cum(h), True and estimated additive movement G(h) and signed retained-effect fraction r(h), Sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value, negative predictive value, and balanced accuracy of S_cum(h)>=0.70 for the truth label r(h)>=0.75, The same classification measures for S_current(h)>=0.70, with a prespecified comparison of balanced accuracy, Within each fixed p60 and horizon, the range of r(h) across switching mechanisms and non-null effect profiles, Monte Carlo SE for every reported performance measure and every large-sample truth. 2000 replicates. The target Monte Carlo SE for the primary 95% coverage estimate is 0.005, or half a percentage point. At anticipated coverage 0.95, R must satisfy sqrt(0.95*0.05/R)<=0.005, so R>=0.95*0.05/0.005^2=1900. Use 2000 replicates per scenario, giving MCSE sqrt(0.95*0.05/2000)=0.00487. The same calculation applies to a 0.05 null rejection rate.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: For each of the nine matched combinations of non-null effect profile and switching mechanism, compare p60=0.10 with p60=0.70. A pair qualifies as material at a horizon when 0<=r_high<r_low, r_low-r_high>=0.20, and abs(RD_TP_high-RD_S)>=0.010. Declare convergence materially capable of obscuring the treatment-policy contrast if at least five of nine pairs qualify at 60 months or at least five qualify at 120 months. Separately declare a convergence rate alone inadequate if, in at least six of the 18 fixed-p60-by-horizon blocks, the range of r across mechanisms and non-null profiles is at least 0.20. Accept the proposed S_cum>=0.70 horizon convention only if sensitivity and specificity for r>=0.75 are both at least 0.80 and its balanced accuracy exceeds that of S_current>=0.70 by at least 0.10.
- Problem is not real if: Declare no practically important convergence effect in this design if no matched pair at any horizon has r_low-r_high>=0.10 and every non-null high-convergence cell has abs(RD_TP-RD_S)<0.005. This conclusion is restricted to the simulated mechanisms and does not establish that switching is harmless elsewhere.
- Uninformative if: Call the materiality result intermediate rather than choosing a favorable label when it meets neither rule. Also call estimator-based conclusions uninformative if more than 10% of AIPW fits fail in any cell, absolute AIPW bias exceeds 0.005 in any primary cell, the truth Monte Carlo SE remains above 0.0005 after extension, or fewer than 95% of planned replicates are completed. Failure of the 0.70 horizon rule is informative evidence against that universal cutoff and is not classified as an inconclusive study.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A DGM containing only a persistent beneficial treatment and monotone comparator initiation would mechanically manufacture attenuation. *Mitigation: Use a fully factorial null profile, homogeneous and heterogeneous benefit profiles, initiation toxicity, nonselective and selective switching, and two timing shapes. Use signed movement metrics so amplification and reversal cannot be relabeled as attenuation. Require material findings in a majority of matched mechanisms.*
- The sustained-strategy effect could be mistaken for a corrected version of the treatment-policy effect. *Mitigation: Name it as a distinct benchmark estimand, publish both arm risks and both risk differences, and make no claim that an applied treatment-policy estimate identifies the sustained effect without additional assumptions.*
- A single cumulative initiation percentage can conceal when switching occurred and who switched. *Mitigation: Hold the 60-month percentage constant while varying prognosis selection and timing shape. Publish monthly adjusted trajectories and compare current separation with cumulative treated-month separation.*
- Observed comparator trajectories are conditional on entering the comparator arm and therefore reflect baseline confounding. *Mitigation: Estimate marginal trajectories under baseline assignment using the same correctly specified inverse-probability standardization used for the treatment-policy effect.*
- Estimator bias could be mistaken for movement caused by the estimand. *Mitigation: Base the substantive movement analysis on high-precision counterfactual truths. Include crude, IPTW, and AIPW estimators, a true-null profile, explicit convergence rules, and complete ADEMP bias and coverage results.*
- The retained-effect ratio is unstable when the sustained effect is near zero. *Mitigation: Do not calculate it for the null profile or when abs(RD_S)<0.005. Always report additive movement and raw risks, and base clinical-scale thresholds on the additive risk difference as well as the ratio.*
- The 0.70 horizon threshold could be selected because it performs well in these data. *Mitigation: Fix 0.70 before simulation, evaluate it in every eligible cell, and do not replace it with a post hoc threshold. A failed rule is reported as a failed universal convention.*
- The generic outcome and switching parameters may not represent a particular health system. *Mitigation: Label the values as stress parameters, release cell-specific results instead of a pooled universal attenuation factor, and provide code allowing a catalog user to replace p60, timing shape, and effect profile with local estimates.*
- Fixed monthly visits, permanent treatment after initiation, and absence of censoring make the simulated data cleaner than claims or EHR data. *Mitigation: State these restrictions in the protocol and conclusion. Do not generalize findings to irregular observation, discontinuation, informative follow-up, or competing risks.*
- Risk-difference movement is scale-dependent. *Mitigation: Make the marginal risk difference the sole decision scale, publish arm-specific risks, and avoid claims about attenuation on odds-ratio or hazard-ratio scales.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The design has 36*2000=72,000 replicates. IPTW and AIPW share one propensity fit, and AIPW adds six outcome fits, for 36*2000*7=504,000 small glm fits; crude estimates and trajectories require no additional models. No longitudinal TMLE or SuperLearner fits are included. At the established machine rate, allow approximately 4 to 7 hours for replicate generation and fitting on six workers. The 36 truth runs conservatively cost 36*4=144 minutes and can run separately in chunks. Including serialization and summaries, the complete run should require about 6 to 10 hours and fit overnight. Implementation, dry runs, result checking, figures, and the public artifact fit within one working week.

**Also bears on.** CNF-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Hernan MA, Robins JM. Using big data to emulate a target trial when a randomized trial is not available. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2016;183(8):758-764.; Cain LE, Robins JM, Lanoy E, Logan R, Costagliola D, Hernan MA. Using observational data to emulate a randomized trial of dynamic treatment-switching strategies. International Journal of Epidemiology. 2016;45(6):2038-2049.; Hernan MA, Hernandez-Diaz S. Beyond the intention-to-treat in comparative effectiveness research. Clinical Trials. 2012;9(1):48-55.; Statistical methods to adjust for treatment switching in real-world clinical studies: a scoping review and decision framework. Clinical Pharmacology and Therapeutics. 2025.

## 2. BEN-02: When replication labels confuse estimand differences with sampling error: a tolerance-calibrated benchmark simulation

*Catalog entry: Replication is reported as a binary label rather than as a calibrated comparison of estimands*

**Question.** Across true trial-emulation risk-difference discrepancies from -0.04 to 0.04 and trial sizes from 500 to 25000, what are the false-reassurance and false-disagreement probabilities of direction agreement, confidence-interval overlap, trial-standardized agreement, and joint-null agreement; can a prespecified 0.02 absolute-risk tolerance rule keep false definitive conclusions below 5% while becoming decisive when both estimates are precise?

**Aims.** Estimate each comparison rule's probability of declaring agreement at every known estimand discrepancy and precision level; quantify how much that probability changes solely because sampling precision changes; evaluate bias, variance estimation, confidence-interval coverage, and convergence of the underlying paired estimators; test whether an alignment-first transported estimate removes a deliberately introduced population estimand difference; and document the instability of effect ratios when the trial effect is near zero.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: The unit of simulation is one benchmark pair comprising two independent cohorts. The randomized-trial cohort and observational-emulation cohort both implement baseline eligibility, static initiation A=1 versus noninitiation A=0, time zero at baseline, no grace period, no switching, no censoring, and a binary outcome Y by day 365. L1 is Bernoulli(0.50), L2 is Bernoulli(0.40), and L1 and L2 are independent. Z is an observed high-risk effect modifier. The trial protocol produces Z distributed Bernoulli(0.50); the emulation eligibility protocol produces Z distributed Bernoulli(qE), with qE fixed by the target discrepancy. Trial treatment is Bernoulli(0.50), independently of all covariates. Emulation treatment follows logit P(A=1|L1,L2,Z) = -0.40 + 0.80 L1 - 0.60 L2 + 0.70 Z, giving true propensities from 0.269 to 0.750. Each cohort is sampled exactly with one multinomial draw over the 32 cells defined by L1, L2, Z, A, and Y. Cell counts are retained rather than expanded into individual rows.
- Outcome model: Two outcome families are used. In the additive-risk family, P(Y=1|A=a,L1,L2,Z) = 0.28 + 0.04 L1 - 0.03 L2 + 0.02 L1 L2 + 0.04 Z + a(tau0 + 0.16 Z), where tau0 is -0.08 for a null marginal trial effect and -0.13 for a marginal trial effect of -0.05. All probabilities lie between 0.12 and 0.44. In the logistic family, logit P(Y=1|A=a,L1,L2,Z) = -1.20 + 0.50 L1 - 0.40 L2 + 0.60 Z + a(gamma0 + 1.00 Z), where gamma0=-0.60 gives a trial marginal risk difference of approximately 0.00013 and gamma0=-0.88 gives approximately -0.05016. Exact logistic-family truths are obtained by summing the eight covariate cells. The negative-control block changes qE from 0.50 to 0.75 but removes risk-difference modification: the additive model sets the A by Z coefficient to zero, and the logistic model sets both the Z outcome coefficient and A by Z coefficient to zero. Its logistic treatment coefficient is 0 for the null-effect stratum and -0.29 for the approximately -0.05 stratum.
- Treatment effect: The primary causal scale is the 365-day marginal risk difference. In the additive family, the trial effect is exactly 0 or -0.05. The emulation effect equals the trial effect plus the prespecified signed discrepancy. In the logistic family, the corresponding trial effects are approximately 0.00013 and -0.05016; qE is calibrated using exact finite-cell expectations so that the emulation-minus-trial risk difference equals the requested discrepancy to numerical precision. Both beneficial and harmful discrepancies are included.
- Effect modification: In the additive core DGM, the conditional treatment risk difference increases by 0.16 when Z changes from 0 to 1. Thus the null marginal trial-effect stratum has conditional effects -0.08 and 0.08, while the moderate-benefit stratum has conditional effects -0.13 and 0.03. In the logistic core DGM, the treatment log odds ratio changes by 1.00 across Z. For gamma0=-0.60, the Z-specific marginal risk differences averaged over L1 and L2 are approximately -0.09488 and 0.09515. For gamma0=-0.88, they are approximately -0.12826 and 0.02793.
- Deliberately true: The 84 core scenarios form a complete 2 by 7 by 3 by 2 factorial. Twelve negative-control scenarios add 2 outcome families by 3 precision patterns by 2 effect magnitudes, so the complete 96-scenario design is not fully factorial. Treatment, outcome, and effect-modification models are correctly specified; Z is measured; positivity holds; consistency holds; there is no missingness, measurement error, nonadherence, censoring, or unmeasured confounding. Trial and emulation samples are independent, so their sampling covariance is exactly zero. The only source of systematic raw-estimand discrepancy is the protocol-defined population distribution of Z. Transporting the emulation to qZ=0.50 therefore truly removes that discrepancy. Comparison rules receive only estimates and standard errors, never the scenario truth.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot evaluate irregular visits, time-varying treatment or confounding, grace periods, informative censoring, outcome-definition mismatch, residual confounding, nuisance-model misspecification, unknown or unmeasured effect modifiers, overlapping trial and emulation samples, nonzero sampling covariance, between-database heterogeneity, or prospective forecasting across multiple clinical questions. It cannot determine which absolute-risk tolerance is clinically or socially correct. It also cannot establish prediction-interval calibration for a database because each replicate contains one independent trial-emulation pair rather than a historical portfolio.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Outcome family | Additive Bernoulli risk model with identity-scale effect modification, Logistic Bernoulli model with nonlinear marginalization | The additive family supplies transparent exact discrepancies; the logistic family tests whether conclusions depend on a DGM constructed on the same scale as the proposed absolute-difference method. |
| Signed true emulation-minus-trial risk difference | -0.04, -0.02, -0.01, 0, 0.01, 0.02, 0.04 | The levels include exact agreement, acceptable differences inside the primary 0.02 tolerance, both tolerance boundaries, and clinically material discrepancies in both directions. For the additive family, qE is respectively 0.25, 0.375, 0.4375, 0.50, 0.5625, 0.625, and 0.75. For the logistic near-null stratum, rounded qE values are 0.28951, 0.39475, 0.44738, 0.50, 0.55262, 0.60525, and 0.71049. For the logistic moderate-benefit stratum, they are 0.24390, 0.37195, 0.43597, 0.50, 0.56403, 0.62805, and 0.75610. Implementation must calculate logistic qE from unrounded finite-cell expectations. |
| Precision pattern | Trial n=500; emulation n=5000, Trial n=2500; emulation n=25000, Trial n=25000; emulation n=250000 | These levels span small trials paired with database studies through precise large trials paired with very large databases. The tenfold emulation-to-trial ratio isolates the effect of absolute precision while retaining the common asymmetry between trial and database sample sizes. |
| Trial effect magnitude | Near null: risk difference 0 in the additive family and approximately 0.00013 in the logistic family, Moderate benefit: risk difference -0.05 in the additive family and approximately -0.05016 in the logistic family | Direction labels and effect ratios behave differently near zero. A five-percentage-point benefit provides a decision-relevant non-null contrast. |
| Negative-control augmentation | Core effect-modified scenarios, Population-shift control with qE=0.75 but true discrepancy 0 | The control tests whether visible population and treatment-pattern differences generate apparent failure when treatment effects do not vary across the populations. The largest core discrepancies serve as positive controls. |

**Estimands.**

- **Trial-protocol 365-day marginal risk difference**: psiT = P(Y^1=1 under the trial eligibility distribution qZ=0.50) - P(Y^0=1 under that distribution). It contrasts baseline initiation with noninitiation, is marginal over L1, L2, and Z, and targets the trial superpopulation rather than the realized sample. *Truth: For the additive family, psiT = tau0 + 0.16(0.50), giving exactly 0 or -0.05. For the logistic family, sum the model-based treated and untreated risks over the four L1-L2 cells and both Z cells using their exact probabilities. No Monte Carlo truth sample is required.*
- **Emulation-protocol 365-day marginal risk difference**: psiE = P(Y^1=1 under the emulation eligibility distribution qZ=qE) - P(Y^0=1 under that distribution). It uses the same treatment strategies, time zero, outcome, and horizon as the trial but targets the emulation superpopulation. *Truth: For the additive family, psiE = tau0 + 0.16 qE. For the logistic family, use exact finite-cell summation. The specified qE calibration makes psiE-psiT equal the requested signed discrepancy.*
- **Raw paired estimand discrepancy**: delta = psiE-psiT on the absolute 365-day risk-difference scale. Acceptable alignment is prespecified as |delta|<0.02; material mismatch is |delta|>0.02; |delta|=0.02 is a boundary used for error-rate calibration. *Truth: The truth is algebraic in the additive family and obtained by deterministic finite-cell summation in the logistic family. Numerical summation error must be below 1e-12.*
- **Protocol-aligned transported discrepancy**: deltaTransport = psiEtoT-psiT, where psiEtoT standardizes the emulation conditional treatment risks to the trial distribution qZ=0.50. It is a marginal trial-population contrast. *Truth: deltaTransport is exactly zero under every core and negative-control scenario because the structural outcome model is shared and all effect modifiers are observed.*
- **Ratio of risk-difference effects**: rho = psiE/psiT, defined only when psiT is nonzero. It is secondary and is not used to decide replication. *Truth: Use the exact psiE and psiT values. The ratio is declared undefined in the near-null additive stratum and treated as scientifically unstable in the near-null logistic stratum.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Underlying trial and emulation causal-effect estimators | The trial estimate is the treated sample risk minus the untreated sample risk. The emulation estimate is parametric augmented inverse-probability weighting. Fit e(L)=P(A=1|L1,L2,Z) by binomial glm with terms L1+L2+Z. Fit m(A,L)=E(Y|A,L1,L2,Z) using weighted lm with A+L1+L2+L1:L2+Z+A:Z for the additive DGM and binomial glm with A+L1+L2+Z+A:Z for the logistic DGM. For each cell, H=m1-m0+A(Y-m1)/e-(1-A)(Y-m0)/(1-e); psiEhat is the frequency-weighted mean of H. | For the trial, use s1^2/n1+s0^2/n0, where each arm variance uses denominator na-1. For the emulation, use the frequency-weighted sample variance of H divided by nE, equivalently the empirical influence-function variance. Independence gives var(deltahat)=var(psiEhat)+var(psiThat). | no | Failure occurs if a trial arm is empty; either fitted model reports failure or rank deficiency; any required coefficient, prediction, estimate, or standard error is nonfinite; an estimated propensity is below 1e-6 or above 1-1e-6; or a variance estimate is nonpositive. Unbounded effect estimates are retained if finite. |
| Direction agreement | Declare agreement when sign(psiEhat)=sign(psiThat); sign(0)=0, so one exact zero and one nonzero estimate disagree. | Use the separately estimated trial and emulation variances, although the label itself discards them. | yes | Nonconvergence is inherited from either underlying estimator. |
| Ninety-five-percent confidence-interval overlap | Declare agreement when the closed 95% Wald intervals for psiT and psiE intersect. | Use the separate trial and emulation model variances. | yes | Nonconvergence is inherited from either underlying estimator or any nonfinite interval endpoint. |
| Trial-standardized estimate agreement | Compute STrial=|psiEhat-psiThat|/seT and declare agreement when STrial<=1.959964, equivalent to requiring the emulation point estimate to lie inside the trial 95% confidence interval. | Use the trial model variance as the standardizing variance, matching the trial-centered benchmark rule. | yes | Failure occurs when either point estimate fails or seT is nonfinite or nonpositive. |
| Joint standardized null-difference agreement | Compute Zdelta=|deltahat|/sqrt(seT^2+seE^2) and declare agreement when Zdelta<=1.959964. This is failure to reject the point null delta=0. | Use seDelta^2=seT^2+seE^2 because the cohorts are independent. | yes | Failure occurs when either underlying estimate fails or seDelta is nonfinite or nonpositive. |
| Absolute discrepancy with a prespecified three-way tolerance rule | Use deltahat=psiEhat-psiThat. The primary tolerance is Delta=0.02. Declare aligned when the entire 90% confidence interval lies inside [-0.02,0.02]; declare materially mismatched when the entire 90% interval lies above 0.02 or below -0.02; otherwise declare indeterminate. | Use seDelta^2=seT^2+seE^2. This accounts for sampling error from both estimates; covariance is zero by design. | no | Failure occurs when either underlying estimate fails or seDelta is nonfinite or nonpositive. Indeterminate is a valid result, not nonconvergence. |
| Protocol-aligned transported AIPW positive control | Reuse the emulation nuisance fits and compute psiEtoThat=mean(w(Z)H), where w=0.50/qE for Z=1 and w=0.50/(1-qE) for Z=0. Compare psiEtoThat with psiThat and apply the same absolute-discrepancy and three-way tolerance procedure. | Estimate var(psiEtoThat) by the frequency-weighted empirical variance of wH divided by nE. Add the independent trial variance for the transported paired difference. | no | Use the underlying fit criteria; also fail if qE is outside (0,1), any transport weight is nonfinite, or the transported variance is nonpositive. |
| Ratio of risk-difference effects with Fieller confidence set | Compute rhohat=psiEhat/psiThat when psiThat is nonzero. Do not convert it into a replication label. | Report the delta-method variance seE^2/psiThat^2 + psiEhat^2 seT^2/psiThat^4 for descriptive comparison. Independence is assumed. | no | An exactly zero denominator makes the point ratio undefined and is recorded as a ratio-method failure. An unbounded or disjoint Fieller set is a valid statistical result and must not be discarded. |

**Performance.** Primary: The scenario-specific false-conclusion probability. For |delta|<0.02, a binary disagreement or a definitive material-mismatch conclusion is false. For |delta|>0.02, a binary agreement or definitive aligned conclusion is false. Boundary scenarios with |delta|=0.02 are excluded from this primary rate and assess test size. For the three-way rule, also report the indeterminate probability and the probability of a decisive correct conclusion so that an always-indeterminate procedure cannot appear successful.. Secondary: Agreement probability for each binary rule in every scenario., False reassurance when |delta|=0.04 and false disagreement when |delta| is 0 or 0.01., Absolute change in each rule's agreement probability between adjacent precision patterns and between nT=500 and nT=25000 at fixed truth, link, and effect magnitude., Probabilities of aligned, materially mismatched, indeterminate, and decisive-correct outcomes for each tolerance margin., Boundary declaration rates at delta=-0.02 and delta=0.02., Bias, empirical standard error, mean model standard error, relative error in model standard error, mean squared error, 95% coverage, bias-eliminated coverage, rejection rate for delta=0, and convergence for psiT, psiE, delta, psiEtoT, and deltaTransport, all with Monte Carlo standard errors from the shared ADEMP harness., Median and interquartile range of the effect ratio, Fieller-set coverage when defined, and frequencies of undefined point ratios and unbounded or disjoint Fieller sets., Negative-control false-disagreement rates and transported-estimator recovery of deltaTransport=0.. 2500 replicates. The primary measure is a Bernoulli event probability. Its worst-case Monte Carlo standard error occurs at p=0.50: MCSE=sqrt(0.50(1-0.50)/R). Requiring MCSE<=0.01 gives R>=0.25/0.0001=2500. At p=0.05 or 0.95, MCSE=sqrt(0.0475/2500)=0.00436. A difference between independent precision scenarios has worst-case MCSE sqrt(0.25/2500+0.25/2500)=0.01414; the prespecified 0.20 precision-effect threshold is therefore well separated from simulation error.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: At least two of the four forced binary rules have a false-conclusion probability of at least 0.30 in one or more nonboundary core scenarios, and at least one such rule changes its agreement probability by at least 0.20 between the lowest and highest precision patterns while delta, outcome family, and trial effect remain fixed. The proposed tolerance procedure counts as an adequate operational response only if false definitive alignment is at most 0.05 in every |delta|=0.04 scenario, false definitive mismatch is at most 0.05 in every |delta|<=0.01 and negative-control scenario, each wrong-side boundary declaration rate is at most 0.06, 95% discrepancy coverage is between 0.93 and 0.97, and decisive-correct probability is at least 0.80 in the highest-precision delta=0 and |delta|=0.04 scenarios for both outcome families.
- Problem is not real if: Every forced binary rule has false-conclusion probability below 0.10 in every nonboundary scenario, no rule's agreement probability changes by 0.05 or more between the lowest and highest precision patterns at fixed truth, and the three-way procedure reduces false conclusions by less than 0.10 in every scenario.
- Uninformative if: The study is methodologically uninformative if any core scenario has more than 1% underlying-estimator nonconvergence, if 95% discrepancy coverage falls outside 0.93 to 0.97 after bias-eliminated coverage is examined, or if Monte Carlo 95% intervals overlap both the real and not-real thresholds. If the binary-label criterion is met but the tolerance procedure fails its error or decisiveness criteria, the result establishes the reporting problem but rejects this particular operational remedy.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM deliberately creates estimand mismatch through a strong observed effect modifier, so a method designed around absolute discrepancy could appear successful because the answer was encoded in its preferred scale. *Mitigation: Use discrepancies in both directions, additive and logistic outcome families, near-null and non-null trial effects, and a population-shift negative control with no true effect discrepancy. Rules are frozen before simulation and never receive scenario labels.*
- The primary 0.02 tolerance is a judgment rather than a statistically discoverable constant. *Mitigation: Label it explicitly as a prespecified illustrative decision threshold. Repeat all tolerance classifications at 0.01, 0.03, and 0.05 using the same stored estimates. Do not claim that simulation identifies the clinically correct margin.*
- Correct nuisance models and complete measurement make the emulation unrealistically clean. *Mitigation: This is intentional isolation of estimand mismatch from random error. Conclusions are restricted to comparison rules conditional on unbiased component estimates; they do not establish robustness to confounding or misspecification.*
- The transported positive control is handed the true effect modifier and protocol distributions. *Mitigation: Use it only to verify that the constructed discrepancy is an estimand-alignment problem and is removable in principle. Do not interpret it as evidence that transport is identified when real modifiers are missing.*
- Wald approximations can fail in small binary samples and could create apparent label failures unrelated to the conceptual problem. *Mitigation: Include nT=500 explicitly; audit bias, empirical versus model standard error, ordinary and bias-eliminated coverage, and convergence before interpreting labels. The problem verdict is invalid if coverage leaves the prespecified 0.93 to 0.97 range.*
- Trial and emulation estimates can be correlated when samples overlap or share estimated calibration quantities. *Mitigation: Generate independent cohorts so zero covariance is known rather than silently assumed. State that applications with overlap require the covariance term in var(psiEhat-psiThat) and are outside this study.*
- Effect ratios can explode or become undefined near a null reference effect, and dropping those replicates would make the ratio look stable. *Mitigation: Include a near-null trial-effect stratum, use Fieller confidence sets, retain unbounded sets, and report all undefined or unbounded frequencies.*
- Multinomial cell aggregation might be mistaken for an asymptotic summary-data simulation. *Mitigation: Draw exact finite-sample counts from the complete individual-level joint distribution. Frequency-weighted fits and influence-function sums reproduce analysis of the corresponding expanded binary records.*
- A single benchmark pair cannot estimate between-question or between-database calibration or validate a prospective prediction interval. *Mitigation: Restrict the claim to pair-level separation of known estimand discrepancy and sampling error. A subsequent portfolio simulation or prospective multi-database benchmark is required for prediction-interval and data-source calibration.*
- Retrospective knowledge of a trial result can alter real emulation design, but simulated analysis code cannot reproduce that human behavior. *Mitigation: Prespecify all rules and blind them to truth. Interpret results as statistical operating characteristics, not evidence about researcher behavior or prospective-versus-retrospective design contamination.*

**Runtime as estimated.** There are 96 scenarios by 2500 replicates, totaling 240000 benchmark pairs. Each replicate requires one small propensity-score binomial glm and one small outcome lm or binomial glm on at most 32 weighted rows. Trial estimation, comparison metrics, transport, and truth calculations are algebraic. The run therefore comprises 480000 small nuisance-model fits, with zero ltmle, SuperLearner, bootstrap, Stan, lmtp, concrete, or CICI fits. On six workers the expected runtime is approximately 3 to 8 hours, with exact truth calculation taking seconds rather than the four minutes per scenario required by brute-force truth enumeration.

**Also bears on.** BEN-02 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Wang SV, Schneeweiss S, RCT-DUPLICATE Initiative. Emulation of randomized clinical trials with nonrandomized database analyses: results of 32 clinical trials. JAMA. 2023;329(16):1376-1385.; Concordance between target trial emulation and randomised controlled trials: systematic review and meta-analysis. BMJ.; Koppe and colleagues. Assessing the replicability of RCTs in RWE emulations. BMC Medical Research Methodology. 2025.

## 3. GMT-01: Causal-score selection of longitudinal nuisance models for sustained treatment strategies

*Catalog entry: Cross-validation for longitudinal nuisance models optimizes prediction, not causal performance*

**Question.** Across 24 longitudinal scenarios, by how much does a computable selection score combining predictive loss, longitudinal balance, weight dispersion, and held-out influence-function drift change absolute bias and 95% interval coverage for the 36-month marginal mean difference, relative to predictive cross-validation and balance-only selection?

**Aims.** Estimate, for each scenario and estimator, the difference between selectors in absolute bias, MSE, 95% coverage, and convergence. Determine whether the combined selector reduces absolute bias by at least 0.02 outcome standard deviations or improves coverage by at least 5 percentage points without materially increasing MSE or nonconvergence. Separately determine whether it improves on balance-only selection, since balance-based longitudinal weighting is already published and is not the unresolved part of GMT-01.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate n = 4000 independent people. All random innovations are mutually independent unless linked by the equations below. Let W1 ~ N(0,1), representing age centered at 60 years and divided by 10; W2 ~ Bernoulli(0.5); and W3 ~ Bernoulli(expit(-0.4 + 0.5 W1 + 0.4 W2)), representing baseline comorbidity. There are treatment decisions at t = 0, 1, and 2 years and an outcome at 36 months. Set R0 = 1. Generate Z0 ~ N(0,1) and L0 = 0.40 W1 + 0.50 W3 + epsilon_L0, where epsilon_L0 ~ N(0,0.8^2). For t = 1,2, generate Zt = 0.60 Z(t-1) + 0.80 epsilon_Zt, epsilon_Zt ~ N(0,1), and Lt = 0.45 L(t-1) + 0.25 W1 + 0.35 W3 - 0.35 A(t-1) + 0.15 t + epsilon_Lt, epsilon_Lt ~ N(0,0.8^2). Thus prior treatment changes a later confounder. For each observed t, set A(-1) = 0 and generate A_t from logit P(A_t = 1 | history,R_t = 1) = -0.20 + 0.15 t + s[0.40 W1 + 0.25 W2 + 0.50 W3 + 0.75 L_t - 0.25 A(t-1) + gamma_Z Z_t + I_G{0.45(L_t^2 - 1) + 0.35 W1 L_t}]. After treatment, generate retention R(t+1) among people with R_t = 1 from logit P(R(t+1) = 1 | history,A_t,R_t = 1) = 2.80 - 0.20 t - 0.25 W1 - 0.35 W3 - 0.45 L_t + 0.15 A_t + I_C{-0.40(L_t^2 - 1) + 0.30 W1 L_t}. Once R becomes zero it remains zero. Generate latent later variables and Y for truth calculations, but suppress them from the observed data after dropout. Z predicts treatment but has no direct or indirect effect on L, retention, or Y. Before estimator runs, a DGM-only population check will report treatment prevalence, the 1st and 99th percentiles of true treatment and retention probabilities, cumulative retention, and regime-specific effective sample sizes. Coefficients will not be retuned after examining estimator performance.
- Outcome model: Generate the continuous 36-month outcome Y = mu_Y + epsilon_Y, where epsilon_Y ~ N(0,1) and mu_Y = 0.25 W1 + 0.20 W2 + 0.45 W3 + 0.15 L0 + 0.25 L1 + 0.35 L2 + sum over t=0,1,2 of [(-0.12 - 0.08 W3) A_t] + I_Q{0.30(L2^2 - 1) + 0.25 W1 L2}. Lower values are better. All coefficients are on the standardized outcome scale. The main-effect outcome mechanism has exactly linear sequential conditional means under the linear L process. The nonlinear-outcome mechanism requires quadratic and interaction terms.
- Treatment effect: The controlled direct effect of treatment at each decision is -0.12 outcome standard deviations when W3 = 0 and -0.20 when W3 = 1. Treatment also lowers subsequent L by 0.35 units, producing an additional mediated benefit because later L raises Y. Effects are additive across the three decisions on the conditional mean scale. The marginal sustained-strategy effect is not set directly; it is induced by these direct and mediated paths.
- Effect modification: W3 modifies every treatment coefficient by -0.08 on the outcome mean scale. No other variable modifies the direct treatment effect. Marginal effects therefore average over a measured, prognostic modifier whose prevalence depends on W1 and W2.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 2 overlap levels times 3 treatment-only-predictor levels times 4 nuisance shapes. Consistency, sequential exchangeability, and mathematical positivity hold. Every common cause of treatment, retention, and outcome is measured. The candidate library contains a correctly specified treatment model, retention model, and sequential outcome model for every scenario. Patient-level outer cross-fitting is honest. Fixed annual decision times, a common sustained strategy, and one treatment-confounder feedback process are deliberately imposed. The strongest-instrument scenarios are intentionally capable of exposing a prediction-versus-causal-selection conflict; conclusions must therefore also hold in at least two gamma_Z = 0 scenarios.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot evaluate unmeasured confounding, exact positivity violations, irregular or informative visit timing, record-level versus patient-level leakage, competing events, death, measurement error, treatment versions, grace periods, dynamic regimes, recurrent outcomes, or simultaneous misspecification of all nuisance components. Its finite library uses generalized linear models with prespecified nonlinear terms. It therefore does not establish performance for trees, boosting, neural networks, an actual SuperLearner ensemble, or arbitrary electronic health record observation processes.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Practical overlap | Good: s = 0.65, Poor: s = 1.25 | The levels preserve mathematical positivity while producing ordinary versus severe probability tails. The DGM-only check must document the achieved tails; the intended ranges are approximately 0.05 to 0.95 under good overlap and 0.01 to 0.99 under poor overlap. |
| Strength of a treatment-only predictor | Absent: gamma_Z = 0, Moderate: gamma_Z = 0.75, odds ratio 2.12 per SD, Strong: gamma_Z = 1.50, odds ratio 4.48 per SD | A treatment-only predictor can improve treatment prediction while worsening weight dispersion without improving confounding control. Including an absent level prevents the DGM from guaranteeing an advantage for a causal selector. |
| Nuisance mechanism shape | All main effects: I_G = 0, I_C = 0, I_Q = 0, Nonlinear treatment: I_G = 1, I_C = 0, I_Q = 0, Nonlinear censoring: I_G = 0, I_C = 1, I_Q = 0, Nonlinear outcome: I_G = 0, I_C = 0, I_Q = 1 | These cells deliberately stress one nuisance component at a time and retain main-effect alignment cells. This follows the catalog's proposed component-wise misspecification experiment without confounding the result through simultaneous misspecification of every nuisance. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary 36-month sustained-strategy marginal mean difference**: For the superpopulation eligible immediately before A0, compare E[Y under A0=A1=A2=1 and R1=R2=R3=1] minus E[Y under A0=A1=A2=0 and R1=R2=R3=1]. This is a marginal mean difference on the standardized outcome scale. Treatment is assigned at 0, 12, and 24 months; there is no grace period; censoring is eliminated; and follow-up ends at 36 months. The target is not conditional on the realized sample. *Truth: For each scenario, simulate 2,000,000 paired complete trajectories under the two sustained strategies. Reuse baseline variables and longitudinal innovations across strategies and evaluate mu_Y without drawing epsilon_Y. This common-random-number evaluation removes irrelevant outcome noise. Report the Monte Carlo SE of the paired mean difference. Two million is expected to place it below 0.0005, one tenth of the smallest material bias threshold; double the population in blocks of 2,000,000 if that target is missed.*
- **Regime-specific 36-month means**: The two superpopulation means E[Y under sustained treatment and no censoring] and E[Y under sustained no treatment and no censoring]. *Truth: Use the same paired population used for the primary contrast. These means diagnose whether a selector's error is concentrated in one treatment strategy.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Predictive-CV cross-fitted IPW | Use two patient-level outer folds. Within each outer training set, use two patient-level inner folds. Fit separate logistic models at each t. Treatment candidates are G0 = W1+W2+W3+A(t-1); G1 = G0+L_t; G2 = G1+Z_t; and G3 = G2+L_t^2+W1:L_t+Z_t^2+W1:Z_t+L_t:Z_t. Retention candidates are C0 = W1+W2+W3+A_t; C1 = C0+L_t; and C2 = C1+L_t^2+W1:L_t+A_t:L_t+W3:L_t. Sequential outcome candidates are Q0, containing W1, W2, W3, available treatment history, and each available A_s:W3 term; Q1 = Q0 plus L0 through L_t; and Q2 = Q1 plus available L_s^2, W1:L_s, Z_s, A_s:L_s, and L_s:L_r terms. Q models use ordinary least squares and are fitted backward separately for each regime after the terminal regression. Select G, C, and Q independently by minimum inner-fold Bernoulli deviance for G and C and minimum backward sequential squared prediction error for Q. Break ties within 1e-8 by fewer coefficients and then G, C, Q order. Refit every candidate on the complete outer training fold, predict the held-out fold, and combine predictions. For regime a, define H2(a) as the indicator of following A0=A1=A2=a and remaining observed divided by the product of the corresponding cross-fitted treatment and retention probabilities. Estimate each regime mean by the Hajek ratio sum[H2(a)Y]/sum[H2(a)] and subtract. | Use the fixed-weight empirical influence contribution D_i(a) = H2_i(a)[Y_i-mu_hat(a)]/mean[H2(a)]. The model SE for the contrast is sd[D_i(1)-D_i(0)]/sqrt(n). This deliberately treats cross-fitted selected weights as fixed and therefore assesses the common interval that ignores first-stage model selection. | yes | A replicate fails if a selected glm does not converge, any coefficient is nonfinite or exceeds 30 in absolute value, more than 0.5% of required predictions fall outside [1e-6,1-1e-6], either regime has no observed adherers, either terminal effective sample size is below 10, any cumulative weight exceeds 1e6, or the estimate or SE is nonfinite. Probabilities are floored only at 1e-6 for arithmetic; this flooring triggers the stated diagnostic and is not scientific truncation. |
| Predictive-CV IPW with current-practice truncation | Use the predictive-CV cross-fitted weights above. Within each regime, truncate nonzero terminal cumulative weights at their pooled 1st and 99th percentiles before Hajek normalization. | Use the same fixed-weight empirical influence variance after truncation. | yes | Use the predictive-CV IPW rules, except the weight above 1e6 rule is applied before percentile truncation and recorded as a numerical failure rather than hidden by truncation. |
| Balance-selected cross-fitted IPW | Within each inner validation fold, evaluate every G by C pair. At each t, compute weighted standardized mean differences for W1, W2, W3, and L0 through L_t between A_t groups and between retained and dropped groups, using cumulative inverse treatment and retention weights through that decision. Standardize continuous variables by their inner-training SD. Define B as 0.5 times the maximum absolute standardized difference plus 0.5 times the root mean square standardized difference across times, variables, and treatment or retention contrasts. Select the G and C pair with smallest mean B across inner folds; select Q by predictive loss, although Q is unused by this IPW point estimate. Break ties by predictive deviance and then fewer coefficients. Refit on outer training folds and use the untruncated Hajek estimator. | Use the fixed-weight empirical influence variance defined for predictive-CV IPW. | no | Apply the IPW fitting, probability, effective-sample-size, weight, estimate, and SE failure rules. A pair also fails if any required standardized difference is undefined. |
| Combined causal-score cross-fitted IPW | Evaluate all 4 by 3 by 3 = 36 G, C, and Q configurations in each inner validation fold. Define P as the average fractional rank of treatment deviance, retention deviance, and sequential outcome squared loss. Define B as the balance score above. Define W as max over regimes of log(p99 nonzero cumulative weight divided by its median) plus log(n_adherent divided by effective sample size). Define D from the longitudinal one-step influence function evaluated on the held-out inner fold using nuisance fits and the regime estimate from the corresponding inner training fold; specifically use the maximum over regimes of abs(mean(D_i))/sqrt(mean(D_i^2)). Convert P, B, W, and D to fractional ranks from 0 best to 1 worst among the 36 configurations within each inner fold. Select the configuration minimizing S = 0.25 rank(P) + 0.25 rank(B) + 0.25 rank(W) + 0.25 rank(D), averaged across inner folds. This D component is called held-out influence-function drift, not an identified estimate of the mathematical second-order remainder. Refit on the outer training fold and calculate the untruncated Hajek estimator. | Use the fixed-weight empirical influence variance defined for predictive-CV IPW. | no | A candidate receiving any failed fit or undefined component receives the worst rank. The replicate fails if every configuration fails or if the selected outer fit violates the common IPW failure rules. |
| Predictive-CV cross-fitted longitudinal one-step estimator | Use the predictively selected G, C, and Q candidates and outer-fold predictions. For each regime a, fit backward Q2(a) = E[Y|H2,A2=a,R3=1], Q1(a) = E[Q2(a)|H1,A1=a,R2=1], and Q0(a) = E[Q1(a)|H0,A0=a,R1=1]. Define Ht(a) as cumulative adherence and retention through t divided by the corresponding cross-fitted G and C probabilities. The person-level estimating contribution is phi_i(a) = Q0_i(a) + H0_i(a)[Q1_i(a)-Q0_i(a)] + H1_i(a)[Q2_i(a)-Q1_i(a)] + H2_i(a)[Y_i-Q2_i(a)]. Estimate the regime mean by mean(phi_i(a)) and subtract a=0 from a=1. | Let D_i = [phi_i(1)-mean(phi(1))] - [phi_i(0)-mean(phi(0))]. The model SE is sd(D_i)/sqrt(n), the cross-fitted empirical efficient-influence-function variance. | yes | A replicate fails if any selected G, C, or Q fit violates the common fit rules, required predictions are missing, either regime effective sample size is below 10, any cumulative clever-covariate weight exceeds 1e6, or the point estimate or SE is nonfinite. Estimates outside the observed outcome range are retained and flagged rather than relabeled as nonconvergence. |
| Balance-selected cross-fitted longitudinal one-step estimator | Use the balance-selected G and C pair, the predictively selected Q pipeline, and the same longitudinal one-step formula. | Use the cross-fitted empirical influence-function variance defined for the predictive-CV one-step estimator. | no | Apply the common balance-selection and one-step failure rules. |
| Combined causal-score cross-fitted longitudinal one-step estimator | Use the G, C, and Q configuration selected by the equal-weight combined score and the same longitudinal one-step formula. | Use the cross-fitted empirical influence-function variance defined for the predictive-CV one-step estimator. | no | Apply the common combined-selection and one-step failure rules. |
| Simulation-only library oracle one-step estimator | Calculate the one-step estimate for all 36 configurations from the already fitted outer-fold predictions. For replicate r, choose the single configuration having the lowest MSE in the other 999 replicates of that scenario and apply it to r. This leave-one-replicate-out oracle uses simulation truths and is not data-deployable. | Use the empirical influence-function variance for the configuration selected without replicate r. | no | A replicate fails if its leave-one-replicate-out oracle configuration fails under the one-step rules. |

**Performance.** Primary: Scenario-specific 95% coverage of the combined-score one-step interval versus predictive-CV and balance-selected one-step intervals. The primary selector contrast is the paired coverage difference within each scenario.. Secondary: Bias and absolute bias of the 36-month mean difference, Empirical SE, Mean model SE, Relative error in model SE, MSE, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for H0 that the marginal mean difference is zero, interpreted as power because the effect is nonzero, Convergence probability with Monte Carlo SE, Regime-specific bias, Selected-candidate frequencies, Maximum and root mean square longitudinal standardized differences, Effective sample size and the median, 95th, and 99th weight percentiles, Influence-function drift, Paired differences in absolute error and squared error, Runtime per replicate and per successful estimate. 1000 replicates. The target Monte Carlo SE is at most 0.01 for coverage as low as 0.90, the boundary used in the decision rule. For a Bernoulli coverage indicator, R must satisfy sqrt(0.90*0.10/R) <= 0.01, giving R >= 900. Using 1000 replicates gives MCSE sqrt(0.09/1000) = 0.00949 at 90% coverage and sqrt(0.95*0.05/1000) = 0.00689 at nominal coverage. Paired selector differences and all ADEMP measures will be reported with their harness-provided Monte Carlo SEs.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: The predictive-versus-causal selection problem is demonstrated in this design if all conditions hold. First, in at least 8 of 24 scenarios, including at least 2 scenarios with gamma_Z = 0 and at least 2 different nonlinear nuisance types, the combined-score one-step estimator reduces absolute bias by at least 0.02 outcome SD or raises coverage by at least 0.05 relative to predictive CV, and the paired Monte Carlo 95% interval excludes zero in the favorable direction. Second, the median reduction across all scenarios is at least 0.01 absolute-bias units or the median coverage gain is at least 0.03. Third, combined-score convergence is at least 95% in every scenario and is no more than 0.02 below predictive-CV convergence. Fourth, its MSE in the good-overlap, gamma_Z = 0, all-main-effects alignment cell is no more than 10% above predictive CV. To claim that the unresolved combined criterion adds value beyond the already published balance direction, it must also reduce absolute bias by at least 0.01 or improve coverage by at least 0.03 relative to balance-only selection in at least 4 nonlinear-treatment, nonlinear-censoring, or nonlinear-outcome scenarios.
- Problem is not real if: The problem is not demonstrated for this DGM and candidate library if predictive-CV one-step coverage lies between 0.925 and 0.975 and absolute bias is at most 0.02 in at least 20 of 24 scenarios, while the upper Monte Carlo 95% bound for the combined selector's improvement is below 0.01 for absolute bias and below 0.03 for coverage in those scenarios. Failure of the combined selector to beat balance-only selection also rules out a claim that the four-component score solves the component left open by published calibration work.
- Uninformative if: The result is uninformative if neither numerical rule is met; if the library oracle has absolute bias above 0.02 or coverage below 0.90 in more than 6 scenarios; if any primary method converges in fewer than 80% of replicates in any scenario; if truth Monte Carlo SE exceeds 0.0005; or if apparent gains occur only in strong-gamma_Z cells deliberately constructed to penalize treatment prediction. Such outcomes identify a library, numerical, or DGM limitation rather than evidence for or against the general methodological claim.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The treatment-only predictor and poor-overlap factors can build the desired phenomenon into the DGM. *Mitigation: Include gamma_Z = 0 and good-overlap alignment cells, publish every factorial cell, and require material improvement in at least two gamma_Z = 0 scenarios before declaring the problem real.*
- The equal-weight composite is arbitrary and may win only because of component scaling. *Mitigation: Use within-fold fractional ranks, prespecify equal weights, and report the prediction-heavy and leave-one-component-out sensitivity analyses without allowing them to replace the primary rule.*
- Held-out influence-function drift is not the unidentified second-order remainder. *Mitigation: Name it as a stability proxy, define it exactly, assess it by ablation, and make no theoretical claim that it consistently estimates the remainder.*
- A carefully curated finite library can make selection easier than real machine learning. *Mitigation: Include underfit, sufficient, prediction-rich, and expanded models; report oracle regret; and restrict conclusions to this GLM library. Do not claim validation for SuperLearner or highly adaptive algorithms.*
- The library contains a correct nuisance configuration in every scenario, which can overstate double-robust performance. *Mitigation: State this design feature explicitly and use it to isolate selection error. Treat simultaneous misspecification as future work rather than silently generalizing.*
- Post-selection inference can be nonregular, especially for IPW. *Mitigation: Perform all selection inside patient-level outer training folds. Evaluate both the common fixed-weight IPW interval and the orthogonal cross-fitted one-step interval, with coverage and bias-eliminated coverage as explicit outcomes.*
- Near-zero probabilities may reflect the true assignment process rather than overfitting. *Mitigation: Save true and estimated probability distributions separately, cross-classify failures by overlap and gamma_Z, and never interpret an extreme but correctly estimated probability as a prediction error.*
- Dropping failed replicates can make a method look accurate. *Mitigation: Use prespecified nonconvergence definitions, retain the planned denominator for convergence, report ADEMP measures both among successful estimates and with failures counted separately, and forbid silent replacement or retry with another candidate.*
- The fixed annual observation process omits the difficult informative-visit problem emphasized for electronic health records. *Mitigation: Limit the conclusion to fixed decision times with measured monotone dropout. State that sequential robustness under irregular informative observation remains untouched.*
- Protocol and analysis strategies may be mismatched. *Mitigation: Generate truth under the exact sustained strategies, no grace period, 36-month horizon, and elimination of censoring that the estimators target.*
- Truth error can be mistaken for estimator bias. *Mitigation: Use paired common-random-number truth evaluations at two million people, report truth MCSE, and extend the truth run if MCSE exceeds 0.0005.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The main run contains 24 scenarios times 1000 replicates = 24,000 replicates. Each training split fits 12 treatment models, 9 retention models, and 15 regime-specific sequential outcome regressions, totaling 36 glm fits. Two inner splits plus one outer refit for each of two outer folds give at most 216 fits per replicate, or 5,184,000 small glm fits overall; model matrices and nuisance fits are cached across the 36 configurations. On 6 workers, reserve 6 to 12 hours for the replicate run and 1.5 to 2 hours for the 24 truth calculations, including I/O and resumability overhead. Run a 100-replicate timing check before dispatch, but do not change the statistical replicate count based on results. The comparison uses zero ltmle fits and zero SuperLearner fits. A direct three-selector LTMLE extension would require 24*1000*3 = 72,000 fits; at 5 seconds each that is 100 core-hours or at least 16.7 ideal wall hours on 6 workers before overhead, so it does not meet the overnight constraint. If that extension later becomes essential, restrict it to 8 prespecified adverse scenarios, 500 replicates, and 3 selectors, giving 12,000 fits and coverage MCSE 0.0134 at 90%; label it a secondary external-validity check.

**Also bears on.** GMT-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Robins JM, Hernan MA, Brumback B. Marginal structural models and causal inference in epidemiology. Epidemiology. 2000;11(5):550-560.; Estimating inverse probability weights using super learner when weight-model specification is unknown in a marginal structural model. Statistics in Medicine. 2017.; Marginal structural models using calibrated weights with SuperLearner: application to type II diabetes cohort. IEEE Journal of Biomedical and Health Informatics. 2022.; Brookhart MA, Schneeweiss S, Rothman KJ, Glynn RJ, Avorn J, Sturmer T. Variable selection for propensity score models. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2006.; Feuerriegel S, Frauen D, Melnychuk V, et al. Causal machine learning for predicting treatment outcomes. Nature Medicine. 2024;30:958-968.
