## 4. TZO-01: Outcome-masked selection of a strategy-preserving time grid for natural grace-period emulations

*Catalog entry: No outcome-blind procedure selects the time grid or the grace period*

**Question.** Across 24 protocol scenarios, how much do two-year marginal risk-difference estimates move among 1-, 2-, 4-, and 8-week grids; does an outcome-blind process-intensity rule keep absolute bias below 0.01 and 95% coverage between 0.93 and 0.97; how much does the estimand change when the grace period changes from 4 to 12 weeks; and does a within-interval ordering-conflict rate above 0.01 identify grid movement above 0.02 with sensitivity and specificity of at least 0.80?

**Aims.** Estimate scenario-specific bias, coverage, and grid movement for a conventional single 4-week clone-censor-weight analysis, an outcome-blind selected grid, four prespecified nested grids, and an exact-order benchmark. Quantify the change in the causal risk difference between 4- and 12-week grace-period strategies while treating them as different estimands. Evaluate whether post-lock grid-invariance and ordering-conflict diagnostics identify materially unreliable coarse-grid results.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains an independent outcome-masked design sample of 1000 people and an analysis sample of 4000 people. Everyone is eligible at time 0 and is followed for 104 weeks. Draw age from N(60, 10^2) truncated to 40 through 80 and define Xage=(age-60)/10. Draw female F from Bernoulli(0.50), comorbidity C from Bernoulli(0.35), S0 from N(0.30 Xage+0.40 C-0.20 F, 1), and baseline recorded marker Z0=S0+N(0, 0.15^2). At the start of week k, untreated people initiate irreversible treatment with probability 1-exp[-exp(etaA)], where etaA is alphaA+0.75 Z+0.35 C+0.20 Xage-0.15 F+0.30 I(a visit occurred in the preceding week)+0.20 k/52. Treatment starts exactly at the weekly boundary. After the treatment decision, latent clinical state evolves as Sk=0.90 S(k-1)+0.05 Xage+0.08 C-0.04 F+N(0, 0.35^2); treatment does not affect this state. A visit is scheduled during week k with probability expit(alphaV+0.35 C+0.25 F+0.35 max(Z,0)+0.12 min(R,12)+0.10 sin(2 pi k/52)), where R is complete weeks since the last visit. Its time is k+Uniform(0.05,0.95). At a visit, Z is replaced by Sk+N(0,0.15^2); otherwise Z is carried forward and R increases by one. At most one visit occurs per week. The treatment and visit processes are generated independently of subsequent outcome innovations conditional on recorded history. The masked design sample retains treatment and visit histories but no outcome labels.
- Outcome model: The first nonfatal clinical event follows a piecewise exponential model. During week k its rate per week is exp[-6.10+0.30 Xage+0.40 C-0.20 F+0.55 Sk+0.15 k/52+psi(s,C)], where s is weeks since treatment initiation and psi is the scenario-specific log hazard ratio. An exponential waiting time is drawn within each week; an event occurs if the waiting time is below one. Exact event time is retained. Follow-up ends at the first event or week 104. There is no competing event or loss to follow-up. Treatment and visit histories do not respond to the nonfatal outcome, which permits strict outcome masking in the independent design sample.
- Treatment effect: Three profiles are used. Under the null profile, psi(s,C)=0. Under immediate benefit, psi(s,C)=log(0.60)+C log(0.80/0.60) from the instant of initiation onward. Under the biphasic profile, psi(s,C)=log(1.40)+C log(1.60/1.40) for 0 through less than 2 weeks after initiation, 0 for 2 through less than 6 weeks, and log(0.55)+C log(0.75/0.55) from week 6 onward. Treatment is irreversible and has no effect on the covariate, treatment, or visit processes.
- Effect modification: Comorbidity modifies treatment effects on the log hazard scale. For immediate benefit, the hazard ratio is 0.60 when C=0 and 0.80 when C=1. For the biphasic profile, the early hazard ratios are 1.40 and 1.60, and the delayed hazard ratios are 0.55 and 0.75, for C=0 and C=1 respectively. The null remains exactly null in both strata.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 2 visit intensities by 2 treatment pressures by 3 outcome profiles by 2 grace protocols gives 24 protocol scenarios. Only 12 observational datasets are generated per replicate because both grace protocols are evaluated on the same data. Eligibility, treatment assignment opportunities, and follow-up all begin at time 0. All candidate analysis grids preserve time 0, G, and week 104 as boundaries, retain the underlying weekly treatment decisions, and therefore target the same estimand within a grace protocol. Conditional exchangeability is true given recorded history. Treatment and visit laws are identical across the three outcome profiles. The null profile has no direct or mediated treatment effect.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot select a clinically meaningful grace period, prove that any process-only selector works for all outcome laws, or treat different grace periods as sensitivity analyses for one estimand. It cannot address treatment decisions or covariate changes within a week, competing risks, recurrent outcomes, fatal outcomes that alter subsequent care, multiple treatment versions, interference, unmeasured confounding, visits driven by unrecorded current health, or treatment-confounder feedback. Because the weekly propensity model has fixed dimension and is correctly specified, the study does not assess the nuisance-model burden of very fine grids, machine-learning estimators, irregular continuous-time decisions, or model misspecification beyond temporal aggregation.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Visit intensity | Frequent: alphaV=-1.75, producing roughly monthly observation, Sparse: alphaV=-3.45, producing roughly observation every 12 weeks | Monthly and approximately three-monthly observation reflect the frequencies compared by Sperling and colleagues. The two levels test whether a process-based selector becomes finer when recorded decision points are dense. |
| Natural treatment pressure | Lower uptake: alphaA=-4.05, targeting approximately 25% initiation by week 12, Higher uptake: alphaA=-2.85, targeting approximately 60% initiation by week 12 | These levels represent sparse and common early initiation while retaining practical positivity for both grace strategies. Realized initiation probabilities and effective sample sizes will be reported rather than assumed from nominal calibration. |
| Outcome effect timing | Exact null, Immediate heterogeneous benefit, Two-week early harm, four-week neutral period, then heterogeneous benefit | The null is a falsification condition. The immediate and biphasic profiles create distinct clinically plausible relationships between treatment timing and within-interval events. Crucially, all three share identical treatment and visit laws, so the outcome-blind selector cannot be engineered to recognize the favorable grid. |
| Grace-period protocol | G=4 weeks, G=12 weeks | Four and 12 weeks lie within the 0- to 180-day range documented in applied emulations and represent materially different clinical initiation windows. They are analyzed as different treatment strategies and estimands. |

**Estimands.**

- **Two-year risk difference for grace period G**: For G equal to 4 or 12 weeks, define gEarly,G as natural treatment initiation at weekly decisions before G followed by forced initiation at the week-G boundary among those still untreated. Define gDelay,G as prevention of initiation before G followed by return to the natural initiation process from G onward. The estimand is RDG=P(TY at or before 104 under gEarly,G)-P(TY at or before 104 under gDelay,G). It is a marginal superpopulation intention-to-follow-the-specified-dynamic-strategy contrast. Negative values favor earlier initiation. *Truth: For each of the 12 structural mechanisms, simulate 2000000 independent people under all four regimes gEarly,4, gDelay,4, gEarly,12, and gDelay,12 using common exogenous random numbers. The conservative independent-sample Monte Carlo standard error bound for each risk difference is sqrt(0.5/2000000)=0.00050. Use the paired empirical variance for the actual standard error. Run truth generation in independent batches and extend to 4000000 if the paired Monte Carlo standard error exceeds 0.00050.*
- **Change induced by changing the grace-period strategy**: DeltaG=RD12-RD4 on the absolute two-year risk scale. This is a contrast between two protocol-defined causal estimands, not movement of one fixed estimand under alternative analysis settings. *Truth: Compute DeltaG from the four coupled intervention risks. Without common random numbers, its conservative standard-error bound at 2000000 people is sqrt(1/2000000)=0.00071; the coupled simulation should be smaller. Extend to 4000000 if its paired Monte Carlo standard error exceeds 0.00050.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Single 4-week endpoint clone-censor-weight analysis | Clone every analysis-sample person to gEarly,G and gDelay,G. For gDelay,G, starts before G cause artificial censoring at the right endpoint of the containing 4-week interval. For gEarly,G, natural starts before G remain adherent; a person untreated at G remains only if natural initiation occurs at G and receives inverse probability 1/pA(G). Estimate weekly pA with a complementary-log-log GLM containing the exact DGM treatment terms. For gDelay,G, multiply inverse probabilities 1/(1-pA(k)) through k<G. Estimate each arm's risk with a normalized weighted product-limit estimator and subtract risks. Intervals are split at G and week 104. | Stack the initiation-model score with the two arm-specific weighted hazard scores. Use the empirical original-person cluster sandwich so the covariance between clones and uncertainty from estimated weights are included. Apply the delta method to the two product-limit risks and their difference. | yes | Nonconvergence occurs if the initiation GLM reports failure, has a nonfinite coefficient, has Hessian condition number above 1e12, produces a nonfinite adherence weight, leaves an empty arm risk set before week 104, or produces a nonpositive or nonfinite sandwich variance. Extreme weights alone are not reclassified as nonconvergence. |
| Prespecified fixed-grid multiverse | Repeat the endpoint clone-censor-weight estimator on nested 1-, 2-, 4-, and 8-week grids. Insert G and week 104 as boundaries even when they interrupt a regular interval. Report all four risk differences, their signed differences from the 1-week estimate, and M=max(RD1,RD2,RD4,RD8)-min(RD1,RD2,RD4,RD8). No grid is selected from these outcome results. | Use one joint stacked original-person cluster sandwich containing the shared initiation-model score and all grid-specific hazard scores. This supplies covariance for paired differences among grids. | no | Apply the fixed-grid criteria separately to each grid. Retain the replicate for methods that converge, flag every failed grid, and require all four grids for calculation of M. |
| Outcome-blind process-adaptive selected-grid CCW | Before revealing any analysis-sample outcomes, use the independent 1000-person design sample. For each candidate grid and each person-bin, count visits plus treatment starts. Let aDelta be the number of bins containing at least two such events divided by the number containing at least one. Select the coarsest grid whose one-sided 95% Wilson upper bound for aDelta is at most 0.20. Require at least 50 treatment starts and 500 visits in the design sample; if no candidate passes the aliasing rule, select 1 week, and if the support counts fail, declare the selector unsupported. Lock the selected grid, reveal analysis outcomes, and apply the same endpoint CCW estimator. | Use the stacked original-person cluster sandwich for the locked grid. The grid is chosen in an independent design sample, so the interval is conditional on the locked grid and does not require an outcome-selection correction. Empirical coverage across replicates evaluates the full two-sample procedure. | no | The method fails if the design sample does not meet the support counts, the locked-grid initiation model or hazard estimator meets a fixed-grid failure criterion, or the sandwich variance is nonpositive or nonfinite. |
| Exact-order clone-censor-weight benchmark | Use the same weekly propensity estimates and grace strategies, but censor a gDelay,G clone at the exact weekly boundary of a prohibited start before accepting any subsequent outcome. Apply the forced action for gEarly,G exactly at G. Estimate arm risks by a weighted product-limit calculation using exact event and censoring times. | Use the stacked original-person cluster sandwich with exact-time weighted hazard increments and the shared initiation-model score. | no | Nonconvergence uses the same GLM, risk-set, weight, and sandwich criteria as the fixed-grid analyses. |

**Performance.** Primary: Empirical coverage of the nominal 95% interval for the outcome-blind selected-grid risk difference in each scenario.. Secondary: Bias and absolute bias of every risk-difference estimator, Empirical standard error, Mean model-based standard error, Relative error in model-based standard error, Mean squared error, Bias-eliminated coverage, Null rejection rate and non-null rejection rate, Convergence and selector-support rates, Selection frequencies for the four grids, Mean, median, 90th percentile, and 95th percentile of M, Signed movement of each coarse-grid estimate from the 1-week and exact-order estimates, DeltaG bias and Monte Carlo uncertainty, Maximum weight, weight coefficient of variation, and arm-specific effective sample size, Ordering-conflict rate ODelta, defined as the proportion of observed first outcomes in gDelay,G clones that occur after a prohibited treatment start but before the right endpoint of the same grid interval, Sensitivity and specificity of O4 greater than 0.01 for identifying M greater than 0.02 and absolute 4-week bias greater than 0.01. 2000 replicates. For coverage near 0.95, MCSE=sqrt[0.95(1-0.95)/R]. Requiring MCSE at most 0.005 gives R at least 0.0475/0.000025=1900. Use 2000 replicates, giving MCSE 0.00487. The existing harness will calculate realized Monte Carlo standard errors for every performance measure.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Define a scenario as materially grid-sensitive when the lower 95% Monte Carlo confidence bound for mean M exceeds 0.02, the Monte Carlo interval for 4-week bias lies wholly outside -0.01 to 0.01, or the upper Monte Carlo confidence bound for 4-week coverage is below 0.90. Conclude that grid choice is a real practical problem within this domain if at least 6 of 24 scenarios meet a criterion and the affected scenarios include both visit-intensity levels and both grace periods. Call the proposed selector a successful partial solution only if at least 22 of 24 scenarios have absolute bias no greater than 0.01, coverage between 0.93 and 0.97, and bias no more than 0.005 worse than the exact-order benchmark. Call O4 useful only if both sensitivity and specificity are at least 0.80. Separately call the grace-period contrast material when the Monte Carlo interval for DeltaG lies wholly outside -0.02 to 0.02; this does not imply that either grace period is incorrect.
- Problem is not real if: Within this simulated domain, conclude that meaningful grid dependence was not demonstrated only if all 24 scenarios have an upper 95% Monte Carlo bound for mean M below 0.01, Monte Carlo intervals for both 4-week and selected-grid bias wholly inside -0.005 to 0.005, and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97. This conclusion does not establish that a clinically meaningful grace period can be selected from process data.
- Uninformative if: Classify the result as uninformative if it falls between the real and not-real regions, if more than 5% of replicates fail for either the status quo or selected method in any scenario, if median arm-specific effective sample size falls below 100, if truth Monte Carlo standard error remains above 0.00050 after extension, or if Monte Carlo intervals cross the materiality thresholds in more than half of the scenarios.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A DGM constructed to place treatment and outcomes inside the same coarse intervals can manufacture the desired finding. *Mitigation: Prespecify all coefficients before running outcomes; include exact-null, immediate-benefit, and biphasic profiles under identical treatment and visit laws; report every factorial cell; and compare endpoint results with an exact-order benchmark. The null cells measure false indications of grid sensitivity, while the paired outcome profiles expose both favorable and unfavorable cases for the outcome-blind selector.*
- Changing G changes the treatment strategy and usually changes the causal estimand. *Mitigation: Define gEarly,G and gDelay,G separately for each G, compute separate truths, and label DeltaG as a contrast between estimands. Never use agreement across G as a validity requirement or select G from estimated outcomes.*
- Treatment and visit processes cannot reveal outcome latency or determine a uniquely adequate grid. *Mitigation: Hold those processes exactly constant across the three outcome profiles. Interpret selector performance as domain-specific operating characteristics, not proof that process information identifies the correct grid.*
- The independent design sample gives the selector more information and cleaner masking than many applied studies possess. *Mitigation: Count its 1000 participants in the computational and data requirements, report unsupported selections, and repeat the selector using design samples of 500 and 2000 as secondary sample-size checks.*
- Correct weekly propensity specification may make weighting look better than it is in practice. *Mitigation: State that the design isolates temporal aggregation. Report weight distributions and effective sample sizes. Do not generalize results to flexible nuisance estimation, latent confounding, or misspecified treatment models.*
- Artificial censoring weights may become unstable when early initiation is uncommon. *Mitigation: Factor treatment pressure explicitly; report untruncated primary results, convergence, maximum weights, and effective sample sizes; and repeat with a hard cap of 20 and empirical 1st- and 99th-percentile truncation.*
- The one-week base process and one visit per week omit finer and more irregular ordering. *Mitigation: Retain continuous visit and outcome timestamps within weeks, but limit conclusions to protocols with weekly treatment decisions. State explicitly that subweekly treatment decisions and multiple same-week visits remain untested.*
- A nonfatal outcome that does not alter subsequent care makes strict masking easier and may understate real feedback. *Mitigation: Use the independent design sample only for selection and stop analysis risk sets at the first outcome. List fatal outcomes and post-outcome treatment or visit changes as required extensions, not as claims answered here.*
- Software mistakes in event ordering could reproduce the bias being studied. *Mitigation: Unit-test hand-constructed trajectories with treatment immediately before and after an outcome; require numerical agreement between the 1-week and exact-order estimators; verify null truths near zero; and save replicate-level seeds and failure records through the existing resumable harness.*
- Twenty-four cells cannot establish universal diagnostic sensitivity or specificity. *Mitigation: Use the numerical diagnostic thresholds only for this prespecified domain, report binomial uncertainty, and publish cell-level results so later studies can add mechanisms without changing the original decision rule.*

**Runtime as estimated.** There are 24 protocol scenarios times 2000 replicates, or 48000 scenario-replicates. Pairing both G values means only 12 times 2000, or 24000, observational datasets are generated. Each dataset requires one weekly complementary-log-log GLM; all four grids, both grace protocols, product-limit estimates, and stacked scores reuse that fit. Thus the main run contains approximately 24000 GLM fits and no longitudinal TMLE or SuperLearner fits. Twelve coupled truth jobs each simulate 2000000 people under four regimes. Streaming summaries avoid storing person-period data across replicates. On 6 workers, allow approximately 3 to 4 hours for truth generation and 3 to 6 hours for the replicate run, diagnostics, and serialization. A 100-replicate timing run must precede release; projected total time above 12 hours triggers reduction of truth batches only after their paired MCSE requirement is met, not reduction of the 2000 analysis replicates.

**Also bears on.** TZO-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019.; Hernan MA, Sauer BC, Hernandez-Diaz S, Platt R, Shrier I. Specifying a target trial prevents immortal time bias and other self-inflicted injuries in observational analyses. Journal of Clinical Epidemiology. 2016.; Cain LE, Robins JM, Lanoy E, Logan R, Costagliola D, Hernan MA. Using observational data to emulate a randomized trial of dynamic treatment-switching strategies. International Journal of Epidemiology. 2016.; Maringe C, Benitez Majano S, Exarchakou A, et al. Reflection on modern methods: trial emulation in the presence of immortal-time bias. International Journal of Epidemiology. 2020.; Ferreira Guerra et al. Impact of discretization of the timeline for longitudinal causal inference methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2020.; Sperling et al. Optimal CD4 count for initiating HIV treatment: impact of CD4 observation frequency and grace periods, and performance of dynamic marginal structural models. Epidemiology. 2014.; Tankpinou Zoumenou H, et al. Time Partitioning in Target Trial Emulation. 2026.; Pharmacoepidemiology and Drug Safety systematic review identified in the catalog audit.; Wanis et al. work on natural and stochastic grace-period strategies.

## 5. PRO-04: When does an operational protocol substitution materially change the target? A factorial benchmark across eligibility, strategy, and outcome components

*Catalog entry: An unobservable protocol component produces a different, data-defined estimand rather than a noisy version of the intended one*

**Question.** Across 24 prespecified mechanisms involving eligibility misclassification, treatment-strategy coarsening, or incomplete endpoint capture, what is the signed difference in the marginal 24-month risk difference between the intended and operational protocols; in which scenarios does its absolute value exceed 0.02; how often does a nominal 95% interval for the operational effect cover the intended effect; and can a 20% random validation sample recover the intended effect and distinguish numerically preserving from question-changing substitutions?

**Aims.** For every scenario, estimate the intended effect psi_I, the operational effect psi_O, and Delta = psi_O - psi_I. Determine whether silent reporting of psi_O as psi_I causes material bias or undercoverage despite valid inference for psi_O. Test whether recovery from a valid internal validation sample produces absolute bias no greater than 0.005 and 95% coverage between 0.93 and 0.97. Evaluate a prespecified paired validation diagnostic that classifies substitutions as numerically preserving, changed, or indeterminate. Numerical agreement is not treated as proof that two estimands are conceptually identical.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 4000 independent individuals. Generate X1 from Bernoulli(0.50), X2 from Uniform(-1,1), and X3 from Bernoulli(expit(-0.30 + 0.60 X1 + 0.50 X2)). Generate B = 0.70 X1 + 0.60 X2 + 0.80 X3 + epsilon_B, where epsilon_B is standard normal, and define ideal eligibility E = I(B >= 0.65). Generate validation membership V from Bernoulli(0.20), independently of every baseline variable, assignment, potential outcome, and operational measurement. All remaining random draws are mutually independent conditional on the stated models. The intended protocol enrolls E = 1 individuals at baseline, assigns one of three fully specified strategies, follows everyone for 24 months without censoring, and measures a binary clinical endpoint. In each scenario exactly one component is operationally compromised. The gold-standard value of that component is available only when V = 1; the other two components are observed exactly.
- Outcome model: Let p0 = 0.16 + 0.05 X1 + 0.04 X2 + 0.06 X3 + 0.05(1-E). Strategy 0 is no treatment, strategy 1 is low intensity, and strategy 2 is high intensity. With effect modification absent, set d1 = d2 = -0.06. With effect modification present, set d1 = -0.02 + 0.02(1-E) + 0.02 X1 and d2 = -0.10 + 0.08(1-E) + 0.04 X1. Define p_z = p0 + d_z, with d0 = 0. These probabilities range from 0.02 to 0.38, so no truncation is used. Generate a common U_Y from Uniform(0,1) and define Y^z = I(U_Y < p_z); the true observed endpoint is Y = Y^Z. The model is on the risk scale. Consequently, the absent-modification setting has a constant risk difference, while the present setting makes the high-intensity benefit larger among eligible individuals with X1 = 0 and small or absent outside the intended population.
- Treatment effect: For eligibility and outcome scenarios, assign Z independently with probabilities 0.50, 0.10, and 0.40 for strategies 0, 1, and 2. For strategy-coarsening scenarios, first assign A from Bernoulli(0.50). If A = 0, set Z = 0. If A = 1, assign Z = 2 with the scenario-specific probability listed under the severity and dependence factors; otherwise set Z = 1. The operational record retains only A_star = I(Z > 0), so low-intensity and high-intensity strategies are merged. The intended contrast always sets Z = 2 versus Z = 0. The operational strategy contrast sets A_star = 1, which invokes the scenario-specific mixture of strategies 1 and 2, versus A_star = 0.
- Effect modification: The absent level makes both active strategies reduce every individual's 24-month risk by exactly 0.06. This is a negative-control setting in which eligibility changes and strategy merging can alter the protocol definition without changing the numerical risk difference. The present level modifies treatment effects jointly by ideal eligibility and X1 using the coefficients in the outcome model. This creates a setting in which changing the enrolled population or the composition of a merged strategy can change the marginal contrast.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 components by 2 accuracy levels by 2 effect-modification levels by 2 dependence levels. Assignment probabilities are known, positivity holds, there is no loss to follow-up, and there is no confounding of the randomized protocol contrasts. Every operational strategy is given an explicit stochastic mixture, every recorded endpoint has an explicit measurement intervention, and validation is missing completely at random. Thus each operational quantity is well-defined and the intended effect is recoverable from validation data. The absent-modification cells deliberately provide cases where a protocol substitution need not create a numerically different risk difference.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot estimate how frequently these mechanisms occur in practice. It cannot assess simultaneous compromise of multiple components, irregular visits, censoring, time-varying treatment or confounding, positivity violations, estimated propensity models, nonrandom validation, unknown measurement models, survival estimands, or scales other than the 24-month risk difference. It also cannot address an operational proxy that identifies only a statistical functional with no coherent intervention-based interpretation, because coherent operational targets are deliberately built into this DGM. It cannot show that a component mapping table changes reporting behavior or establish a universal language for partially specified interventions.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Compromised protocol component | Eligibility: replace E with E_star and analyze E_star = 1, Treatment strategy: replace exact Z with A_star = I(Z > 0), Outcome: replace Y with the recorded endpoint Y_star | These instantiate the three concrete substitutions in PRO-04: an unverifiable criterion changes the population, indistinguishable strategies change the intervention contrast, and incomplete capture changes the endpoint. The remaining protocol components are held fixed to isolate each substitution. |
| Operational accuracy | Mild: under nondifferential mapping, eligibility sensitivity and specificity are 0.90 and 0.95; 85% of active assignments are high intensity; outcome sensitivity is 0.90, Severe: under nondifferential mapping, eligibility sensitivity and specificity are 0.70 and 0.85; 55% of active assignments are high intensity; outcome sensitivity is 0.70 | The levels represent a usable but imperfect record definition and a substantially coarser definition. They are transparent stress-test bands rather than estimates attributed to a particular disease. Outcome specificity is 0.995 at both levels, reflecting a setting in which missed events are more common than false events. |
| Treatment-effect modification | Absent: d1 = d2 = -0.06 for every individual, Present: d1 = -0.02 + 0.02(1-E) + 0.02 X1 and d2 = -0.10 + 0.08(1-E) + 0.04 X1 | Substitution should not be forced to matter. The absent level provides exact numerical preservation controls for eligibility and strategy substitutions. The present level represents plausible qualitative heterogeneity across a clinical eligibility criterion and a measured patient characteristic. |
| Operational dependence | Nondifferential: eligibility error is independent of X1 given E; active-strategy composition is independent of X1; outcome sensitivity is independent of assigned strategy, Differential: for eligibility, mild sensitivity and specificity are 0.95 and 0.97 when X1 = 0 and 0.80 and 0.90 when X1 = 1, while severe values are 0.80 and 0.90 when X1 = 0 and 0.55 and 0.75 when X1 = 1; for strategy coarsening, P(Z = 2 | A = 1,X1) is 0.95 and 0.70 across X1 = 0 and 1 under mild severity and 0.70 and 0.40 under severe severity; for outcome capture, sensitivities for Z = 0, 1, and 2 are 0.95, 0.88, and 0.75 under mild severity and 0.80, 0.65, and 0.50 under severe severity | Dependence on an effect modifier or assigned strategy is the mechanism most likely to turn operational coarsening into a material change. For outcome scenarios, generate Y_star with the stated sensitivity when Y = 1 and false-positive probability 0.005 when Y = 0. For eligibility scenarios, generate E_star from the stated sensitivity and specificity. Components not selected for compromise equal their ideal values. |

**Estimands.**

- **Intended protocol effect, psi_I**: The superpopulation marginal 24-month risk difference P(Y^2 = 1 | E = 1) - P(Y^0 = 1 | E = 1), comparing assignment to the fully specified high-intensity strategy with assignment to no treatment among individuals satisfying the ideal eligibility criterion. The endpoint is the true clinical event, follow-up is fixed at 24 months, and there is no censoring. *Truth: For each scenario, generate 2000000 baseline covariate and eligibility draws. Average p2 and p0 among E = 1 rather than drawing Bernoulli outcomes, then subtract. Use the same baseline draws for every estimand in the scenario. Estimate Monte Carlo error by 100 equal-sized batch means. Require the Monte Carlo SE to be at most 0.0005; if it is larger, add draws in blocks of 1000000 until the criterion is met.*
- **Operational protocol effect, psi_O**: For eligibility scenarios, psi_O is P(Y^2 = 1 | E_star = 1) - P(Y^0 = 1 | E_star = 1). For strategy scenarios, psi_O is the risk under A_star = 1, which assigns the scenario-specific X1-dependent or X1-independent mixture of Z = 1 and Z = 2, minus the risk under A_star = 0, among E = 1. For outcome scenarios, psi_O is P(Y_star^2 = 1 | E = 1) - P(Y_star^0 = 1 | E = 1). Each is a marginal superpopulation 24-month risk difference under the operational protocol. *Truth: Use the same 2000000 baseline draws as for psi_I. Integrate eligibility classification probabilities, strategy-mixture probabilities, and endpoint sensitivity and specificity analytically conditional on each draw. This avoids unnecessary Bernoulli noise. Use batch means and require Monte Carlo SE at most 0.0005.*
- **Protocol substitution gap, Delta**: Delta = psi_O - psi_I on the risk-difference scale. A scenario is numerically preserving when |Delta| <= 0.01, materially changed when |Delta| >= 0.02, and in a gray region otherwise. These are methodological decision margins, not disease-specific minimal clinically important differences. Equality within the margin means numerical benchmark agreement only; it does not make the two protocol definitions identical. *Truth: Subtract paired truth estimates computed with common baseline draws. Estimate the Monte Carlo SE of Delta directly from batch-specific paired differences and require it to be at most 0.0007. The 0.01 and 0.02 decision margins are therefore at least 14 times the maximum accepted truth MCSE.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Silent operational analysis | This is the status quo comparator. It ignores validation data, analyzes the operational eligibility population, strategy, or endpoint, and reports the resulting risk difference as though it were psi_I. For arm t, define h_it = S_i I(T_i = t)/pi_t(X_i), d_t = mean(h_it), and mu_t = mean(h_it W_i)/d_t. Report mu_1 - mu_0, where eligibility scenarios use S = E_star, T = Z in {2,0}, W = Y, and pi_2 = 0.40 and pi_0 = 0.50; strategy scenarios use S = E, T = A_star in {1,0}, W = Y, and both probabilities equal 0.50; outcome scenarios use S = E, T = Z in {2,0}, W = Y_star, and pi_2 = 0.40 and pi_0 = 0.50. | For each arm, compute IF_it = h_it(W_i - mu_t)/d_t. For the risk difference compute IF_i = IF_i1 - IF_i0 and SE = sd(IF_i)/sqrt(4000), using the sample standard deviation. This is the empirical influence-function sandwich SE for the two Hájek means with known assignment probabilities. | yes | Declare failure if either weighted denominator is zero or if the estimate or SE is nonfinite. Retain estimates with arm-specific effective sample size below 20 but flag them as unstable; do not silently discard them. |
| Explicitly labeled operational analysis | Use exactly the same observations, weights, and numerical estimator as the silent operational analysis, but declare psi_O as its target and attach the component-specific operational protocol definition to the result. | Use the same arm influence functions and sandwich SE as the silent analysis. Evaluate model SE, bias, and coverage against psi_O rather than psi_I. | no | The same zero-denominator and nonfinite-value rule as the silent analysis. |
| Internal-validation recovery of the intended effect | Use only records with V = 1 and the validated ideal component. Set S = E, T = Z in {2,0}, and W = Y. Use h_it = V_i S_i I(T_i = t)/[0.20 pi_t(X_i)] and the Hájek arm means. In strategy scenarios, pi_2(X1) = 0.50 q(X1), with q given by the scenario, and pi_0 = 0.50. In eligibility and outcome scenarios, pi_2 = 0.40 and pi_0 = 0.50. The contrast targets psi_I. | Use IF_it = h_it(W_i - mu_t)/mean(h_it), IF_i = IF_i2 - IF_i0, and SE = sd(IF_i)/sqrt(4000). The validation probability and assignment probabilities are treated as known because they are generated by the protocol. | no | Declare failure if no validated, ideally eligible individual has positive weight in either required arm, or if an estimate or SE is nonfinite. Record effective sample size below 20 as instability without deleting the replicate. |
| Full-data ideal oracle | Use all simulated gold-standard E, Z, and Y values with the same known-probability Hájek estimator as the validation method, omitting V/0.20. This method is unavailable in the operational dataset and exists only to verify the simulation and interval machinery. | Use the empirical influence-function sandwich SE for the two weighted arm means, with known assignment probabilities. | no | Declare failure for a zero weighted arm denominator or a nonfinite estimate or SE. |
| Paired validation estimand-gap diagnostic | Within V = 1, estimate psi_O,V using operational variables and psi_I,V using ideal variables on the same individuals, then calculate Delta_hat = psi_O,V - psi_I,V. Use the same known assignment and validation weights as the preceding methods. Classify a replicate as numerically preserving when the entire 90% interval for Delta lies inside [-0.01,0.01]. Classify it as changed when the point estimate has absolute value at least 0.02 and the 95% interval lies wholly outside [-0.01,0.01]. Otherwise classify it as indeterminate. | Calculate the influence function for each constituent risk difference on the common validation sample. Use IF_Delta,i = IF_O,i - IF_I,i so that covariance from shared individuals is retained. Set SE_Delta = sd(IF_Delta)/sqrt(4000). | no | Declare failure if either constituent estimator fails. An indeterminate classification is a substantive result, not nonconvergence. |

**Performance.** Primary: Per-scenario empirical coverage of the silent operational 95% interval for psi_I. This directly measures how often a statistically calibrated interval is attached to the wrong protocol target.. Secondary: Signed and absolute true Delta for every scenario, Bias of each estimator against its declared estimand, plus silent-estimator bias against both psi_I and psi_O, Empirical SE, Mean influence-function model SE, Relative error in model SE, MSE, Coverage against psi_I and psi_O as applicable, Bias-eliminated coverage, Two-sided rejection rate for a zero risk difference, Nonconvergence and low-effective-sample-size rates, Changed-class sensitivity, preserving-class specificity, and indeterminate rate for the paired validation diagnostic. 2500 replicates. For a coverage proportion p, Monte Carlo SE is sqrt[p(1-p)/R]. Requiring at most 0.01 under the worst case p = 0.50 gives R >= 0.25/0.01^2 = 2500. At nominal coverage p = 0.95, R = 2500 gives Monte Carlo SE sqrt(0.95 x 0.05/2500) = 0.00436. Thus deviations of approximately 0.02 from nominal coverage are well separated from Monte Carlo noise.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Restrict the conclusion to this DGM family. First require calibration: the full-data oracle and explicitly labeled operational estimator must each have absolute bias <= 0.005 and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97 in at least 22 of 24 scenarios. Then call silent substitution empirically consequential if every component family contains at least one scenario with true |Delta| >= 0.02, silent intended-estimand coverage is <= 0.80 in at least 75% of materially changed scenarios, and silent intended-estimand coverage is >= 0.93 in at least 75% of numerically preserving scenarios. Call validation recovery successful if it has absolute bias <= 0.005 and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97 in at least 22 scenarios. Call the paired diagnostic usable only if changed-class sensitivity is >= 0.80, preserving-class specificity is >= 0.80, and its indeterminate probability is <= 0.25 after averaging equally across scenarios in each true class.
- Problem is not real if: Provided the oracle and explicitly labeled operational analyses pass calibration, conclude that no practically important numerical mismatch was demonstrated if all 24 scenarios have true |Delta| <= 0.01 and silent intended-estimand coverage lies between 0.93 and 0.97 in at least 22 scenarios. This conclusion applies only to the specified accuracy, dependence, effect, and risk ranges; it does not establish conceptual identity of the protocols.
- Uninformative if: Treat the study as uninformative if truth Monte Carlo SE exceeds its threshold, either calibration estimator fails the bias or coverage guard in more than two scenarios, an algebraic negative-control cell has |Delta| > 0.01, more than half of the scenarios fall in the 0.01 < |Delta| < 0.02 gray region, or binomial Monte Carlo intervals around the scenario-count criteria cross the stated thresholds. If material gaps occur but the paired diagnostic misses its accuracy thresholds, conclude that the substitution problem is demonstrated but the proposed sample-level separator remains unanswered.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A DGM built only from heterogeneous effects and differential measurement would guarantee the desired finding. *Mitigation: The factorial includes all cells, including exact negative controls. With absent effect modification, eligibility replacement and strategy merging preserve the risk difference by construction even when classification or strategy labels are coarse. Mild nondifferential outcome capture is expected to produce only a small gap. No cell will be removed after truth is computed, and results will be displayed continuously rather than only as threshold crossings.*
- Agreement with an ideal-trial estimate can arise from compensating biases and therefore does not prove that the estimand was preserved. *Mitigation: Define psi_I and psi_O through separate interventions before generating data and compute both structural truths. Use Delta between those truths as the benchmark. Estimator agreement alone never determines a scenario's preserving or changed label.*
- Known randomization, complete follow-up, and random validation make recovery easier than in observational emulations. *Mitigation: These assumptions isolate estimand mismatch from confounding, censoring, and validation-selection bias. State explicitly that successful recovery establishes possibility under a valid measurement or missing-data design, not general recoverability in routine EHR data.*
- Only one component is compromised at a time, while real emulations can compromise several linked components. *Mitigation: Label conclusions as component-specific first-order effects. Release the generator so a later study can cross multiple substitutions, but do not expand this 24-cell study and confound interpretation.*
- The operational strategy is deliberately made into a coherent stochastic intervention, which excludes mappings with no causal interpretation. *Mitigation: Write the operational mixture probabilities into the protocol and describe this as a boundary of the study. Do not call a generic observed-data functional a substituted causal estimand based on these results.*
- The 0.01 preservation and 0.02 change margins are judgment calls. *Mitigation: Report signed Delta and component risks without categorization. Repeat only the classification summary at margin pairs 0.005 and 0.015, and 0.02 and 0.03, while retaining the prespecified primary decision rule.*
- A 20% validation sample may yield correct but imprecise recovery and many indeterminate diagnostic results. *Mitigation: Treat indeterminate as an outcome rather than forcing a classification. Report effective sample sizes and interval widths. Separate calibration of the validation estimator from the power of the gap diagnostic.*
- Wald interval failure could be mistaken for estimand mismatch. *Mitigation: The DGM keeps event risks away from zero and one and provides substantial expected arm counts. Require oracle and correctly labeled operational coverage calibration before interpreting silent undercoverage. Also report bias-eliminated coverage and low-effective-sample-size flags.*
- The accuracy and differential-measurement values are not calibrated to one named clinical database. *Mitigation: Describe them as stress-test bands, publish every parameter, and avoid claims about prevalence in applied emulations. The study establishes conditional numerical behavior. External empirical calibration remains necessary before claiming how common each regime is.*
- A fixed baseline assignment and 24-month endpoint omit time-varying protocol failures. *Mitigation: State this limitation in the protocol and catalog attachment. Do not extrapolate results to grace periods, treatment switching, irregular observation, dynamic regimes, or informative loss to follow-up.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The design has 24 x 2500 = 60000 replicate-scenarios. Each replicate requires weighted sums for three unique estimators plus the paired diagnostic; it requires zero glm, ltmle, SuperLearner, Stan, or cmdstanr fits. Scaling from the established machine benchmark suggests approximately 1 to 2 hours for replicates on 6 workers. Truth enumeration at the conservative observed rate costs 24 x 4 minutes = 96 minutes. Allowing 30 to 60 minutes for checkpointing, summaries, figures, and validation gives an expected total of 3 to 5 hours, comfortably within an overnight run.

**Also bears on.** PRO-04 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Hernán MA, Robins JM. Using big data to emulate a target trial when a randomized trial is not available. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2016;183(8):758-764.; Moreno-Betancur M, Wijesuriya R, Carlin JB. The ideal trial: defining causal estimands that balance relevance and feasibility in target trial emulations and actual randomized trials. Epidemiology. 2026;37(2):153-162.; Wang, et al. An operational target trial emulation framework for causal inference using electronic health records. npj Digital Medicine. 2026.; Applying the estimand and target trial frameworks to external control analyses using observational data: a case study. Frontiers in Pharmacology. 2024.

## 6. PRO-02: Coverage after data-adaptive selection of a target trial protocol

*Catalog entry: Inference conditions on a fixed target trial that was in fact selected after seeing the data*

**Question.** Across 24 scenarios varying cohort size, outcome frequency, candidate-library size and treatment-effect structure, what is the marginal coverage of a nominal 95% confidence interval for the selected protocol's risk difference when selection uses pooled outcome prevalence or covariate-outcome associations, how many percentage points of coverage are lost relative to prespecified-protocol inference, and do 50:50 sample splitting or simultaneous Bonferroni intervals restore coverage to at least 93%?

**Aims.** The primary aim is to estimate coverage for the randomly selected superpopulation estimand under an outcome-count workability selector that never examines a treatment effect. Secondary aims are to estimate coverage under prognostic-association selection, an effect-seeking positive control and an outcome-blinded selector; quantify bias, interval width and nonconvergence; compare ordinary intervals with sample splitting and simultaneous inference; and determine whether the observed multiverse spread predicts post-selection bias or noncoverage. The study evaluates inferential corrections for a recorded algorithmic search. It does not repeat the already demonstrated use of multiverse displays as descriptive analyses.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate independent individuals. Draw U_Z uniformly between Phi(-2) and Phi(2), set Z=Phi_inverse(U_Z), and define age=55+10Z years. Draw S from Bernoulli(0.50). Draw C from Bernoulli(expit(-0.40+0.50Z+0.35S)). Draw epsilon_B from N(0,1) and set B=0.30Z+0.40(C-0.5)+0.20(S-0.5)+0.80epsilon_B. Draw baseline treatment A from Bernoulli(e), where logit(e)=-0.30+0.45Z+0.35(S-0.5)+0.60(C-0.5)+0.35B. A denotes initiation at time zero followed by perfect adherence through follow-up; A=0 denotes no initiation. Everyone is event-free at time zero. There is no loss to follow-up or competing event. Candidate protocols compare these strategies among people with age at least c and use first event by horizon h. The K=4 library is c in {50,55} crossed with h in {36,60} months. The K=16 library is c in {45,50,55,60} crossed with h in {24,36,48,60} months. The prespecified reference protocol is c=55 and h=60.
- Outcome model: Draw U_T from Uniform(0,1). Let lambda0 be 0.00070 or 0.00320 per month. Define eta(A)=0.35Z+0.25(S-0.5)+0.50(C-0.5)+0.30B+A(theta0+theta1Z), and set T=-log(U_T)/(lambda0*exp(eta(A))). Observe time=min(T,60), delta=I(T<=60), and Y_h=I(T<=h) for every candidate horizon. Thus the conditional hazard is constant in time on the proportional-hazards scale. The two lambda0 values are calibrated to approximately 5% and 20% untreated 60-month risks in the unrestricted population; exact risks are recorded from the independent truth calculation.
- Treatment effect: Three structures are used. The null structure sets theta0=0 and theta1=0. The homogeneous protective structure sets theta0=-0.287682072, corresponding to a hazard ratio of 0.75, and theta1=0. The heterogeneous structure sets theta0=-0.287682072 and theta1=0.25.
- Effect modification: Only the heterogeneous structure contains effect modification. Its conditional hazard ratio is exp(-0.287682072+0.25Z), equal to 0.455, 0.750 and 1.237 at Z=-2, 0 and 2. This creates protocol-specific causal effects because the age eligibility thresholds select different distributions of Z. The null and homogeneous structures ensure that any finding is not built entirely from effect heterogeneity.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 2 cohort sizes by 2 outcome frequencies by 2 library sizes by 3 effect structures. Consistency, conditional exchangeability given Z, S, C and B, positivity, independent sampling, perfect measurement and a correctly specified propensity model are deliberately true. Every candidate is a legitimate target trial with time zero, eligibility, strategies, outcome and horizon defined before its estimate is calculated. Candidate estimates remain strongly correlated because eligibility populations and follow-up horizons are nested. This isolates post-selection inference from confounding-model failure. Selection algorithms and tie rules are frozen before simulation. The effect-seeking selector is only a positive control and is excluded from the substantive decision rule.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot assess undocumented human searches, candidates outside the recorded library, scientific validity of the selected question, time-varying treatment, grace periods, treatment switching, informative censoring, competing events, irregular observation, measurement error, nuisance-model misspecification, machine-learning estimators or longitudinal TMLE. It estimates marginal coverage for the selected superpopulation estimand, not exact conditional coverage given a particular selection event. Findings for the recorded algorithms cannot establish that every data-informed protocol revision invalidates inference.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Cohort size | n=4000, n=8000 | These represent moderate and large single-database emulations while retaining sparse outcomes in the stricter eligibility groups. The lower level matches the locally benchmarked simulation size. |
| Untreated outcome frequency | lambda0=0.00070 per month, approximately 5% by 60 months, lambda0=0.00320 per month, approximately 20% by 60 months | The levels distinguish a precision-constrained outcome from a common outcome. Outcome prevalence is itself one of the quantities investigators inspect while revising protocols. |
| Recorded protocol search size | K=4, K=16 | Four candidates represent a small revision exercise. Sixteen candidates remain modest relative to the eligibility, comparator, grace-period, washout and outcome choices cataloged in applied guidance. The nested libraries isolate the effect of expanding a known search. |
| Treatment-effect structure | null, homogeneous hazard ratio 0.75, age-modified hazard ratio exp(-0.287682072+0.25Z) | The null detects selection artifacts without a causal effect, the homogeneous level checks a conventional proportional effect, and the modified level allows legitimate protocol-specific estimands. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary randomly selected protocol risk difference**: For selector s and observed selection data D_s, let K_s(D_s)=(c_s,h_s). The estimand is Psi_s=P(T^1<=h_s | age>=c_s)-P(T^0<=h_s | age>=c_s), where T^a is the event time under sustained strategy A=a. It is a marginal superpopulation risk difference. Coverage is marginal over cohort generation and protocol selection. Each replicate is scored against the truth for the protocol actually selected in that replicate, never against the reference protocol. *Truth: For each of the six unique lambda0 by treatment-effect combinations, independently generate 2,000,000 covariate vectors. For every one of the 16 possible protocols and a in {0,1}, calculate p_a,h(X)=1-exp(-lambda0*h*exp(0.35Z+0.25(S-0.5)+0.50(C-0.5)+0.30B+a(theta0+theta1Z))) and average it among age>=c. Use common covariate draws for both strategies and all protocols. Divide the draws into 200 batches and calculate the Monte Carlo SE of every risk difference. Extend in increments of 1,000,000 if any truth MCSE exceeds 0.00005. Truth is calculated only six times because n and K do not change it. The 0.00005 threshold is at most about 1% of the anticipated sampling SE and prevents truth noise from materially changing coverage.*
- **Prespecified candidate risk differences**: For each fixed k=(c,h), Psi_k=P(T^1<=h | age>=c)-P(T^0<=h | age>=c). Candidatewise coverage when k is treated as prespecified is a calibration control for sparse-data and Wald-approximation failures. *Truth: Use the same locked table of Monte Carlo-integrated truths. Report candidatewise results for every candidate and separately for the reference protocol c=55, h=60.*
- **Protocol-induced target movement**: For each replicate, report Psi_Ks-Psi_ref, where Psi_ref is the 60-month risk difference among people aged at least 55. This is target movement, not estimator bias. Estimator bias is separately defined as estimated Psi_Ks minus Psi_Ks. *Truth: Look up both values in the locked candidate-truth table. Do not score the selected interval against Psi_ref.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Prespecified-candidate IPW calibration | Fit e_gamma(X)=P(A=1|X) by logistic regression with predictors Z, S-0.5, C-0.5 and B. For each candidate k and a in {0,1}, estimate mu_a,k as the Hajek mean solving sum I(age>=c)I(A=a)/p_a(X)*(Y_h-mu_a,k)=0, with p_1=e_gamma and p_0=1-e_gamma. Estimate Psi_k=mu_1,k-mu_0,k. Do not truncate weights. | Use a joint empirical M-estimation sandwich. Stack the propensity score U_gamma=x(A-e_gamma) with both weighted-mean equations for every candidate. Let Ahat be the empirical negative derivative matrix and Bhat the empirical outer product of the stacked scores. Use Ahat_inverse Bhat Ahat_inverse_transpose divided by n, then apply the contrast mu_1,k-mu_0,k. This accounts for propensity estimation and supplies all cross-candidate covariances. | no | Failure occurs if the propensity model does not converge, is rank deficient, has nonfinite coefficients or probabilities, a treatment-specific weighted denominator is zero, the sandwich matrix cannot be inverted, or the estimate or SE is nonfinite or the SE is nonpositive. Also record effective sample size below 20 in either arm as an inferential failure. |
| Naive interval after pooled-outcome workability selection | Order candidates by decreasing eligibility threshold and then increasing horizon. Select the first candidate having at least 100 eligible people in each treatment group and at least 70 pooled observed events, where event counts ignore treatment labels. Seventy corresponds to ten events for each of seven prespecified prognostic and treatment terms. If none qualifies, select the candidate with the largest pooled event count; break ties using the same order. Report its fitted-propensity IPW risk difference. | Use only the selected candidate's ordinary M-estimation sandwich SE. The selection rule is omitted from the variance calculation. | yes | Apply the IPW failure rules to the selected candidate. Record separately whether no candidate met the workability thresholds. |
| Naive interval after prognostic-association selection | Before examining outcomes, define the external prognostic score R=0.35Z+0.25(S-0.5)+0.50(C-0.5)+0.30B and standardize it using the full cohort. Among candidates with at least 100 people in each treatment group and variation in Y_h, select the candidate maximizing the squared Pearson correlation between R and Y_h within its eligible population. The calculation pools treatment groups and never uses an effect contrast. Break ties by decreasing eligibility threshold and then increasing horizon. Report the selected fitted-propensity IPW risk difference. | Use the selected candidate's ordinary M-estimation sandwich SE and ignore selection. | yes | Failure occurs under the IPW rules or if no candidate has both outcome variation and the required treatment-group sizes. |
| Naive interval after effect-seeking selection | Calculate every candidate's ordinary IPW estimate and SE, then select the smallest standardized statistic estimate divided by SE because benefit is defined as a negative risk difference. Break exact ties using the fixed candidate order. | Use the selected candidate's ordinary M-estimation sandwich SE and ignore selection. | no | Exclude nonestimable candidates from ranking. Fail the method if all candidates are nonestimable. |
| Outcome-blinded metadata selection with full-sample inference | Using only Z, S, C, B and A, calculate inverse-probability-weighted absolute standardized mean differences for those four covariates within each eligibility population. Select the eligibility threshold minimizing the maximum absolute standardized difference, requiring effective sample size at least 20 in both groups. Set h=60 because outcomes are unavailable to choose a horizon. Break ties by threshold closest to 55, then by the lower threshold. After unmasking outcomes, report the full-sample IPW risk difference. | Use the selected candidate's ordinary M-estimation sandwich SE. Selection is not added to the variance estimator, so whether outcome blinding alone is sufficient is evaluated rather than assumed. | no | Failure occurs if propensity estimation fails, no threshold meets the effective-sample-size requirement, or the selected IPW estimate or SE fails. |
| Recorded-search simultaneous Bonferroni inference | Use the same selected IPW estimate produced by each full-data selector. | Use the same candidate-specific M-estimation sandwich SE. No independence between candidates is assumed. | no | The method fails if any candidate required by the declared simultaneous family is nonestimable or if the selected estimate fails. |
| Fifty-fifty design and analysis split | Randomly assign individuals 1:1 to design and analysis partitions using a replicate-specific seed, independently of all data. Apply each outcome-informed selector only in the design partition. Fit a new correctly specified propensity model in the analysis partition and estimate only the selected protocol's IPW risk difference there. Never reuse design-partition outcomes or estimates. | Use the analysis-partition M-estimation sandwich, including analysis-partition propensity estimation. | no | Failure occurs if selection cannot be completed in the design half or the selected protocol fails the IPW, effective-sample-size or sandwich criteria in the analysis half. |
| Existing multiverse display | Retain and display all K candidate IPW risk differences, their ordering and the reference protocol. Calculate the range, interquartile range and range divided by the median candidate SE. | Use each candidate's ordinary M-estimation sandwich SE. | no | Report the proportion and identity of nonestimable candidates. The display is incomplete if any candidate fails. |

**Performance.** Primary: Marginal coverage of the nominal 95% naive interval for Psi_s under pooled-outcome workability selection, reported by scenario with Monte Carlo SE. Also report its paired coverage loss relative to candidatewise prespecified calibration.. Secondary: Coverage under prognostic-association, effect-seeking and outcome-blinded selection, Coverage of simultaneous Bonferroni and 50:50 split intervals, Bias of estimate minus the truth for the selected protocol, Empirical SE of selected-protocol estimation errors, Mean model SE and relative error in model SE, MSE, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for the selected protocol's true null, Median interval width and width ratio relative to naive full-data inference, Overall and selector-specific convergence, Selection frequency for every protocol, Frequency with which the selected estimand differs from the reference estimand by more than 0.01 risk-difference units, Selection agreement between full-data and split-sample selectors, Propensity-weight effective sample size and maximum weight, Multiverse range, standardized range and AUC for predicting naive-interval noncoverage. 2000 replicates. For coverage near 0.95, MCSE=sqrt(0.95*0.05/R). Requiring MCSE no greater than 0.005 gives R at least 0.95*0.05/0.005^2=1900. Round upward to 2000 replicates per scenario, giving MCSE 0.00487 at 95% coverage and 0.00671 at 90% coverage. Use paired replicate-level indicators and their empirical covariance for MCSEs of coverage differences.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require calibration: every candidate selected in at least 5% of replicates must have prespecified candidatewise coverage between 0.93 and 0.97, truth MCSE at most 0.00005, and method nonconvergence below 5%. The problem is materially real for the studied effect-blind searches if at least four of the 48 scenario-selector cells formed by 24 scenarios and the two outcome-informed effect-blind selectors have naive selected coverage at most 0.90 and a coverage loss of at least 0.05 relative to calibrated candidatewise inference, with the 95% Monte Carlo lower limit for the loss above 0.02. These cells must include a null-effect scenario and both K=4 and K=16. In the same cells, at least one correction must attain coverage of at least 0.93 with a 95% Monte Carlo lower limit above 0.90.
- Problem is not real if: After passing calibration, every one of the 48 effect-blind scenario-selector cells must have naive selected coverage at least 0.93, its 95% Monte Carlo lower limit above 0.90, and absolute coverage loss below 0.02 with the 95% Monte Carlo upper limit for loss below 0.03. This conclusion is restricted to the two declared algorithms and libraries of at most 16 protocols.
- Uninformative if: Classify the result as mixed or uninformative if neither numerical rule is met, if a candidate selected at least 5% of the time has prespecified coverage outside 0.93 to 0.97, if nonconvergence exceeds 5%, if truth MCSE remains above 0.00005 after 10,000,000 integration draws, or if both corrections fail in the same cells as naive inference. Effect-seeking undercoverage alone cannot establish that effect-blind protocol development is problematic.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A simulation can manufacture post-selection failure by defining a selector that explicitly chooses the most favorable treatment estimate. *Mitigation: Treat effect-seeking selection only as a positive control. Base the decision on selectors using pooled outcome counts or covariate-outcome associations without treatment-effect contrasts. Include null, homogeneous and heterogeneous effects, require failures across multiple cells, and verify candidatewise fixed-protocol calibration.*
- Scoring a selected estimate against the original reference estimand would falsely label legitimate target changes as estimator bias. *Mitigation: Score every interval against Psi_K for the protocol selected in that replicate. Report Psi_K-Psi_ref separately as target movement.*
- Sparse outcomes or unstable weights can cause ordinary Wald undercoverage even without selection. *Mitigation: Report every candidate's prespecified coverage, effective sample size and maximum weight. Treat cells failing the calibration or convergence thresholds as uninformative rather than evidence of selection failure.*
- The correctly specified treatment model and absence of censoring make the data cleaner than applied emulations. *Mitigation: State this as deliberate isolation of the selection mechanism. Do not generalize the numerical results to misspecified nuisance models, informative censoring or time-varying strategies.*
- Outcome-blinded selection based on treatment and covariates may still target a protocol associated with an unusual empirical covariate distribution. *Mitigation: Evaluate outcome-blinded coverage rather than declaring it valid by construction. Keep the superpopulation selected estimand explicit and report selected-threshold frequencies.*
- Bonferroni inference may appear successful only because it is excessively wide. *Mitigation: Report interval-width ratios, rejection rates and candidate correlations alongside coverage. Define restoration as coverage at least 0.93, but do not call a method preferable without its precision cost.*
- A known algorithmic search is much simpler than iterative human protocol development. *Mitigation: Freeze and publish the candidate library, selectors, tie rules and decision log. Limit the conclusion to recorded searches. State that no correction can cover undocumented candidates without defining a larger family.*
- Monte Carlo error in candidate truths can be mistaken for estimator bias and can distort coverage. *Mitigation: Use common-random-number integration of conditional risks, batch MCSEs, a 0.00005 stopping threshold and locked truth files created before the simulation replicates.*
- The nested candidate library may understate multiplicity relative to weakly correlated protocol choices. *Mitigation: Report the empirical candidate-estimate correlation matrix and effective library size. Interpret K=4 and K=16 as realistic nested searches, not worst-case bounds for arbitrary protocols.*
- A single 50:50 split can choose an unstable protocol and sacrifices half the analysis information. *Mitigation: Report selection agreement, nonconvergence and interval-width inflation. Judge sample splitting on both coverage and precision rather than coverage alone.*

**Runtime as estimated.** Run 24 scenarios by 2000 replicates, for 48,000 replicates total, through studies/_shared/R/harness.R on 6 workers. Each replicate needs one full-data propensity glm, one design-partition glm and one analysis-partition glm; all candidate IPW estimates, selectors and sandwich matrices are algebraic. This is 144,000 small logistic fits, zero ltmle fits and zero SuperLearner fits. The six unique 2,000,000-person truth integrations should cost about 24 minutes using the supplied benchmark. A 100-replicate corner pilot should verify timing and memory. The expected main run is approximately 3 to 6 hours and should finish overnight. lmtp, concrete, CICI, Stan and cmdstanr are unnecessary.

**Also bears on.** PRO-02 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019; Hansford HJ, et al. Design and implementation of observational studies emulating a target trial. JAMA Network Open. 2026; Hansford HJ, Cashin AG, Jones MD, et al. Transparent reporting of observational studies emulating a target trial: the TARGET statement. JAMA. 2025; Uncertainty in the estimated effects of statin initiation on risk of dementia: using a multiverse analysis. European Journal of Epidemiology. 2025; Berk R, Brown L, Buja A, Zhang K, Zhao L. Valid post-selection inference. Annals of Statistics. 2013; Hernan MA, Robins JM. Using Big Data to emulate a target trial when a randomized trial is not available. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2016; Peduzzi P, Concato J, Kemper E, Holford TR, Feinstein AR. A simulation study of the number of events per variable in logistic regression analysis. Journal of Clinical Epidemiology. 1996
