## 7. SEQ-01: When Does Common Effect Pooling Conceal Calendar Time Heterogeneity in Sequential Target Trial Emulation?

*Catalog entry: Sequential emulation creates repeated participation and calendar-time confounding that its efficiency gain conceals*

**Question.** Across 24 prespecified scenarios, by how many percentage points does the estimated 12-month risk difference change when a common trial-start treatment effect is replaced by a flexible treatment-by-start-time effect, how often does the common-effect interval cover an explicitly defined equal-start estimand, does a person-clustered Wald diagnostic identify a true trial-specific risk-difference range of at least 0.02, and what empirical precision remains relative to a single middle-start trial?

**Aims.** Estimate, for every scenario, the bias and 95% coverage of the equal-start average risk difference under package-like common-effect pooling, common-effect pooling with flexible nuisance adjustment, spline effect-modified pooling, and start-saturated estimation. Quantify the mean absolute movement between common-effect and effect-modified estimates. Estimate the sensitivity and specificity of a 0.05-level person-clustered Wald test for detecting a true trial-specific risk-difference range of at least 0.02. Estimate the empirical variance ratio for the month-12 risk difference from spline pooling versus a standalone month-12 emulation. Separate calendar-time confounding, calendar-time effect modification, and changes in the later eligible population through a fully crossed design.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 2000 independent people observed on fixed monthly cycles t = 0,...,33, with failure assessed through month 34. Age is 40 + 50B years, where B follows Beta(3,3), and R = (Age - 65)/10. Sex follows Bernoulli(0.5). Baseline comorbidity C follows Bernoulli(expit(-0.8 + 0.45R + 0.35Sex)). Baseline severity L_0 follows Normal(0.35R + 0.50C + 0.20Sex, 1). For an event-free person, L_(t+1) = 0.65L_t + 0.20R + 0.30C + 0.10Sex + 0.10q_t - 0.30Z_t + epsilon_(t+1), where epsilon follows Normal(0, 0.70^2), Z_t is current treatment, and q_t = max(-1, min(1, (t - 11)/11)). All innovations are mutually independent conditional on recorded history. Trial starts are K = {0,2,4,...,22}. A person is eligible at k if event-free and untreated immediately before treatment assignment at k. Each trial has 12 monthly outcome intervals. Thus one person can enter as many as 12 trials. There is no nonadministrative loss to follow-up.
- Outcome model: Among people event-free at the start of month t, treatment is assigned first and failure then follows Bernoulli probability expit(-4.50 + s(0.15Sex + 0.55R + 0.55C + 0.45L_t) + bq_t + Z_t[log(0.65) + log(0.85/0.65)C + h(S)]), where S is the first treatment month. Untreated people have Z_t = 0. The event is a composite failure endpoint, so competing events are absent. The coefficient scale is the conditional log odds of the discrete monthly failure hazard. The intercept gives approximately 15 to 20 percent 12-month risk around the central calendar period for common covariate profiles, with scenario-specific risks established by truth enumeration rather than assumed from the intercept.
- Treatment effect: Treatment initiation I_t is available only while untreated. Conditional on remaining untreated and event-free, I_t follows Bernoulli(expit(-3.60 + s(0.20Sex + 0.30R + 0.45C + 0.55L_t) + aq_t)). Treatment is absorbing: Z_t = 1 from the first initiation month onward. For a trial starting at k, strategy 1 forces initiation at k and continued treatment through k + 11. Strategy 0 forces no initiation from k through k + 11. The total causal effect includes both the direct hazard coefficient and the subsequent treatment-induced reduction of L. In the observational emulation, people assigned to no initiation at k are artificially censored immediately before any later initiation.
- Effect modification: On the monthly conditional hazard odds-ratio scale, the direct treatment effect is 0.65 among people without baseline comorbidity and 0.85 among people with comorbidity when h(S) = 0. Calendar-time effect modification h(S) is varied independently. Because treatment changes later L, marginal 12-month effects need not equal these direct conditional odds ratios. Risk-selective survival and treatment initiation can also change the comorbidity and severity distribution among later eligible people, creating trial-specific marginal risk differences even when h(S) = 0.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 by 2 by 2 by 2 equals 24 scenarios. Sequential eligibility, calendar-time confounding, effect modification, and risk-selective depletion are switched independently. All treatment and outcome predictors are measured, consistency holds, treatment probabilities remain away from deterministic assignment for practically occupied histories, and the fitted treatment model contains the true assignment form. These choices isolate pooling and interpretation from unmeasured confounding. The nonselective and no-heterogeneity levels ensure that a method must remain calibrated when the studied complications are absent.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot detect bias from unmeasured confounding, an incorrect eligibility date, irregular observation, informative visit timing, exposure misclassification, nonadherence after initiation, treatment discontinuation, competing risks, nonadministrative dropout, interference, or structural positivity failure. Fixed bimonthly trial starts say nothing about daily or irregular decision points. The absorbing-treatment protocol does not generalize automatically to sustained strategies with switching. Correctly specified initiation models mean the study cannot establish robustness to treatment-model misspecification.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Calendar pattern of the conditional treatment effect | None: h(S) = 0, Monotone: h(S) = 0.30q_S, Transient loss of benefit: h(S) = 0.30cos(pi q_S) | The null level measures false detection. The monotone pattern represents progressive changes in effectiveness. The cosine pattern represents a temporary era-specific loss of effectiveness, such as a transient variant, co-intervention, or guideline period. A magnitude of 0.30 changes the conditional treatment odds ratio by a multiplier between exp(-0.30) = 0.74 and exp(0.30) = 1.35. This is a calibrated stress test, not a claimed estimate from an applied study. Both nonnull shapes are required so that a method is not evaluated only under the exact shape it fits. |
| Calendar trend in treatment availability | None: a = 0, Strong adoption: a = 0.80 | The strong level produces a first-to-last trial-start initiation odds ratio of exp(1.60) = 4.95, approximating a major formulary, guideline, or availability change. At central covariate values, monthly initiation remains in the low single digits and rises into the range where later trials retain usable treated groups. Low treatment prevalence is a motivating finite-sample setting in the published sequential-emulation inference work. |
| Calendar trend in untreated background risk | None: b = 0, Strong secular change: b = 0.35 | The strong level produces a first-to-last trial-start hazard-odds ratio of exp(0.70) = 2.01 before covariate and treatment effects. This represents a substantial change in circulating disease, supportive care, coding, or background prevention. Calendar time is a pooled confounder only in combinations where it predicts both treatment and outcome, so the treatment and outcome trends are varied separately. |
| Risk-selective later eligibility | Nonselective negative control: s = 0, Risk-selective eligibility: s = 1 | At s = 0, pretreatment covariates predict neither initiation nor untreated failure, so later eligibility does not preferentially deplete measured risk groups. At s = 1, a 10-year age increase has an untreated failure hazard-odds ratio of exp(0.55) = 1.73, comorbidity has odds ratio 1.73, and a one-unit severity increase has odds ratio 1.57. The exact negative control is necessary because a DGM containing selective survival in every scenario could only show that the mechanism it imposed exists. |

**Estimands.**

- **Equal-start average 12-month risk difference**: For each k in {0,2,...,22}, let E_k denote eligibility under the natural data-generating process immediately before assignment at k. Define RD_k = P(Y by k + 12 under initiation at k and continued treatment through k + 11 | E_k) minus P(Y by k + 12 under no initiation through k + 11 | E_k). Negative values favor initiation. The primary estimand is theta_equal = sum_k RD_k/12. It is marginal, gives every prespecified trial start equal policy weight, and targets the superpopulation rather than the realized replicate. *Truth: For each scenario, simulate an independent truth population of 2000000 people in chunks of 100000. Follow natural histories to identify E_k. At every eligible start, simulate both strategies for 12 months using common covariate and innovation draws across the paired strategies. Compute every RD_k and their equal average. With at least 40 percent late eligibility, the worst-case binomial Monte Carlo SE for one arm risk is below 0.00056, and the paired risk-difference SE is below approximately 0.0008. This is small relative to the 0.005 and 0.010 decision thresholds.*
- **Trial-specific 12-month risk-difference curve**: The vector of RD_k for all 12 starts, each marginal over that start's eligible superpopulation. The prespecified heterogeneity magnitude is Delta_RD = max_k RD_k minus min_k RD_k. Delta_RD of at least 0.02 is defined as materially heterogeneous. *Truth: Use the same 2000000-person paired truth simulation. Store arm-specific risks, RD_k, eligibility probability, and Monte Carlo SE for every start rather than retaining only the pooled average.*
- **Natural person-trial mixture risk difference**: theta_PT = sum_k P(E_k)RD_k divided by sum_k P(E_k). This marginal superpopulation estimand gives more weight to starts contributing more eligible person-trials and approximates the implicit target of an unqualified pooled expanded-data analysis. *Truth: Estimate P(E_k) and RD_k jointly in the truth population. Report theta_PT beside theta_equal. Their difference quantifies the consequence of leaving the trial-start weighting rule implicit.*
- **Middle-start effect and pooling efficiency**: RD_12 is the marginal 12-month initiation-versus-no-initiation risk difference among the month-12 eligible superpopulation. The efficiency contrast is the empirical variance of the spline-pooled estimator of RD_12 divided by the empirical variance of the standalone month-12 estimator. *Truth: Use the month-12 eligible subset of the truth population and the same paired intervention simulation. A variance ratio below 1 indicates retained precision from borrowing across starts.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Package-like common-effect sequential emulation | Create one person-trial for every eligible k. Set baseline assignment G to initiation at k versus no initiation at k. Artificially censor G = 0 observations immediately before later initiation. Estimate denominator initiation probabilities with pooled logistic regression I_t on 1 + q_t + Sex + R + C + L_t among untreated event-free person-months. Estimate numerator probabilities with I_t on 1 + q_t. Form stabilized baseline assignment weights and, for controls, multiply the corresponding stabilized probabilities of remaining untreated through each risk interval. Do not truncate weights. Fit the weighted pooled-logistic MSM event on G + j + j^2 + G:j + G:j^2 + q_k + q_k^2. Predict 12-month risks under both assignments at each start and average the 12 risk differences equally. | Use the one-way person-clustered sandwich covariance for the weighted MSM, summing all score rows from every trial and follow-up interval within original person. With bread B = sum w p(1-p)xx transpose and person score U_i = sum within i of wx(y-p), use V = B inverse times sum_i U_i U_i transpose times B inverse, multiplied by n_id/(n_id-1). Estimated treatment weights are treated as fixed, matching the installed package's standard robust outcome-model calculation. Obtain the risk-difference variance by the analytic delta method through the product-survival predictions and equal-start average. | yes | Failure of either weight or outcome glm to report convergence; rank deficiency affecting a required contrast; any nonfinite coefficient, estimate, or SE; any absolute required coefficient above 20; any fitted initiation probability below 0.000001 or above 0.999999; or failure to obtain both treatment arms at a required start. No fallback estimator is substituted. |
| Rich-nuisance common-effect sequential emulation | Use the same expanded data and estimated stabilized weights. Fit event on factor(j) interacted with splines::ns(q_k, df = 4), plus G interacted with factor(j), but include no G-by-q_k term. Predict each start-specific risk under G = 1 and G = 0 and average equally. This estimator has the same common-start treatment-effect restriction as the status quo but the same baseline-risk flexibility as the proposed estimator. | Use the same person-clustered sandwich, fixed-weight convention, finite-cluster correction, and analytic delta method as the package-like estimator. | no | The common weight-model criteria apply. The method also fails if its outcome glm does not converge, a required model-matrix column is rank deficient, a required coefficient is nonfinite or exceeds 20 in absolute value, or the standardized contrast or SE is nonfinite. |
| Spline effect-modified sequential emulation | Use the same weights and rich nuisance model, then add G interacted with splines::ns(q_k, df = 4). Predict RD_k for every start, theta_equal, theta_PT, and RD_12. The four interaction coefficients are tested jointly with a person-clustered Wald statistic against a chi-squared distribution with 4 degrees of freedom. The diagnostic is positive at p below 0.05. | Use the same person-clustered sandwich and analytic delta method. Use the full covariance among all spline, follow-up, and interaction coefficients for trial-specific and averaged contrasts. | no | The shared weight-model criteria apply. The method fails if the outcome glm or robust covariance fails, any required interaction is nonestimable or exceeds 20 in absolute value, the covariance block needed for the 4-degree-of-freedom test is singular, or any required standardized estimate or SE is nonfinite. |
| Start-saturated weighted life-table benchmark | Within every start, assignment arm, and follow-up month, estimate the weighted discrete hazard as sum(wY) divided by sum(w at risk). Form arm risk as 1 minus the product of one minus these 12 hazards. Compute all RD_k and average them equally. This imposes no smoothing or common treatment effect across starts. | Treat weights as fixed. For each start-arm-month cell c, use person contribution phi_ic = sum within person and cell of w(Y-p_c) divided by the total cell weight. Stack all cell contributions by original person, sum their outer products with correction n_id/(n_id-1), and apply the delta method through product survival, arm contrasts, and equal-start averaging. This retains covariance induced by the same person entering several trials. | no | Undefined cell weight, an empty arm or risk set, a nonfinite hazard or variance, or effective sample size below 10 in any cell required for a reported contrast. Effective sample size is square of sum(w) divided by sum(w^2). |
| Standalone month-12 emulation | Restrict the start-saturated weighted life-table estimator to people eligible at k = 12 and estimate RD_12 without borrowing from any other trial start. | Use the person-level influence-function covariance for the weighted month-12 life table, with the same fixed-weight convention and finite-cluster correction. | no | Undefined weights or hazards, an empty assignment arm or follow-up risk set, a nonfinite estimate or SE, or effective sample size below 10 in any required cell. |

**Performance.** Primary: Scenario-specific 95 percent interval coverage of theta_equal for the rich-nuisance common-effect and spline effect-modified estimators. Bias-eliminated coverage is interpreted jointly with ordinary coverage so that bias from common-effect pooling is separated from SE calibration already studied in the published variance literature.. Secondary: Bias with Monte Carlo SE, Empirical SE, Mean model SE, Relative error in model SE, Mean squared error, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate, Convergence rate, Mean and 95th percentile absolute movement from common-effect to effect-modified theta_equal, Trial-specific curve root mean squared error and maximum absolute error, Sensitivity of the 0.05 Wald diagnostic when true Delta_RD is at least 0.02, Specificity of the Wald diagnostic when true Delta_RD is below 0.005, Probability that the Wald test does not reject while the common-effect interval width is smaller than true Delta_RD, Empirical variance and MSE ratios for spline-pooled versus standalone RD_12, Difference between theta_equal and theta_PT, Maximum weight and effective sample size by trial start and assignment arm. 2000 replicates. Coverage is a binomial proportion. At nominal coverage 0.95, requiring Monte Carlo SE no greater than 0.005 gives R at least 0.95 times 0.05 divided by 0.005 squared, which equals 1900. Round upward to 2000 replicates per scenario. The resulting MCSE is sqrt(0.95 times 0.05 divided by 2000) = 0.00487, or 0.49 percentage points. At coverage 0.90, the MCSE is 0.00671. All decision comparisons use the metric estimate plus or minus 1.96 Monte Carlo SE.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require at least 8 of the 16 scenarios with nonzero h to have true Delta_RD of at least 0.02. The pooling problem is demonstrated if, in at least half of all scenarios with Delta_RD at least 0.02, either the rich-nuisance common-effect absolute-bias interval lies wholly above 0.010 or its coverage interval lies wholly below 0.90, while the spline or start-saturated absolute-bias interval lies wholly below 0.005. In the four negative-control scenarios with h = 0 and s = 0, the rich common-effect absolute-bias interval must lie below 0.005. Independently, the diagnostic is declared adequate if its lower 95 percent Monte Carlo bound is at least 0.80 for sensitivity and at least 0.90 for specificity. Repeated-participation efficiency is declared to survive if the upper Monte Carlo bound for the spline-pooled to standalone RD_12 empirical variance ratio is below 1 in at least 75 percent of scenarios with Delta_RD below 0.005.
- Problem is not real if: At least 8 scenarios must have true Delta_RD of at least 0.02, and in at least 90 percent of them the rich common-effect absolute-bias upper bound must be below 0.005, its coverage lower bound must be at least 0.93, its MSE must be no more than 10 percent above spline pooling, and the probability of a nonrejecting Wald test combined with a common-effect interval narrower than Delta_RD must be below 0.05. These criteria would show that common-effect pooling remains numerically adequate for the declared equal-start estimand within this DGM class despite visible trial-specific variation.
- Uninformative if: The result is uninformative if fewer than 8 nonnull scenarios achieve Delta_RD of 0.02, either proposed estimator has absolute bias above 0.005 in the negative controls, convergence is below 0.95 in more than four scenarios, effective sample size falls below 10 in more than 10 percent of replicates for any required cell, both common-effect and effect-modified methods fail in the same direction, or the 95 percent Monte Carlo intervals cross the numerical decision thresholds. No conclusion may be based only on a significant heterogeneity test or only on raw interval coverage.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM could manufacture the calendar-time effect that the study is intended to find. *Mitigation: Calendar effect modification, treatment adoption, background risk, and selective eligibility are crossed independently. Exact null levels are included. The decision rule requires calibration in negative controls, requires at least eight genuinely material alternatives, and attributes failure to common-effect pooling only when spline or saturated estimation removes it. The nonmonotone alternative prevents evaluation solely under the fitted monotone shape.*
- A correctly specified initiation model favors inverse-probability weighting and may conceal behavior under routine model misspecification. *Mitigation: The treatment model is intentionally correct so that pooling can be isolated. Conclusions will explicitly be limited to measured confounding with adequate treatment-model specification. No claim about robustness to omitted nonlinearities, interactions, or unmeasured confounding is permitted.*
- Failure of the package-like estimator could arise from its quadratic nuisance model rather than its common-effect restriction. *Mitigation: The rich-nuisance common-effect estimator uses exactly the nuisance basis used by the spline effect-modified estimator. The package-like versus rich-common comparison measures nuisance-model failure; the rich-common versus spline comparison measures the start-time treatment-effect constraint.*
- The simple person-clustered sandwich treats estimated treatment weights as fixed, and published work shows that alternative inference procedures can behave differently in sparse settings. *Mitigation: The study accepts the published variance comparison as resolved and does not present itself as a new variance-method study. Conclusions about pooling require concordant point bias, empirical SE, MSE, and bias-eliminated coverage. The naive independent-row variance is not used. Extreme weights and effective sample sizes are reported so sparse scenarios cannot be mistaken for evidence about calendar pooling.*
- Marginal risk differences can vary across trial starts even with a constant conditional treatment coefficient because baseline risk and eligible-population composition change. *Mitigation: This is measured rather than labeled automatically as bias. The protocol reports every RD_k, theta_equal, theta_PT, and their differences. The s = 0 negative control distinguishes calendar effect modification from risk-selective changes in the eligible population.*
- A conclusion on the conditional hazard odds-ratio scale could disagree with a policy conclusion on the risk-difference scale. *Mitigation: All decision rules use marginal 12-month risks and risk differences computed by intervention. Conditional odds ratios are used only to define the DGM.*
- Fixed monthly measurement and bimonthly trial starts simplify the observation and eligibility processes. *Mitigation: The limitation is built into the estimand and stated explicitly. No conclusion will be extended to irregular EHR visits, daily treatment decisions, or informative observation without another study.*
- Absorbing treatment, absence of competing events, and absence of external dropout may overstate usable information. *Mitigation: These mechanisms are excluded to isolate sequential pooling. The catalog attachment will label the result as applying to absorbing initiation and a composite failure endpoint, not to discontinuation, switching, competing risks, or informative censoring.*
- Monte Carlo error in the enumerated truth could be mistaken for estimator bias. *Mitigation: Truth uses 2000000 people with paired common random numbers, is generated independently of evaluation replicates, and carries an MCSE for every arm risk and contrast. A truth estimate with MCSE above 0.001 is extended in 100000-person chunks before analysis.*
- A single sample size and a finite set of effect patterns cannot establish universal performance. *Mitigation: The conclusion is restricted to moderate cohorts with low monthly initiation, 12 repeated starts, and the stated effect magnitudes. The full scenario results and code remain public so later studies can add sample-size, discontinuation, and observation-process factors without changing this protocol's decision rule.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The full experiment has 24 scenarios times 2000 replicates, totaling 48000 replicates. Each replicate fits two low-dimensional initiation-weight glms and three weighted outcome glms, totaling 240000 ordinary glm fits. The package-like, matched common-effect, and spline fits use at most 2000 times 12 times 12 = 288000 expanded risk rows before failures and censoring reduce the count. The start-saturated and standalone estimators use closed-form grouped calculations. There are no ltmle or SuperLearner fits. At five seconds for an entire replicate, 48000 replicates require about 11.1 hours on 6 workers. Allowing truth enumeration at approximately 4 minutes per scenario, serialization, and summaries gives an expected total of 13 to 16 hours. The existing seeded resumable harness permits interruption without lost completed replicates. No bootstrap, jackknife, or new variance-method comparison is included because that part of SEQ-01 has already been answered.

**Also bears on.** SEQ-01 (answers)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019; Limozin L, Seaman SR, Su L. Inference procedures in sequential trial emulation with survival outcomes: comparing confidence intervals based on the sandwich variance estimator, bootstrap and jackknife. Statistical Methods in Medical Research. 2025; DeMonte, Shook-Sa and Hudgens. Assessing COVID-19 vaccine effectiveness in observational studies via nested trial emulation. 2024; Keogh RH, et al. Causal inference in survival analysis using longitudinal observational data: sequential trials and marginal structural models. Statistics in Medicine. 2023; Caniglia EC, et al. Emulating target trials to avoid immortal time bias: an application to antibiotic initiation. Epidemiology. 2023; Su L, et al. TrialEmulation: an R package to emulate target trials for causal analysis of observational time-to-event data. 2024

## 8. GMT-04: A common-estimand benchmark of IPTW, the parametric g-formula, and longitudinal TMLE under treatment-confounder feedback, model misspecification, overlap loss, and hidden confounding

*Catalog entry: No estimator is defensible as a default, and no study is required to show that its choice mattered*

**Question.** Across 24 longitudinal target trial scenarios, in what proportion of repeated samples does choosing IPTW, the parametric g-formula, or longitudinal TMLE change the estimated 36-month marginal risk difference by more than 0.03, change its sign, cross a benefit threshold of -0.03, or determine whether an estimate is returned at all? Which family has the lowest MSE in each scenario, and how often do prespecified diagnostics detect absolute error greater than 0.03?

**Aims.** Estimate scenario-specific probabilities of consequential estimator-choice disagreement; compare bias, MSE, interval coverage, and convergence for three estimator families targeting exactly the same sustained strategies; determine whether the best-performing family changes between treatment-model and outcome-model stress; compare fixed-weight and nuisance-adjusted IPTW variance estimators; and quantify apparently reassuring agreement when all methods share an identification failure caused by an unmeasured confounder.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate 4000 independent eligible individuals, all event-free at month 0. Decisions occur at k=0,...,5, corresponding to months 0, 6, 12, 18, 24, and 30; follow-up ends at month 36. Let expit(x)=1/{1+exp(-x)}. Generate independent W1~Bernoulli(0.50), W2~Bernoulli(0.35), and U~Bernoulli(0.30). Generate L0~Bernoulli(expit(-0.70+1.00W1+0.70W2+0.50W1W2+0.80U)). Set L[-1]=A[-1]=A[-2]=0. At each event-free visit, generate A_k~Bernoulli(expit(-0.20+s{1.20L_k+0.50W1-0.50W2+0.70U+1.10A_[k-1]-0.70}+delta_g{1.00L_[k-1](W2-0.35)-0.80A_[k-2](W1-0.50)})). Generate monotone loss during interval k with probability expit(-4.00+0.70L_k+0.35W2+0.30A_k+0.25U+0.12k+delta_g{0.80L_[k-1](A_[k-1]-0.50)-0.50A_[k-2](W1-0.50)}). Full trajectories are generated first; loss then masks the current outcome and every subsequent node. Among event-free individuals and for k<5, generate L_[k+1]~Bernoulli(expit(-0.80+1.35L_k+0.45W1+0.35W2+0.55U-0.75A_k+0.15k+delta_Q{0.65L_[k-1](W2-0.35)-0.45L_[k-1](L_k-0.50)})). Conditional draws are independent given the stated history.
- Outcome model: Let E_[k+1] indicate a first event during interval k. Among individuals event-free at its start, P(E_[k+1]=1)=expit(-4.60+0.20k+1.00L_k+0.55W1+0.35W2+0.75U-0.45A_k-0.30A_kW1+delta_Q{0.70L_[k-1](W2-0.35)-0.50L_[k-1](L_k-0.50)}). An event is absorbing. The terminal outcome is Y=1 if any E_[k+1]=1 by month 36. The centered omitted terms alter conditional structure without uniformly increasing treatment prevalence or event risk.
- Treatment effect: Treatment has an interval-specific direct log odds coefficient of -0.45 for W1=0 and also lowers the odds of the next adverse covariate state through a coefficient of -0.75. These correspond to conditional odds ratios of 0.64 and 0.47, respectively. The total sustained-strategy effect is therefore history-dependent and is computed marginally rather than interpreted from either coefficient.
- Effect modification: The A_k by W1 coefficient is -0.30. The direct conditional treatment odds ratio is exp(-0.45)=0.64 when W1=0 and exp(-0.75)=0.47 when W1=1. U affects treatment, L, censoring, and outcome but does not modify the treatment coefficient.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 overlap levels by 4 nuisance structures by 2 identification states. In observed-U scenarios, consistency, sequential exchangeability, positivity, correct temporal ordering, and conditional independent censoring hold. Treatment-confounder feedback is real because A_k changes L_[k+1], which changes later treatment and event risk. In hidden-U scenarios, sequential exchangeability is deliberately false. All probabilities remain strictly between zero and one.
- Cannot detect: The study says nothing about irregular visits, continuous treatment, grace periods, treatment versions, interference, measurement error, competing events, intermittent covariate missingness, more than six decisions, matching, structural nested models, highly adaptive machine learning, or violations other than the specified hidden U. The hidden-U scenarios can measure silent failure against known truth, but no observed-data diagnostic can generally establish that U exists.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Practical overlap scale s | 0.60: mild; under delta_g=0 the possible one-visit treatment probabilities are approximately 0.29 to 0.82, 1.00: moderate; under delta_g=0 the possible one-visit treatment probabilities are approximately 0.20 to 0.93, 1.70: severe cumulative practical positivity stress; under delta_g=0 the possible one-visit probabilities are approximately 0.10 to 0.99 | The levels preserve structural positivity while creating increasingly rare six-visit always-treated and never-treated histories. This targets the cumulative overlap problem emphasized for longitudinal weights rather than inserting impossible treatment assignments. |
| Nuisance-model structure | G0Q0: delta_g=0 and delta_Q=0; the prespecified treatment, censoring, transition, event, and sequential outcome models contain the true conditional functions, G1Q0: delta_g=1 and delta_Q=0; treatment and censoring models omit two-lag centered interactions, while outcome-side models remain correct, G0Q1: delta_g=0 and delta_Q=1; transition, event, and sequential outcome models omit lagged centered interactions, while treatment and censoring models remain correct, G1Q1: delta_g=1 and delta_Q=1; both nuisance sides omit the stated terms | The four quadrants create a benign reference, one scenario favorable to outcome modeling, one favorable to weighting, and one in which double robustness supplies no guarantee. The centered coefficients have moderate conditional effects and avoid making every misspecified scenario a simple prevalence shift. |
| Identification status of U | Observed U: U is supplied to every estimator and included in every nuisance model, Hidden U: the same generated U is removed before analysis | U has prevalence 0.30, a treatment coefficient of 0.70 before overlap scaling, and an event coefficient of 0.75. Hiding it creates a known non-identifiable contrast and tests whether agreement or narrow intervals are mistakenly treated as evidence of validity. Paired observed-U and hidden-U analyses use the same generated data. |

**Estimands.**

- **36-month marginal sustained-strategy risk difference**: RD36=Pr(Y under A_0=...=A_5=1 and no loss by month 36)-Pr(Y under A_0=...=A_5=0 and no loss by month 36). All simulated individuals are eligible at month 0, there is no grace period, treatment is assigned at six fixed visits, follow-up begins immediately, and the first event by month 36 is the outcome. The estimand is a superpopulation marginal risk difference; negative values favor sustained treatment. *Truth: Compute each regime risk exactly by forward state recursion over the eight W1,W2,U strata, L0, the required lagged L states, and event-free status. At each visit set A_k to the regime value, set loss to zero, add event mass using the stated hazard, and propagate surviving mass through the L transition. Double-precision state probabilities must sum to one within 1e-12. The truth differs with delta_Q but not with s, delta_g, or whether U is hidden. There is no Monte Carlo error in the primary truth. As an implementation check, compare the recursion with a seeded 5,000,000-person intervention simulation for one delta_Q=0 and one delta_Q=1 case; require agreement within three simulation MCSEs, expected to be below 0.0003 for each risk difference.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IPTW clone-censor pooled-logistic MSM with fixed-weight sandwich | Clone each individual to the always-treated and never-treated protocols and artificially censor a clone at its first treatment deviation. Fit pooled logistic models A_k~factor(k)+W1+W2+U+L_k+A_[k-1] and retention~factor(k)+W1+W2+U+L_k+A_k, omitting U in hidden-U scenarios. Replace fitted probabilities below 0.01 or above 0.99 by those bounds. Form cumulative inverse probabilities of observed adherence and retention. Fit a weighted pooled logistic event model saturated in regime by interval; convert its six hazards to each 36-month risk and subtract. Scaling weights to mean one within regime and interval is permitted because it leaves the saturated hazard estimates unchanged. | Use the individual-cluster empirical sandwich from the weighted event model while treating fitted and clipped weights as fixed: V=A_inverse times the sum of subject-level score outer products times A_inverse-transpose. Use the joint delta-method covariance of the two product-limit risks to obtain the RD variance. | yes | Failure of any nuisance or event glm to report convergence; rank deficiency affecting a required coefficient; absolute fitted coefficient above 20; any nonfinite probability, weight, estimate, gradient, or SE; sandwich condition number above 1e12; no weighted event or survivor information in a regime-interval cell; or RD outside [-1,1]. Probability clipping alone is a diagnostic flag, not nonconvergence. |
| IPTW clone-censor pooled-logistic MSM with nuisance-adjusted sandwich | Use exactly the same weights, weighted saturated hazard model, regime risks, and RD as the fixed-weight IPTW method. | Stack the treatment-model scores, retention-model scores, weight-normalization equations, and weighted event-model scores at the individual level. Estimate A as the derivative of the stacked mean score and B as the empirical covariance of individual stacked scores; use A_inverse B A_inverse-transpose divided by n. Apply the joint delta method to the two risks and their difference. | no | Use the IPTW point-estimator rules, plus singular stacked derivative matrix, stacked-matrix condition number above 1e12, or a negative RD variance below numerical tolerance of -1e-10. |
| Parametric longitudinal g-formula | Fit pooled logistic transition models L_[k+1]~factor(k)+W1+W2+U+L_k+A_k and event models E_[k+1]~factor(k)+W1+W2+U+L_k+A_k+A_k:W1 among the relevant observed risk sets, omitting U in hidden-U scenarios. Standardize over the empirical baseline distribution and calculate both sustained-regime risks by exact state recursion, not by simulated covariate trajectories. Subtract the risks. | Use a subject-cluster stacked M-estimation sandwich for all transition and event regression coefficients. Add the empirical-baseline contribution m_a(W1,W2,U,L0)-psi_a to each regime influence function. Obtain derivatives of the exact recursion with central differences using step 1e-5 times max(1, absolute coefficient). Form the RD influence function by subtraction. | no | Any component glm failure, required rank deficiency, absolute coefficient above 20, nonfinite transition or event probability, recursion mass error above 1e-10, nonfinite gradient or SE, sandwich condition number above 1e12, or a risk outside [0,1]. |
| Longitudinal TMLE | Run ltmle::ltmle separately for the two static treatment vectors using survivalOutcome=TRUE. Use the same treatment and censoring formulas and the same 0.01 to 0.99 g bounds as IPTW. At each time use a prespecified Q formula saturated in the current binary state W1 by W2 by U by L_k by A_k, with separate time-specific regressions; omit U in hidden-U scenarios and omit the delta_Q lagged terms in every scenario. Convert the two targeted survival estimates to event risks and subtract. Use formula-driven glm fits only; no SuperLearner ensemble is used. | Extract the subject-level influence curve for each targeted regime mean. After converting survival to risk, estimate Var(RD36) as the empirical variance of IC_always-IC_never divided by n, retaining their covariance because both calls use the same individuals. | no | Any ltmle error; any internal glm with converged=FALSE or required rank deficiency; absolute nuisance or fluctuation coefficient above 20; nonfinite Q, g, influence-curve, risk, RD, or SE; targeted risk outside [0,1]; or zero influence-curve variance. More than 1 percent of g predictions clipped, an absolute fluctuation coefficient above 1, or a maximum clever covariate above 50 is retained as a diagnostic warning rather than automatically discarded. |

**Performance.** Primary: Scenario-specific choice-consequence probability Pr(Z=1), where Z=1 if at least one of the three unique family point estimators fails while another succeeds, or if all three succeed and max(IPTW RD, g-formula RD, LTMLE RD)-min(IPTW RD, g-formula RD, LTMLE RD)>0.03. Report the two components separately and report all-family failure separately.. Secondary: For every method: bias, empirical SE, mean model SE, relative error in model SE, MSE, 95 percent coverage, bias-eliminated coverage, two-sided rejection rate for RD36=0, convergence, and each corresponding Monte Carlo SE from the shared ADEMP harness., Mean, median, 90th percentile, and 95th percentile of the within-replicate three-family absolute range among complete replicate triplets., Probability that family estimates disagree in sign and probability that they fall on opposite sides of the prespecified RD=-0.03 benefit threshold., Scenario-specific identity of the lowest-MSE family and MSE ratios relative to it. The two IPTW variance versions count once for point-estimate rankings., Probability that all three families agree within 0.01 while each has absolute bias above 0.02 in hidden-U scenarios., Diagnostic performance for gross failure, defined as nonconvergence, absolute estimation error above 0.03, or a confidence interval excluding the truth on the wrong side. The prespecified warning is positive if any fitted g probability is clipped in more than 1 percent of rows, minimum regime-specific ESS/n is below 0.20, the 99th percentile cumulative weight exceeds 20, the maximum weight exceeds 50, a required current-state cell has fewer than 20 observations, the LTMLE fluctuation coefficient exceeds 1 in absolute value, or a five-fold cross-validated expanded lag model improves log loss by at least 0.005. Report sensitivity, specificity, false-positive rate, and positive predictive value., Report unconditional failure rates first. Conditional-on-convergence performance may be reported only as a secondary decomposition and must retain the original denominator.. 800 replicates. The primary measure is Bernoulli. Requiring worst-case Monte Carlo SE no greater than 0.018 gives R at least 0.25/(0.018 squared)=771.6, so use 800 replicates per scenario. The worst-case MCSE is 0.5/sqrt(800)=0.0177; at a choice-consequence probability of 0.20 it is sqrt(0.20 times 0.80/800)=0.0141. The worst-case 95 percent Monte Carlo half-width is approximately 0.0346.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Use the eight primary identifiable scenarios with observed U and s equal to 0.60 or 1.00; reserve severe-overlap and hidden-U scenarios for boundary and failure-awareness conclusions. Declare estimator choice materially consequential if all three conditions hold: the lower 95 percent Monte Carlo bound for Pr(Z=1) exceeds 0.20 in at least two of the eight scenarios; in at least two such scenarios one family has absolute bias above 0.02 or coverage below 0.90 while another has absolute bias below 0.01 and coverage from 0.93 through 0.97; and the lowest-MSE family changes between a G1Q0 and a G0Q1 scenario with an MSE ratio of at least 1.25 and a gap exceeding two MCSEs. Separately declare three-family agreement insufficient as a validity check if at least six of twelve hidden-U scenarios have three-family mean spread below 0.01, absolute bias above 0.02 for every family, coverage below 0.80 for every family, and convergence above 0.95. Call the diagnostic rule useful only if its lower 95 percent Monte Carlo bound for sensitivity exceeds 0.80 and its upper bound for false-positive rate is below 0.20 in observed-U scenarios.
- Problem is not real if: Within this benchmark, declare no material estimator-choice problem if, in every one of the eight primary identifiable scenarios, the upper 95 percent Monte Carlo bound for Pr(Z=1) is below 0.10, every family has absolute bias below 0.01, current-practice coverage is between 0.93 and 0.97, convergence is at least 0.99, all pairwise differences between scenario mean estimates are below 0.01, and no family has an MSE more than 1.10 times another by more than two MCSEs. This conclusion is restricted to the benchmark and cannot establish a universal default.
- Uninformative if: Classify the result as uninformative if neither declaration is met; if consequential disagreement occurs only at s=1.70; if all three families fail in more than 20 percent of replicates in two or more primary scenarios; if fewer than 90 percent of planned replicates complete; if the exact-truth recursion fails its independent check; or if an implementation audit finds that any method used a different treatment protocol, population, censoring intervention, horizon, or effect scale.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM could be constructed to force the desired estimator disagreement. *Mitigation: Include all four G and Q quadrants, including six benign G0Q0 scenarios; use centered omitted terms with opposing signs; fix all coefficients before generating results; separate severe-overlap scenarios from the primary declaration; and require disagreement to correspond to truth-based bias or coverage failure and a reversal of the lowest-MSE family. Disagreement alone cannot satisfy the decision rule.*
- A mandatory three-family comparison could be mistaken for an identification check. *Mitigation: Analyze paired observed-U and hidden-U versions of the same data. Hidden-U scenarios are used to quantify shared silent failure and cannot be used to rank methods as if one could recover a non-identifiable effect.*
- Methods could silently target different protocols or populations. *Mitigation: Use one eligibility rule, two static six-visit strategies, no grace period, intervention on censoring, one superpopulation, one 36-month horizon, and one marginal risk-difference scale. Automated checks must compare all regime arrays, risk sets, censoring times, and baseline standardization weights before a replicate is accepted.*
- Oracle formulas could unfairly favor a method. *Mitigation: The same declared current-state information is available to every estimator. Correct-model scenarios establish an attainable reference; selective stress omits the same lagged features from the relevant family. No formula may be changed after inspecting performance.*
- Probability clipping could hide practical positivity failure or change comparisons. *Mitigation: Use the same fixed 0.01 to 0.99 bounds for IPTW and TMLE, report the fraction clipped, cumulative weight distribution, ESS, and clever covariates, and retain severe-overlap results as boundary evidence. Do not tune bounds by scenario.*
- Different interval behavior could be misattributed to different point estimators. *Mitigation: Give the identical IPTW point estimate both a common fixed-weight sandwich and a stacked nuisance-adjusted sandwich. Compare their coverage separately and count the point estimate only once in family disagreement.*
- Conditioning on successful fits could make unstable estimators look accurate. *Mitigation: Retain every planned replicate in the convergence denominator, make differential nonconvergence part of the primary choice-consequence measure, and label conditional performance explicitly.*
- A coding error in the truth or event ordering could determine every conclusion. *Mitigation: Use exact probability recursion, state-mass conservation checks, a separately written intervention simulator, common-random-number comparisons for observed-U versus hidden-U analyses, and test absorbing events and censoring masks before the main run.*
- Simple parametric nuisance models may not represent modern data-adaptive practice. *Mitigation: State this as a parametric benchmark of estimator families and assumption stress. No claim about SuperLearner ensembles, irregular EHR processes, or universal estimator rankings is permitted.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The full design contains 24 scenarios times 800 replicates, giving 19,200 analyzed datasets. Longitudinal TMLE requires two regime fits per replicate, so the run contains 24 times 800 times 2=38,400 ltmle fits. At 5 seconds per fit this is 53.3 core-hours or 8.9 ideal wall-hours on 6 workers. The pooled glm fits, state recursion, diagnostics, serialization, and imperfect parallel scaling should bring the full run to approximately 10 to 14 hours. Truth evaluation is exact and takes seconds rather than four minutes per scenario. The existing seeded, resumable harness should dispatch scenario-replicate units to six workers and checkpoint after every replicate. Run a 100-replicate timing pilot without changing coefficients or methods. If it projects beyond 18 wall-hours, use the prespecified smaller design that removes only s=1.70: 16 scenarios times 800 replicates times 2=25,600 ltmle fits, equal to 35.6 core-hours or 5.9 ideal wall-hours. This preserves both identification levels and all four nuisance quadrants. No SuperLearner ensemble is used.

**Also bears on.** GMT-04 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods; Robins JM, Hernán MA, Brumback B. Marginal structural models and causal inference in epidemiology; Cole SR, Hernán MA. Constructing inverse probability weights for marginal structural models; McGrath S, Young JG, Hernán MA. gfoRmula: An R package for estimating the effects of sustained treatment strategies via the parametric g-formula; Bang H, Robins JM. Doubly robust estimation in missing data and causal inference models; ltmle: Longitudinal Targeted Maximum Likelihood Estimation. CRAN; Su L, et al. TrialEmulation: an R package to emulate target trials for causal analysis of observational time-to-event data

## 9. LRN-05: Operating characteristics of counterfactual calibration, discrimination, and Brier scores under unmeasured confounding

*Catalog entry: A counterfactual prediction is validated under the assumptions that produced it, and nothing maps its output to a decision*

**Question.** Across 24 longitudinal scenarios, by how much does omitting a binary confounder move estimated 24-month counterfactual calibration, AUC, and Brier scores under always-treat and never-treat strategies when the confounder-treatment odds ratio ranges from 0.40 to 4.00 and the confounder-outcome rate ratio ranges from 1.00 to 2.00; at which combinations does 95% interval coverage fall below 90%, and do conventional weight diagnostics identify those failures?

**Aims.** The primary aim is to estimate the bias and 95% interval coverage of the weak-calibration intercept for an exactly calibrated 24-month always-treat risk score when artificial-censoring weights condition on the complete observed history but omit U. Secondary aims quantify bias in the calibration slope, ten-bin calibration curve, Brier score, AUC, and always-treat versus never-treat contrasts; determine whether unmeasured confounding can make a deliberately miscalibrated score appear better calibrated; separate exchangeability failure from propensity-model misspecification and practical positivity; and measure the sensitivity of prespecified weight diagnostics for consequential metric error. The study addresses operating characteristics under assumption failure. It does not redesign prediction-to-action mapping, which existing work already addresses through explicit decision rules and utility evaluation.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 4000 independent individuals followed for 24 fixed monthly cycles, indexed t=0,...,23. Draw X1 from N(0,1), X2 from Bernoulli(0.50), and the unmeasured binary frailty U from Bernoulli(0.30), independently. Draw L0 from Bernoulli(expit(-0.40+0.45X1+0.65X2+0.80U)). For each later month among event-free individuals, draw Lt from Bernoulli(expit(-0.70+1.35L(t-1)+0.25X1+0.35X2-0.50A(t-1))). Thus Lt is an observed time-varying confounder affected by prior treatment. At t=0, draw A0 from Bernoulli(expit(alpha+0.35X1+0.50X2+0.90L0+gammaU)). For t=1,...,23, draw At from Bernoulli(expit(alpha+3.50(2A(t-1)-1)+0.25X1+0.35X2+0.75Lt+gammaU)). Within each month the order is Lt, At, and then the event. Treatment and covariate generation stop after the event. There is no loss to follow-up, competing event, or missing measured covariate.
- Outcome model: Among individuals event-free at the start of month t, generate the event from a piecewise-exponential discrete-time model with probability 1-exp(-exp(eta_t)), where eta_t=-4.80+0.30X1+0.40X2+0.50Lt+deltaU-0.40At-0.30AtX2. At eta=-4.80, the untreated 24-month risk before covariate contributions is 1-exp(-24exp(-4.80))=0.179. Once an event occurs, Y(24)=1; otherwise Y(24)=0 after month 23.
- Treatment effect: The protocols are g1: set At=1 at every monthly decision while alive, and g0: set At=0 at every monthly decision while alive. On the piecewise-exponential rate scale, the direct treatment rate ratio is exp(-0.40)=0.670 when X2=0 and exp(-0.70)=0.497 when X2=1. Treatment also lowers the odds of Lt=1 in the next month by exp(-0.50)=0.607, producing an additional mediated effect because Lt raises the event rate. Marginal risk differences are therefore time-dependent and are not constrained to be constant.
- Effect modification: X2 modifies the direct treatment effect through the -0.30AtX2 interaction. The treatment rate ratio changes from 0.670 for X2=0 to 0.497 for X2=1. X1 and L history produce additional heterogeneity in absolute benefit without interacting with treatment.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 4 treatment associations times 3 outcome associations times 2 support conditions. The target-trial protocol is fixed before analysis: eligibility at t=0, treatment assignment every month, no grace period, 24-month follow-up, all-cause binary event by month 24, and sustained always-treat versus never-treat strategies. Consistency, no interference, correct temporal ordering, perfect measurement of X1, X2, L, A, and the event, and positivity in principle are true. The fixed oracle prediction is exactly P(Y under g by month 24 given X1,X2,L0); prediction-model estimation error is absent. A second score, expit(-0.35+0.75logit(p)), introduces known moderate miscalibration. The exact observed-history propensity benchmark eliminates nuisance-model error while still omitting individual U.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot establish exchangeability in an application or provide a universal confounding threshold. It says nothing about irregular visits, informative observation times, continuous doses, grace periods, dynamic treatment rules, competing risks, ordinary loss to follow-up, measurement error, interference, transport between databases, multiple or continuous hidden confounders, prediction-model training uncertainty, nonparametric prediction models, or clinical utility under a specified loss function. Fixed monthly observation and a binary treatment are deliberate first-study restrictions.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Direct U-to-treatment association | gamma=log(0.40)=-0.916291, gamma=0, odds ratio 1.00, gamma=log(2.50)=0.916291, gamma=log(4.00)=1.386294 | The levels include reverse selection, a no-association control, a moderate association, and a strong stress test. Crossing the direction of selection with the harmful effect of U prevents the design from forcing all bias in one direction. |
| Direct U-to-outcome association | delta=0, rate ratio 1.00, delta=log(1.50)=0.405465, delta=log(2.00)=0.693147 | These levels provide a no-prognostic-effect control and moderate and strong residual prognostic effects on the event-rate scale. Exchangeability is genuinely violated only when delta is positive and gamma is nonzero. |
| Support for sustained treatment | alpha=-0.50, adequate-support condition, alpha=-2.00, stressed-support condition | For X1=0, X2=0, L=0, and U=0, baseline treatment probabilities are 0.378 and 0.119. After prior treatment, the corresponding continuation probabilities are 0.953 and 0.818. All probabilities remain strictly between zero and one, so the factor creates practical rather than structural nonpositivity. |

**Estimands.**

- **Counterfactual weak calibration and ten-bin calibration curve**: For each strategy g in {g0,g1} and fixed score r_g, weak calibration is the superpopulation pair (alpha_g,beta_g) satisfying logit P(Y under g by month 24=1 given r_g)=alpha_g+beta_g logit(r_g). The calibration curve additionally contains c_gk=P(Y under g by month 24=1 given r_g in population risk decile k), k=1,...,10, with calibration error c_gk-E(r_g given decile k). For the oracle score p_g, the true weak-calibration values are alpha_g=0 and beta_g=1. For q_g=expit(-0.35+0.75logit(p_g)), the true values are alpha_g=0.35/0.75=0.466667 and beta_g=1/0.75=1.333333. Strategy contrasts are alpha_g1-alpha_g0, beta_g1-beta_g0, and c_g1k-c_g0k. All quantities are marginal over the superpopulation represented by X1,X2,L0 rather than conditional on the realized validation sample. *Truth: For each baseline profile B=(X1,X2,L0), calculate P(U=1 given B) exactly by Bayes' rule from the stated U and L0 models. Under each strategy, propagate survivor mass over the two U states and two Lt states for 24 months using the stated L transition and event probabilities. This finite-state recursion gives p_g(B) without outcome simulation. Oracle and transformed-score weak-calibration coefficients are therefore known exactly. Freeze score-decile cut points using the first 200000 observations of a two-million-person truth sample for each delta level; calculate bin risks from the remaining 1.8 million observations.*
- **Counterfactual Brier score**: For each strategy and score, BS_g=E[(Y under g by month 24-r_g)^2] in the baseline superpopulation. Report BS_g1, BS_g0, and BS_g1-BS_g0. Lower values indicate better squared-error performance, but the cross-strategy difference is descriptive because each strategy induces a different counterfactual outcome distribution. *Truth: For every truth-sample baseline profile, use the exact p_g(B) recursion and evaluate p_g(B)(1-p_g(B))+(p_g(B)-r_g(B))^2. Average this quantity over 1.8 million evaluation profiles. Use common baseline draws across strategies and scores to reduce Monte Carlo error in contrasts.*
- **Counterfactual cumulative-dynamic AUC**: At month 24, AUC_g is the probability that r_g(B_i)>r_g(B_j), plus one half the tie probability, for independent individuals with Y_i under g=1 and Y_j under g=0. Report AUC under each strategy and AUC_g1-AUC_g0. This is a marginal superpopulation discrimination estimand. *Truth: In each truth sample, sort scores once and compute the AUC in O(n log n) using p_g(B) as the case weight and 1-p_g(B) as the control weight. Estimate truth Monte Carlo SE from 18 independent batches of 100000 evaluation profiles. Add another one-million-person batch if any truth Monte Carlo SE exceeds 0.0005.*
- **Marginal 24-month strategy risk contrast**: RD_24=E[Y under g1 by month 24]-E[Y under g0 by month 24], with negative values favoring sustained treatment. This contextual estimand verifies that the prediction targets correspond to the prespecified protocols; it is not the primary object of the study. *Truth: Average the exact recursively calculated p_g1(B) and p_g0(B) over the same truth population and subtract. Common random numbers are used for both strategies.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Full-history oracle artificial-censoring IPW | For strategy g, define C_i^g=1 when the observed treatment equals g through the earlier of the event month and month 23. For an event-free individual, adherence is required through month 23. Use the exact product probability pi_i^g of the observed adherence path conditional on X1,X2,L history,A history,U and being event-free. Set w_i^g=C_i^g/pi_i^g and calculate products on the log scale. Estimate bin risks and Brier scores by normalized weighted means, weak calibration by weighted logistic regression of Y on 1 and logit(r), AUC by the normalized weighted case-control rank statistic, and marginal risk by the normalized weighted mean of Y. No weight truncation is used. | Use a subject-level empirical sandwich. Let psi_i contain the weighted score equations for calibration coefficients, weighted mean equations for bin risks, Brier score and risk, and the first-order Hajek projection for the weighted AUC. With J=-n^(-1)sum d psi_i/d theta and K=n^(-1)sum psi_i psi_i transpose, estimate covariance as J^(-1)KJ^(-T)/n. Estimate cross-strategy covariance by stacking g0 and g1 equations for the same individual. The treatment probabilities are known, so no nuisance-score block is added. | no | Failure occurs if either weighted case or control mass is zero, a calibration Newton solve fails to reach a maximum score component below 1e-8 within 50 iterations, the calibration Hessian condition number exceeds 1e12, or any estimate, SE, or covariance element is nonfinite. Large weights alone are reported as diagnostics and are not silently classified as failure. |
| Exact observed-history artificial-censoring IPW | Use the same estimators as the full-history oracle, but replace each treatment probability by the exact P(At=g given observed X1,X2,L and A history and survival to t), integrating over rather than conditioning on U. Compute this probability with a two-state forward filter. Initialize P(U given X1,X2,L0) by Bayes' rule; before each treatment update it using observed prior treatment, survival, and the new Lt; average the two U-specific treatment probabilities; after observing At, update the posterior with its treatment likelihood. This method knows the exact observed-data propensity and therefore isolates exchangeability failure from propensity-model error. | Use the same subject-level sandwich J^(-1)KJ^(-T)/n as the oracle method, including the weighted AUC Hajek projection and joint g0-g1 covariance. The filtered observed-history probabilities are treated as known DGM quantities, so there is no fitted-propensity nuisance block. | no | Apply the oracle method's zero-mass, Newton tolerance, Hessian, and finite-value rules. Also fail if a filtered posterior or prescribed-action probability is outside [0,1] by more than 1e-12; values within numerical tolerance are clipped to that range before log-weight calculation. |
| Estimated measured-history artificial-censoring IPW | Fit a baseline logistic model A0 on X1+X2+L0 and a pooled follow-up logistic model At on X1+X2+Lt+A(t-1) among event-free person-months. U is omitted. Use fitted prescribed-action probabilities to form untruncated artificial-censoring weights and calculate all metrics exactly as in the oracle method. This is the current-practice counterfactual validation comparator. | Stack the two logistic treatment-model score vectors, clustered by individual, with the metric estimating equations. Compute the full numerical Jacobian by centered finite differences with step 1e-6 times max(1,absolute parameter value), and use J^(-1)KJ^(-T)/n. For AUC, use its weighted Hajek projection in the meat and the finite-difference derivative of the weighted rank statistic with respect to treatment-model coefficients. This incorporates estimated-weight uncertainty and within-person treatment-score dependence. | yes | Failure occurs if either treatment glm reports converged=FALSE, has a nonfinite coefficient, or has an absolute coefficient above 20; if a metric meets the oracle method's zero-mass, Newton, Hessian, or finite-value failure rule; or if the stacked covariance cannot be inverted after symmetric eigenvalues below 1e-10 times the largest eigenvalue are declared singular. Do not discard a replicate solely because of an extreme fitted probability or weight. |
| Unweighted observed strategy adherers | Restrict to individuals whose observed treatment follows the strategy through the event or month 23, assign weight one, and calculate the same calibration, Brier, AUC, and risk estimators. This is the naive comparator that treats observed adherers as if they were a representative validation sample. | Use the subject-level sandwich for the unweighted metric equations and the ordinary Hajek projection for AUC. No treatment-model score block is included. | no | Failure occurs when there are no adherent cases or controls, fewer than two nonempty calibration deciles, failure of the calibration Newton or Hessian criteria, or any nonfinite estimate or SE. The number of adherers and events is retained as a performance outcome. |

**Performance.** Primary: Scenario-specific coverage of the nominal 95% interval for the true weak-calibration intercept alpha=0 of the oracle score under g1, estimated by exact observed-history artificial-censoring IPW. Bias in alpha is the co-primary descriptive quantity used to interpret undercoverage.. Secondary: Bias, empirical SE, mean model SE, relative error in model SE, MSE, 95% coverage, bias-eliminated coverage, rejection rate, and convergence for every scalar estimand and method, all with Monte Carlo SE from the existing ADEMP harness., Maximum absolute bias across the ten calibration-bin risks and pointwise coverage for every bin., Bias and coverage for the weak-calibration slope, Brier score, AUC, marginal risk, and g1 minus g0 contrasts., For the deliberately miscalibrated score, the change in absolute distance of the estimated calibration intercept from 0 and slope from 1. False reassurance is recorded when omission of U moves the intercept at least 0.10 closer to 0 or the slope at least 0.10 closer to 1 while moving it away from its true value., Normalized maximum weight, 99th percentile weight, effective sample size ESS=(sum w)^2/sum(w^2), and the proportion of prescribed-action probabilities below 0.05., A prespecified diagnostic flag equal to one if ESS/4000 is below 0.25, the maximum mean-one-normalized weight exceeds 50, or more than 1% of required treatment probabilities are below 0.05. Report its flag probability in cells with and without consequential metric bias., Differences between full-history oracle, exact observed-history, estimated observed-history, and unweighted estimates, which separate exchangeability failure, nuisance-model error, and selection among adherers.. 2000 replicates. Coverage is the primary performance measure. To estimate coverage near the decision boundary of 0.90 with Monte Carlo SE no larger than 0.007 requires R at least 0.90(1-0.90)/(0.007^2)=1836.7. Rounding to 2000 gives MCSE sqrt(0.90(0.10)/2000)=0.00671 at 90% coverage and sqrt(0.95(0.05)/2000)=0.00487 at nominal 95% coverage.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Define consequential failure for a metric as absolute calibration-intercept bias at least 0.10 log-odds, maximum absolute calibration-bin risk bias at least 0.02, absolute Brier-score bias at least 0.01, or absolute AUC bias at least 0.02, together with coverage at or below 0.90 for the corresponding scalar or bin. The operating-characteristic problem is practically demonstrated if exact observed-history IPW has consequential failure in at least 3 of the 12 cells with delta greater than 0 and gamma not equal to 0, including at least one alpha=-0.50 adequate-support cell; the full-history oracle has calibration-intercept bias below 0.03 and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97 in those same cells; and at least 10 of the 12 no-joint-confounding control cells have calibration-intercept bias below 0.05 and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97. Direction reversal between gamma=log(0.40) and positive gamma is supportive but not required. A separate false-reassurance finding is declared if the prespecified miscalibrated-score criterion occurs in at least one adequate-support joint-confounding cell.
- Problem is not real if: The chosen confounding grid does not demonstrate a practically important failure if all 12 joint-confounding cells have absolute calibration-intercept bias below 0.05, maximum calibration-bin bias below 0.01, absolute Brier bias below 0.005, absolute AUC bias below 0.01, and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97 under exact observed-history IPW, with no false-reassurance cell. This conclusion is restricted to the stated binary-confounder DGM and is not evidence that observational validation tests exchangeability generally.
- Uninformative if: The result is uninformative about exchangeability if failures occur only under alpha=-2.00 and the full-history oracle fails in the same cells; fewer than 95% of replicates yield the primary estimate in any adequate-support cell; more than 2 of the 12 control cells violate the control thresholds; truth Monte Carlo SE remains above 0.0005 after the additional batch; or observed results fall between the numerical real and not-real criteria. Failure of only the fitted-propensity method while the exact observed-history method passes is classified as nuisance-model failure, not evidence about unmeasured confounding.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM could be constructed so that unmeasured confounding must produce the desired result. *Mitigation: Treatment and outcome associations are crossed independently. The grid includes no-association controls, treatment-only and outcome-only controls, reverse selection, two positive association strengths, and two support conditions. The decision rule requires failure under adequate support, recovery after conditioning on U, and acceptable behavior in no-joint-confounding controls.*
- Propensity-model misspecification could be mistaken for failure of exchangeability. *Mitigation: The primary comparison uses the exact observed-history treatment probability obtained by latent-state filtering. The fitted pooled-logistic method is reported separately. Their difference quantifies nuisance-model error.*
- Practical positivity could dominate the result. *Mitigation: Support is an explicit factor. No weight is silently truncated. Weight distributions, prescribed-action probabilities, ESS, convergence, and oracle performance are reported. The main positive decision requires at least one adequate-support cell.*
- A correctly calibrated prediction model could conceal whether confounding makes a bad model look good. *Mitigation: Every dataset is evaluated with both an exact oracle score and a deterministic score having known intercept and slope miscalibration. This separates failure to recognize miscalibration from apparent miscalibration created by confounding.*
- Prediction-model training error or overfitting could obscure validation behavior. *Mitigation: Prediction functions are fixed by the DGM and finite-state recursion before replicates begin. No model is trained in the validation sample.*
- An incorrect artificial-censoring implementation could create the reported bias. *Mitigation: The protocol fixes the within-month ordering and requires adherence only through the event for cases and through month 23 for event-free controls. Unit checks must compare large-sample oracle-IPW estimates with directly enumerated counterfactual truths under every delta level before the replicate run.*
- The sandwich variance or weighted AUC influence calculation could be wrong. *Mitigation: Before the full run, compare sandwich SEs with full individual-level bootstrap SEs using 199 resamples in 25 datasets from each of four anchor cells. A relative difference above 10% triggers code review before simulation results are released. This bootstrap is an implementation check, not a substituted interval method.*
- Monte Carlo error in the truth could be interpreted as estimator bias. *Mitigation: Calibration parameters are analytically known. Other truths use 1.8 million independent evaluation profiles after decile construction, common random numbers for contrasts, batch Monte Carlo SEs, and automatic extension when truth SE exceeds 0.0005.*
- Dropping failed replicates could make unstable methods look accurate. *Mitigation: Convergence is a reported outcome. Performance summaries give both the unconditional failure rate and conditional estimates among successful replicates; no method is compared only after selecting a common successful subset without also reporting that subset's size.*
- The study could be misread as validating exchangeability or clinical utility in real data. *Mitigation: Claims are restricted to operating characteristics under the stated confounding grid. The protocol excludes action thresholds, utilities, transportability, and empirical tests of exchangeability; these exclusions appear in the abstract, catalog attachment, and discussion.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The full design contains 24 scenarios times 2000 replicates, or 48000 replicates. The fitted status-quo method requires two treatment GLMs per replicate, totaling 96000 GLM fits. The remaining propensity calculations are direct recursions; calibration uses small one- or two-parameter Newton solves. There are zero ltmle fits and zero SuperLearner fits. The two-million-person truth calculation is needed only for the three delta levels because gamma and alpha do not change counterfactual truth; both strategies are evaluated in the same pass. Expected wall time is approximately 4 to 8 hours on 6 workers, including truth generation and checkpointing, plus roughly 1 hour for the prespecified bootstrap implementation checks. This fits an overnight run. No uninstalled package, including lmtp, concrete, or CICI, is necessary.

**Also bears on.** LRN-05 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine 2019;38(11):2074-2102; Keogh RH, Van Geloven N. Prediction under interventions: evaluation of counterfactual performance using longitudinal observational data. Epidemiology 2024;35(3):329-339; Boyer CB, Dahabreh IJ, Steingrimsson JA. Estimating and evaluating counterfactual prediction models. Statistics in Medicine 2025;44(23-24):e70287; Lin L, Sperrin M, Jenkins DA, Martin GP, Peek N. A scoping review of causal methods enabling predictions under hypothetical interventions. Diagnostic and Prognostic Research 2021;5(1):3; Sachs MC, Sjolander A, Gabriel EE. Aim for clinical utility, not just predictive accuracy. Epidemiology 2020;31(3):359-364
