## 10. MER-01: Validation-anchored latent-state correction for longitudinal phenotype error in sustained-strategy emulation

*Catalog entry: Phenotypes enter emulations as error-free binary variables*

**Question.** Across sex-standardized sensitivity and specificity values of 0.70, 0.85, and 0.99, differential error odds ratios of 1 and 4, and internal validation samples of 100, 250, 500, and 1000 participants, how large are the bias, nonadditive treatment-confounder error interaction, and 95% interval undercoverage for the marginal 12-month risk difference comparing sustained treatment with sustained no treatment; what is the smallest validation sample for which latent-state multiple imputation reduces absolute bias by at least 50%, leaves less than 0.01 absolute bias, restores coverage to 0.925 through 0.975, and does not increase MSE by more than 25%?

**Aims.** Estimate the regime-effect bias caused by treating longitudinal treatment, confounder, and outcome phenotypes as error-free binary variables. Quantify nonadditivity as B_AL minus B_A minus B_L plus B_0, where B denotes risk-difference bias under joint treatment-confounder error, treatment-only error, confounder-only error, and no error. Estimate the incremental contribution of outcome error as B_ALY minus B_AL. Determine the smallest prespecified internal validation sample that makes full uncertainty propagation preferable to thresholded analysis under explicit bias, coverage, MSE, and convergence criteria. Test whether favorable performance depends on correct specification of the latent outcome model.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 4000 independent eligible participants followed at fixed monthly visits t=0,...,11. Define expit(x)=1/(1+exp(-x)). Generate X1 from N(0,1) and X2 from Bernoulli(0.5), independently. Generate the true binary time-varying confounder L0 from Bernoulli(expit(-0.40+0.50*X1+0.40*X2)). For t=1,...,11, generate Lt from Bernoulli(expit(-0.80+1.40*L(t-1)-0.50*A(t-1)+0.35*X1+0.30*X2+0.03*t)). Generate natural treatment A0 from Bernoulli(expit(-0.70+0.80*L0+0.25*X1+0.20*X2)). For t=1,...,11, generate At from Bernoulli(expit(-1.40+2.00*A(t-1)+0.90*Lt+0.25*X1+0.20*X2+0.02*t)). This creates measured treatment-confounder feedback: Lt predicts subsequent treatment, while prior treatment reduces subsequent Lt. There is no loss to follow-up, competing event, or eligibility error. For each designated error-prone node Z in A or L, generate Zstar=Z XOR E. Initialize E0 from Bernoulli(e_x). At later visits retain the preceding error state with probability 0.80; otherwise redraw it from Bernoulli(e_x). Treatment and confounder error chains are conditionally independent. When the differential error odds ratio is 1, e_x is 0.30, 0.15, or 0.01 for accuracy levels 0.70, 0.85, or 0.99. When the differential error odds ratio is 4, the error probabilities for X2=0 and X2=1 are respectively 0.1627 and 0.4373 at accuracy 0.70, 0.0696 and 0.2304 at accuracy 0.85, and 0.0040 and 0.0160 at accuracy 0.99. Thus the odds of classification error are four times greater for X2=1, and the 50:50 sex-standardized sensitivity and specificity equal the named accuracy. Nodes outside the designated error pattern are observed exactly. At the start of each replicate, select a simple random internal validation sample independently of all variables. Validation participants have gold-standard A, L, and Y at every relevant time as well as their proxies. Use one random ranking to make samples of 100, 250, 500, and 1000 participants nested.
- Outcome model: After treatment at t=11, generate the true 12-month binary adverse outcome Y from Bernoulli(expit(-2.00+0.40*X1+0.30*X2+0.90*L11-0.60*A11-0.40*A11*X2)). In model-misspecification stress scenarios, add -0.60*A11*I(X1>0.5) to this linear predictor. When Y is designated error-prone, generate Ystar=Y XOR EY using the same sex-specific error probabilities as the longitudinal nodes, with an independent terminal error draw. Otherwise set Ystar=Y.
- Treatment effect: The intervention sets every monthly treatment value rather than conditioning on the natural treatment process. At month 11, the controlled direct treatment odds ratio is exp(-0.60)=0.549 for X2=0 and exp(-1.00)=0.368 for X2=1. Earlier treatment also affects Y indirectly by reducing subsequent L. The primary contrast therefore includes both the direct final-month effect and the mediated effect of the complete sustained treatment history.
- Effect modification: X2 modifies the direct treatment effect by -0.40 on the log-odds scale and also determines differential phenotype accuracy. This makes differential error consequential for the marginal contrast rather than merely a precision issue. The stress DGM adds a nonlinear treatment effect of -0.60 on the log-odds scale among participants with X1 greater than 0.5; the correction model deliberately omits this threshold interaction.
- Deliberately true: The 24 primary scenarios form a full factorial of four error-node patterns, three accuracy levels, and two differential error odds ratios at validation n=500. Twelve validation-size extensions use all-node error at accuracy 0.70 or 0.85, differential odds ratios 1 or 4, and validation n=100, 250, or 1000; the corresponding n=500 cells already occur in the primary factorial. Four misspecification scenarios use all-node error, accuracy 0.70 or 0.85, differential odds ratios 1 or 4, and validation n=500. Two exact no-error controls complete the 42 scenarios. The overall design is therefore not fully factorial. Sequential exchangeability, consistency, positivity in the generating law, perfect internal gold standards, independent validation sampling, fixed visit times, correct treatment-model functional form, and the core latent-state model are deliberately true. Serial phenotype error is deliberately present with persistence 0.80, so errors do not accumulate as independent monthly coin flips.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot assess transport of sensitivity or specificity from another site, convenience-sample validation, imperfect gold standards, error in eligibility, irregular observation times, informative visit processes, loss to follow-up, competing events, asymmetric sensitivity and specificity, treatment-dependent outcome detection beyond dependence on X2, correlated errors between different phenotype nodes, continuous phenotype scores, or interventions defined by the observed proxy rather than the latent clinical state. It evaluates one binary confounder, one binary treatment, one terminal binary outcome, and sustained static strategies over 12 months. It does not establish that native probabilistic-input software is required because the correction deliberately wraps a deterministic estimator.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Error-prone nodes | Treatment A only, Confounder L only, Treatment A and confounder L, Treatment A, confounder L, and outcome Y | The first three levels permit the prespecified nonadditivity calculation. Comparing the last two isolates the incremental contribution of outcome error. Existing one-node corrections are treated as component prior art; the research target is their joint longitudinal behavior. |
| Sex-standardized sensitivity and specificity | 0.70, 0.85, 0.99 | These levels span the catalog's proposed range from a weak broad phenotype through moderate performance to near-manual abstraction. The 0.99 level is a near-null condition, while a separate 1.00 control supplies an exact null. |
| Differential error odds ratio | 1, 4 | An odds ratio of 1 represents nondifferential error conditional on the modeled history. An odds ratio of 4 is the upper stress level proposed by the catalog. The primary decision cannot rely solely on the 0.70 and odds-ratio-4 combination. |
| Internal validation sample size | 100, 250, 500, 1000 | These are 2.5%, 6.25%, 12.5%, and 25% of the cohort. They cover a small chart review, two intermediate efforts, and an unusually large validation exercise. The full node-pattern factorial uses 500; the other sizes are evaluated for joint A, L, and Y error at accuracy 0.70 and 0.85. |
| Latent outcome-model congruence | Core model represented by the correction, Stress model containing an omitted X1 threshold interaction | The stress level prevents the study from evaluating the correction only under the model that generated the data. |
| Exact no-error control | Accuracy 1.00 under the core outcome model, Accuracy 1.00 under the stress outcome model | These controls must show that the analysis itself does not manufacture a measurement-error effect. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary marginal 12-month risk difference**: RD12=Pr(Y under A0=...=A11=1 equals 1)-Pr(Y under A0=...=A11=0 equals 1) in the superpopulation. Eligibility is assessed at baseline for all generated participants; assignment occurs before A0; treatment is sustained monthly with no grace period; follow-up ends after the month-11 treatment and confounder values; the outcome is the true adverse state at month 12. The estimand is defined using latent treatment and outcome states, not their EHR proxies. *Truth: For each of the two outcome mechanisms, core and misspecified stress, simulate 4000000 new participants under each intervention. Use paired baseline draws and paired random uniforms for L and Y across intervention arms. Compute the difference in empirical risks. Measurement accuracy and validation size do not alter the causal truth, so only two truth evaluations are needed. Even without variance reduction, the worst-case Monte Carlo SE for an overall difference of two risks at this size is sqrt(0.5/4000000)=0.000354; subgroup truth has approximately 2000000 participants per X2 stratum and worst-case SE about 0.0005. Record these truth Monte Carlo SEs and use random streams separate from the finite-sample replicates.*
- **Effect-modifier-specific 12-month risk differences**: The same sustained-treatment contrast calculated separately for X2=0 and X2=1 in their respective superpopulations. *Truth: Use the same 4000000-person intervention simulations and calculate arm risks within each X2 stratum. These estimands reveal whether marginal cancellation hides differential phenotype bias in the subgroup whose treatment effect and error rate differ.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Thresholded clone-censor inverse-probability estimator | Treat Astar, Lstar, and Ystar as truth whenever those nodes are error-prone. Clone each participant to d=1 and d=0. Censor a clone at the first month for which Astar differs from d. Fit a baseline propensity model by binomial glm with A0star as the response and L0star, X1, and X2 as predictors. Fit a follow-up model with Atstar as the response and A(t-1)star, Ltstar, X1, X2, and numeric month t as predictors. For each clone consistent through month 11, form the unstabilized cumulative weight as the inverse product of the fitted probabilities of its observed treatment values. Do not truncate weights. Estimate each arm risk by the weighted Hajek mean of Ystar among clones consistent through month 11; estimate RD12 as risk for d=1 minus risk for d=0. | Use a participant-clustered M-estimation sandwich that stacks the two treatment-model score vectors with the two weighted risk equations I(adherent to d)*Wd*(Ystar-mu_d)=0. Include both clones in the same participant score so their covariance is retained. With A equal to the mean derivative of the stacked scores and B equal to the mean outer product of participant scores, use A inverse times B times the transpose of A inverse, divided by 4000. Compute the bread by central differences with step 0.000001*(1+absolute parameter value), and obtain the RD variance with contrast vector mu1-mu0. | yes | Declare failure if either glm reports converged=FALSE, any coefficient or fitted probability is nonfinite, either arm has no fully consistent clone, a cumulative weight is nonfinite, the stacked bread has condition number above 1e12, the estimated variance is negative or nonfinite, or the interval cannot be formed. Finite extreme weights remain in the analysis and are reported through effective sample size and weight-tail diagnostics. |
| Validation-anchored latent-state multiple-imputation IPW | Use validation truths to fit the latent data law: separate logistic models for L0, Lt for t=1,...,11, A0, At for t=1,...,11, and Y. The core formulas contain exactly the predictors in the DGM. Give nonintercept coefficients independent N(0,2.5 squared) priors and intercepts N(0,5 squared) priors; obtain a penalized mode and Hessian, then draw coefficients from the corresponding multivariate-normal Laplace approximation. For each error-prone node and X2 stratum, estimate the initial error probability and the transition probabilities Pr(Et=1|E(t-1)=0) and Pr(Et=1|E(t-1)=1) using independent Beta(0.5,0.5) priors. Estimate terminal outcome error probabilities with the same prior. For each of M=20 imputations, draw all model and error parameters, then sample each nonvalidated participant's latent history conditional on all observed proxies using a log-scale forward-filtering backward-sampling algorithm on the 16-state process (Lt,At,E_Lt,E_At). Incorporate Ystar through its terminal likelihood before backward sampling, then draw Y conditional on the sampled terminal state and Ystar. Retain gold-standard states for validated participants. Analyze each completed data set with the same untruncated Hajek IPW estimator using imputed true A, L, and Y. Average the 20 RD estimates. In stress scenarios, fit the same core latent outcome model and deliberately omit A11*I(X1>0.5). | Within each imputation use the complete-data participant-clustered M-estimation sandwich. Let Wbar be the mean of the 20 completed-data RD variances and B be the sample variance of the 20 RD estimates. Use T=Wbar+(1+1/20)*B. For Barnard-Rubin degrees of freedom, set r=(1+1/20)*B/Wbar, nu_old=19*(1+1/r)^2, lambda=(r+2/(nu_old+3))/(r+1), nu_com=3988, nu_obs=((nu_com+1)/(nu_com+3))*nu_com*(1-lambda), and nu=(1/nu_old+1/nu_obs)^(-1). If B=0, use T=Wbar and nu=3988. | no | Declare failure if a penalized fit does not reach a gradient maximum below 1e-7, its negative Hessian is not positive definite, a forward recursion has zero or nonfinite normalizing probability after log-sum-exp stabilization, any of the 20 completed-data IPW analyses fails, T is negative or nonfinite, or the interval cannot be formed. Do not replace failed completed-data draws, because selective redrawing would favor the method. |
| Oracle complete-data IPW | Apply the same clone-censor Hajek estimator to true A, L, and Y for every participant, using the correctly specified baseline and follow-up treatment models. This is a design benchmark, not an available-data proposal. | Use the same participant-clustered stacked M-estimation sandwich as the thresholded estimator. | no | Use the same complete-data failure criteria as the thresholded estimator. |

**Performance.** Primary: Empirical coverage of the nominal 95% interval for the marginal RD12, reported for every method and scenario. Coverage is calculated among valid estimates, with the convergence proportion reported beside it; a sensitivity analysis counts every failed interval as noncoverage.. Secondary: Bias with Monte Carlo SE, Absolute bias, Empirical SE, Mean model SE, Relative error in model SE, MSE, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for H0: RD12=0, Mean interval width, Convergence proportion, Arm-specific effective sample size defined as (sum W)^2/sum(W^2), Median and 99th percentile cumulative weights, Nonadditive bias interaction B_AL-B_A-B_L+B_0 with a paired Monte Carlo SE, Incremental outcome-error bias B_ALY-B_AL with a paired Monte Carlo SE, Bias and coverage for X2-specific risk differences, Smallest validation size meeting the prespecified worth-it rule. 500 replicates. At nominal coverage 0.95, the binomial Monte Carlo SE with 500 replicates is sqrt(0.95*0.05/500)=0.00975, less than one percentage point. If true coverage is as low as 0.90, it is sqrt(0.90*0.10/500)=0.01342. The good-coverage lower boundary of 0.925 and failure boundary of 0.90 are separated by 0.025, so this precision can distinguish the prespecified decisions without spending the majority of the overnight budget on repetitions. All method comparisons and bias decompositions use common random numbers within a replicate.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require all validity gates: in the exact no-error core control, thresholded absolute bias is at most 0.005 and coverage is 0.925 through 0.975; in every key scenario, oracle absolute bias is at most 0.01, oracle coverage is 0.925 through 0.975, oracle convergence is at least 0.95, and median oracle effective sample size is at least 200 in each arm. Conditional on those gates, call the problem real if at least three of the four all-node mechanisms formed by accuracy 0.70 or 0.85 and differential odds ratio 1 or 4 have thresholded absolute bias of at least 0.02, have absolute bias minus 1.96 times its Monte Carlo SE greater than 0.01, and have coverage at most 0.90. Also require absolute nonadditivity |B_AL-B_A-B_L+B_0| of at least 0.005 and at least 25% of |B_AL| in at least two of those four mechanisms. Separately call a validation size worth its variance cost if, in at least three of those four mechanisms, the corrected estimator reduces absolute bias by at least 50%, leaves absolute bias below 0.01, has coverage from 0.925 through 0.975, has MSE no greater than 1.25 times thresholded MSE, and converges in at least 95% of replicates. Report the smallest qualifying size; if none qualifies, report that the required size exceeds 1000 under this design.
- Problem is not real if: After all validity gates pass, call the studied version of the problem not real if every one of the four key all-node mechanisms has thresholded absolute bias below 0.01 with an upper Monte Carlo bound below 0.02, coverage from 0.925 through 0.975, and absolute nonadditivity below 0.005. This conclusion is restricted to the stated 12-month protocol and error mechanisms.
- Uninformative if: Call the study uninformative if any no-error or oracle validity gate fails, fewer than 95% of replicates yield valid estimates in a key cell, median oracle effective sample size is below 200 in either arm, or results fall between the real and not-real thresholds. If correction succeeds only under the congruent core model and fails all four misspecification stresses, conclude that correction performance is model-dependent rather than that the open problem is resolved.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A DGM constructed from the correction model can guarantee favorable correction performance. *Mitigation: The primary claim about thresholding uses naive bias and does not require correction success. Four stress scenarios add an omitted nonlinear treatment-effect modifier. Exact no-error controls test for manufactured effects. One-node and joint-error scenarios permit decomposition rather than selection of the most favorable cell.*
- Independent monthly treatment errors would almost guarantee artificial censoring under a sustained strategy and make the result tautological. *Mitigation: Treatment and confounder errors have persistence 0.80. The protocol reports observed and oracle adherence, effective sample size, and weight tails. The decision is invalid if oracle support is inadequate.*
- Prescribing, dispensing, and ingestion are different constructs rather than automatically misclassified versions of one variable. *Mitigation: The target protocol explicitly defines treatment as the latent clinical treatment state. Astar is declared to be a proxy for that state. No conclusion is made for an estimand that intentionally defines treatment by the recorded proxy.*
- A simple random internal validation sample with a perfect gold standard is more favorable than convenience validation from another site. *Mitigation: Validation selection and perfect truth are explicit DGM assumptions. The study makes no transport claim and classifies MER-01 as only partly answered.*
- Symmetric sensitivity and specificity and differential error only through X2 omit many realistic error structures. *Mitigation: Report stratum-specific operating characteristics and subgroup effects. Limit conclusions to the simulated mechanisms; do not generalize to asymmetric, treatment-dependent, outcome-dependent, or cross-node-correlated errors.*
- The latent-state correction depends on weak priors and a Laplace approximation, especially with validation n=100 and accuracy 0.99. *Mitigation: Use proper fixed priors to avoid separation, report posterior error-rate summaries, and repeat the n=100 boundary cells with coefficient prior standard deviations doubled to 5 and 10. Treat a change greater than 0.005 in RD bias or 0.02 in coverage as prior sensitivity.*
- Weight instability could be mistaken for phenotype bias. *Mitigation: Use an oracle estimator with the identical weighting procedure, no weight truncation, prespecified effective-sample-size gates, and bias-eliminated coverage. Compare thresholded and oracle estimates within the same replicate.*
- Fixed monthly visits, no loss to follow-up, and a terminal binary outcome make the emulation cleaner than routine EHR studies. *Mitigation: These restrictions are stated as scope boundaries. The result cannot be used as evidence about irregular visits, observation-process bias, competing risks, or informative dropout.*
- A finite Monte Carlo truth can be mistaken for estimator bias. *Mitigation: Use 4000000 participants per arm for each of only two distinct causal truths, pair intervention simulations, report truth Monte Carlo SEs, and keep truth random streams separate from replicate streams.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The design has 42 scenario cells and 500 replicates, for 21000 replicate-cell analyses. The correction performs 20 completed-data analyses per cell, giving 420000 imputed analyses. With two treatment glm fits per completed analysis, five small penalized latent-process fits per replicate cell, and cached naive and oracle fits across nested validation sizes, the run requires approximately 1000000 logistic fits plus finite-state recursions. There are zero ltmle fits and zero SuperLearner fits. Using six workers, vectorized 16-state recursions, and cached latent cohorts, the expected wall time is 7 to 11 hours. The two 4000000-per-arm truth evaluations should cost about 16 minutes at the supplied benchmark. A 20-replicate-per-cell timing run must be completed before the blinded full run to verify memory use and extrapolated wall time; it does not permit changing scenarios after performance results are viewed.

**Also bears on.** MER-01 (answers-part)

Epidemiology. 2020.; Edwards JK et al. Outcome misclassification sensitivity analysis within the parametric g-formula. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2018.; Cole SR, Hernan MA. ## 11. MIS-01: When informative biomarker observation changes a sustained-treatment target trial estimate

*Catalog entry: The visit process is treated as a nuisance to be imputed rather than as part of the causal structure*

**Question.** At 24 months, by how many risk-difference percentage points do complete-record and last-observation-carried-forward analyses miss the effect of sustained treatment when the conditional odds ratio linking current unrecorded severity to observation increases from 1 to 2 or 4, under monthly reference observation probabilities of 0.30 or 0.08 and treatment-to-observation odds ratios of 1 or 2? Which visit-aware estimators retain 95% coverage, and can observable visit-model and weight diagnostics detect replicate-level absolute errors exceeding 0.02?

**Aims.** The primary aim is to estimate unconditional 95% confidence-interval coverage for the 24-month marginal risk difference produced by each method in each scenario. Secondary aims are to estimate absolute bias and its paired change from the noninformative-observation scenario, determine whether any visit-aware method keeps absolute bias at or below 0.01 with coverage between 0.925 and 0.975, quantify the bias-variance cost of raw, capped, and calibrated inverse-intensity weights, and measure whether observable diagnostics classify absolute risk-difference errors above 0.02 with area under the ROC curve of at least 0.80.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate 4000 independent individuals and 24 monthly cycles. Let B indicate age 65 years or older, with B distributed Bernoulli(0.45). Let F indicate female sex, with F distributed Bernoulli(0.52). Generate baseline comorbidity C from Bernoulli(expit(-2.00 + 0.80B + 0.30F)). Generate the true binary prognostic severity state L0 from Bernoulli(expit(-1.20 + 0.55B + 0.80C - 0.15F)). Everyone is event-free and untreated immediately before baseline, A[-1]=0. Baseline observation is required by eligibility, so R0=1 and L0 is recorded. For months t=1,...,23 among event-free individuals, generate Lt before observation, treatment, and outcome. In the primary first-order mechanism, Lt is Bernoulli with logit probability -1.65 + 2.10L[t-1] + 0.45B + 0.70C - 0.55A[t-1] + 0.10(t/24). In the second-order challenge mechanism, use -1.80 + 1.25L[t-1] + 0.75L[t-2] + 0.45B + 0.70C - 0.55A[t-1] + 0.10(t/24) + 0.35L[t-1]C, with L[-1]=L0. Generate the structured observation indicator Rt from Bernoulli with logit probability alpha + gamma_L Lt + gamma_A A[t-1] + 0.405R[t-1] + 0.30C. Record Lt exactly when Rt=1 and record it as missing otherwise. Generate treatment from Bernoulli with logit probability -1.15 + 1.60A[t-1] + 0.95Lt + 0.35C + 0.20B + 0.25Rt. The true state can influence treatment without a structured measurement because it represents symptoms and clinical information available in unstructured care but absent from the analytic extract. All Bernoulli draws are conditionally independent given the stated history.
- Outcome model: For each event-free individual, generate the monthly event indicator Y[t+1] from Bernoulli with logit probability -5.10 + 1.20Lt + 0.40C + 0.25B - 0.50At + 0.30AtLt. Follow-up stops after the first event. Events are completely ascertained through linkage. There is no competing event, administrative loss, or measurement error in an observed severity state.
- Treatment effect: Natural treatment is time varying and follows the stated treatment model. Under the target interventions, replace the natural treatment draw with At=1 at every month for sustained treatment or At=0 at every month for sustained no treatment. Treatment has a direct conditional log-odds effect of -0.50 when Lt=0 and also lowers the next severity-state log odds by 0.55. The target effect therefore includes the pathway through subsequent severity. The observation process is not intervened upon.
- Effect modification: The only outcome-scale treatment interaction is At by Lt with coefficient 0.30 on the monthly log-odds scale. The conditional treatment odds ratio for the event is exp(-0.50)=0.607 when Lt=0 and exp(-0.20)=0.819 when Lt=1. There are no treatment interactions with B, F, or C.
- Deliberately true: The core mechanism makes the joint first-order latent-state likelihood correctly specified. Missingness is MAR with respect to the stated recorded history when gamma_L=0 and MNAR when gamma_L is positive. Severity is a time-varying confounder and effect modifier. Treatment affects later severity and future observation. Observation itself has no direct effect on severity or outcome. Sequential treatment probabilities remain strictly between zero and one. The 12 core scenarios are a 3 by 2 by 2 factorial; the 4 second-order scenarios are prespecified robustness checks.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot evaluate continuous-time point processes, truly irregular event times within a month, continuous or multivariate biomarkers, measurement error at observed visits, informative outcome ascertainment, competing risks, loss to follow-up, treatment that can only be changed during an encounter, or a direct health effect of healthcare use. It cannot establish nonparametric identification under latent-state-dependent observation. It also cannot establish that a joint parametric model will remain reliable under forms of misspecification other than the specified second-order challenge.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Current latent-severity dependence of observation | gamma_L=0, conditional observation odds ratio 1, gamma_L=0.693147, conditional observation odds ratio 2, gamma_L=1.386294, conditional observation odds ratio 4 | The first level is a negative control in which observation is conditionally independent of current unrecorded severity given recorded history. The other levels represent moderate and strong selective measurement without claiming that marginal visit-rate heterogeneity itself proves unstable weighting. |
| Reference monthly observation probability | alpha=-0.847298, reference probability 0.30, alpha=-2.442347, reference probability 0.08 | These probabilities correspond to median geometric waiting times of approximately 2 and 9 months for the reference patient. They represent relatively dense monitoring and sparse EHR biomarker capture. |
| Prior-treatment dependence of observation | gamma_A=0, conditional observation odds ratio 1, gamma_A=0.693147, conditional observation odds ratio 2 | This distinguishes selective observation caused only by health history from monitoring that is also changed by prior treatment. |
| Latent-state dynamics | First-order model used in all 12 core factorial scenarios, Second-order challenge model used in 4 edge scenarios | The 12 core scenarios fully cross the first three factors. Four additional scenarios use the second-order mechanism at gamma_L values 0 and 1.386294, both observation-rate levels, and gamma_A=0.693147. The overall design is therefore not fully factorial. The four challenge cells test whether a joint method wins only because its first-order latent model reproduces the primary generator. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary 24-month marginal risk difference**: The superpopulation cumulative incidence by the end of month 24 under At=1 for every t=0,...,23 minus the cumulative incidence under At=0 for every t=0,...,23. Eligibility requires an observed baseline state, but subsequent observation is not part of either treatment intervention. *Truth: Enumerate the exact joint probabilities of B, F, C, and L0. Under each treatment strategy, propagate survivor probability through the binary latent-state transition and event hazard for 24 months. The second-order scenarios propagate the pair (L[t-1],Lt). Double-precision recursion provides effectively exact truth without a large Monte Carlo truth sample. Validate the implementation independently in four corner scenarios with 2000000 simulated individuals per strategy. Require each simulated risk to be within 3 Monte Carlo standard errors and 0.001 of the recursive value.*
- **Secondary 24-month marginal risk ratio**: The same superpopulation sustained-strategy contrast expressed as cumulative incidence under sustained treatment divided by cumulative incidence under sustained no treatment. *Truth: Use the two risks from the exact recursion. Analyze the logarithm of the estimated risk ratio for inference.*
- **Secondary 12-month marginal risk difference**: The sustained-treatment minus sustained-no-treatment cumulative-incidence contrast through month 12 in the same superpopulation. *Truth: Stop the same exact recursion after month 12.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Oracle full-state parametric g-formula | Use Lt at every month. Fit pooled logistic models for the severity transition and event hazard, then calculate intervention risks by exact binary-state recursion standardized over the empirical baseline distribution. In second-order scenarios, include L[t-2] and L[t-1] by C in the transition model. | Stack the subject-level scores for all fitted regressions, empirical baseline cell probabilities, and the two intervention-risk equations. Use the person-clustered M-estimation sandwich and the numerical gradient of the exact recursion. This benchmark includes nuisance-model estimation and empirical-standardization uncertainty. | no | A replicate fails if any regression does not converge, has a rank-deficient design, produces a nonfinite coefficient or covariance, has an information-matrix condition number above 1e12, or produces a nonfinite intervention risk or standard error. |
| Complete-record parametric g-formula | Fit the severity transition only on adjacent person-month pairs with R[t-1]=Rt=1. Fit the event model only on person-months with Rt=1. Use the same first-order working formulas as the primary generator and calculate risks by exact recursion. Do not model or weight the observation process. | Use a person-clustered stacked estimating-equation sandwich for the selected-row regression scores, empirical baseline distribution, and recursive risk functional. | yes | A replicate fails for regression nonconvergence, separation, rank deficiency, an information-matrix condition number above 1e12, absence of an event or state transition in a required treatment-state cell, or a nonfinite risk or standard error. |
| Last-observation-carried-forward parametric g-formula | Replace every missing Lt with the most recently recorded state, which is always available because R0=1. Treat the carried value as the current state. Fit first-order severity-transition and event models to every observed person-month, ignore the observation process, and calculate intervention risks by exact recursion through the fitted surrogate-state model. | Use a person-clustered stacked estimating-equation sandwich covering the transition model, event model, empirical baseline distribution, and recursive risk functional. | yes | Use the oracle regression criteria. Also fail a replicate if carried values create a constant response or an unidentified treatment by state interaction. |
| MAR joint-model multiple imputation g-formula | Fit a two-state observed-data likelihood with gamma_L fixed at zero. Include the baseline-state, first-order state-transition, observation, treatment, and event models. Draw 20 parameter vectors from the large-sample normal approximation and draw missing state paths by forward-filtering backward-sampling. Fit the complete-data first-order transition and event models in each completed dataset, calculate exact intervention risks, and average the 20 estimates. | Within each imputation use the complete-data stacked sandwich. Combine the within-imputation variance Ubar and between-imputation variance B as T=Ubar+(1+1/20)B. | no | A replicate fails if the constrained likelihood does not converge, its Hessian is not positive definite, forward-filtering probabilities become nonfinite, any completed-data analysis fails, or fewer than all 20 prespecified imputations are available. |
| Recorded-history inverse-intensity-weighted g-formula | Fit a pooled logistic observation model to every at-risk row using B, F, C, A[t-1], R[t-1], last recorded L, months since the last observation capped at 12, t/24, and (t/24)^2. The model cannot include current unrecorded Lt. For an observed outcome-model row use weight 1 divided by the predicted observation probability. For an adjacent observed transition pair use the inverse predicted joint observation probability, with R0 treated as probability one. Normalize weights to mean one within month. Fit weighted severity-transition and event regressions and calculate risks by exact recursion. | Stack the visit-model score, weighted transition and event scores, empirical baseline equations, and risk equations. Use a person-clustered sandwich so estimated visit weights contribute to uncertainty. | no | A replicate fails if the visit, transition, or event model fails; a predicted observation probability is numerically zero; weights or sandwich terms are nonfinite; the information matrix has condition number above 1e12; or the recursive estimate is nonfinite. |
| Capped recorded-history inverse-intensity-weighted g-formula | Use the same models and estimated observation probabilities as the raw inverse-intensity method. Normalize each analytic weight to mean one within month, cap the final outcome or transition weight at 10, renormalize, fit the weighted regressions, and calculate risks by exact recursion. | Use the same stacked sandwich with the piecewise derivative of the fixed cap. The probability of a weight falling exactly at the cap is zero under the continuous fitted probabilities. | no | Use the raw inverse-intensity criteria. Capping does not convert a numerically failed raw fit into a converged replicate. |
| Calibrated inverse-intensity-weighted g-formula | Begin with the raw inverse-intensity weights. Apply exponential-tilting calibration so the weighted observed rows reproduce full risk-set totals of 1, B, F, C, A[t-1], R[t-1], last recorded L, capped observation gap, t/24, (t/24)^2, and A[t-1] by last recorded L. Calibrate transition-pair weights to the corresponding adjacent-history totals. Fit weighted transition and event models and calculate risks by exact recursion. | Include the calibration moment equations and multipliers in the person-clustered stacked sandwich together with the visit, transition, event, baseline, and risk equations. | no | A replicate fails if the base visit model fails, the entropy-calibration solver does not reach a maximum absolute balance residual below 1e-8, calibrated weights are nonfinite, or subsequent model or variance calculations fail. |
| Recorded-history visit-process parametric g-formula | Represent the analysis state by the last recorded L and the capped number of missed months. Fit an observation model from recorded history, a new-measurement model among Rt=1 rows, and an event model using treatment, last recorded L, current observation, gap, baseline covariates, and treatment by last recorded L. Under each treatment strategy, recursively simulate or exactly sum over observation, new recorded state, carried state, gap, survival, and event. The unobserved true state is not propagated between visits. | Use a person-clustered stacked sandwich for all visit-process, measurement, event, empirical-baseline, and recursive-risk equations. | no | A replicate fails if any component regression or the stacked sandwich fails, a required visit-gap state has no support, or exact recursion produces a nonfinite probability. |
| Joint latent-state likelihood g-formula | Maximize the observed-data likelihood obtained by summing over missing Lt with a two-state forward algorithm. Jointly fit the baseline-state, first-order state-transition, current-state-dependent observation, treatment, and event models. Anchor state labels using visits at which L is observed. Standardize the fitted transition and event models over the empirical baseline distribution and calculate sustained-treatment risks by exact recursion. | Use the robust quasi-maximum-likelihood sandwich from individual likelihood scores and the observed Hessian. Add empirical baseline-distribution score equations. Apply the numerical delta method to the exact risk recursion. | no | Fit with L-BFGS-B from starting gamma_L values 0, 0.693147, and 1.386294, retaining the highest likelihood. A replicate fails if no start converges, the maximum absolute score exceeds 1e-4, the Hessian is singular or has condition number above 1e12, gamma_L lies within 0.01 of its bounds [-3,3], or any risk or standard error is nonfinite. |

**Performance.** Primary: Unconditional empirical coverage of the nominal 95% confidence interval for the 24-month risk difference in each method and scenario. A nonconverged replicate counts as failure to cover. Also report coverage among converged replicates so numerical failure and interval calibration can be distinguished.. Secondary: Bias and absolute bias of the 24-month risk difference, Empirical standard error, Mean model-based standard error, Relative error in model-based standard error, Mean squared error, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for a zero risk difference, Convergence and numerical-failure rate, Bias, coverage, and MSE for the 12-month risk difference and 24-month risk ratio, Paired change in absolute bias between gamma_L=0 and gamma_L=1.386294 under matched random-number streams, Maximum, 99th percentile, coefficient of variation, and effective sample-size fraction for visit weights, Observable visit-model Brier score, calibration intercept, and calibration slope, Simulation-only calibration conditional on the true latent state, Bias and interval coverage for the joint model's gamma_L estimate, Leave-one-scenario-out AUC and sensitivity at 0.80 specificity for detecting replicate-level absolute risk-difference error above 0.02 using log weight coefficient of variation, effective sample-size fraction, observable calibration slope, Brier score, and maximum observation gap, Elapsed time and peak memory by method. 1000 replicates. The target Monte Carlo standard error for nominal coverage is at most 0.007. At coverage 0.95, R must satisfy sqrt(0.95 times 0.05 divided by R) at most 0.007, giving R at least 970. Round to 1000 replicates, for MCSE sqrt(0.0475/1000)=0.00689, or 0.69 percentage points. Report harness-derived Monte Carlo standard errors for every performance estimate. Use common random numbers for matched gamma_L comparisons and calculate their Monte Carlo errors from replicate-level paired differences.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require the oracle gate: absolute oracle bias at most 0.005, coverage from 0.925 through 0.975, and convergence of at least 0.99 in at least 15 of 16 scenarios. Conditional on that gate, declare a practically material visit-process problem if LOCF has absolute bias at least 0.02 or coverage at most 0.90 in at least 6 of the 10 latent-informative scenarios, including at least 3 of the 8 core informative scenarios, and if moving from gamma_L=0 to gamma_L=1.386294 increases LOCF absolute bias by at least 0.01 in at least 4 of the 6 matched pairs. A visit-aware method is declared practically adequate only if it has absolute bias at most 0.01, coverage from 0.925 through 0.975, and convergence of at least 0.95 in at least 8 of 10 informative scenarios, including both second-order informative challenges. Observable diagnostics count as useful only if leave-one-scenario-out AUC is at least 0.80 and sensitivity is at least 0.70 at specificity 0.80.
- Problem is not real if: Require the oracle gate and then require both LOCF and complete-record analyses to have absolute bias below 0.01 and coverage from 0.925 through 0.975 in at least 9 of 10 informative scenarios. Also require the matched increase in absolute bias from gamma_L=0 to gamma_L=1.386294 to remain below 0.005 in at least 5 of 6 pairs. This conclusion applies only to the simulated two-year envelope and does not imply general identification under MNAR.
- Uninformative if: Classify the study as uninformative if the oracle gate fails, neither materiality rule is met, every visit-aware method has nonconvergence above 0.10 in more than four scenarios, conclusions reverse solely in the second-order challenge cells, or fewer than 900 valid generated replicates remain in any scenario because of harness or data-generation failures. A joint-model victory confined to the correctly specified first-order cells is evidence of parametric compatibility, not a general solution.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A generator constructed from the joint model can guarantee that the joint model wins. *Mitigation: Include six gamma_L=0 negative-control scenarios, compare matched changes as gamma_L increases, add four second-order state-process challenges omitted from the joint working model, and require the oracle gate. The materiality decision depends on status-quo deterioration relative to the oracle, not merely on joint-model superiority.*
- The observed data may not identify current-latent-state dependence without parametric restrictions. *Mitigation: Label the joint estimator explicitly as selection-model dependent. Report bias and coverage for gamma_L itself, Hessian condition numbers, likelihood profiles, and boundary failures. Do not interpret good performance as nonparametric identification.*
- Observable visit calibration can look satisfactory while the intensity model is conditionally wrong. *Mitigation: Report both observable calibration and simulation-only calibration conditional on true Lt. Evaluate whether observable diagnostics predict estimation error rather than treating calibration as proof of validity.*
- Weight capping or calibration can hide positivity problems while changing the target approximation. *Mitigation: Retain raw, fixed-cap, and calibrated variants as separate prespecified methods. Report their uncapped weight distributions and effective sample sizes. Do not select a variant after viewing bias.*
- The discrete monthly observation indicator is not a continuous-time visit process. *Mitigation: State that conclusions apply to gridded EHR emulations. Do not claim validation of marked point-process estimators or irregular within-month timing.*
- A binary state observed without measurement error understates real biomarker complexity. *Mitigation: Use the binary state to permit exact truth and an auditable latent likelihood. Restrict conclusions accordingly and treat continuous, noisy, and multivariate states as future studies.*
- Observation has no direct effect on health in the generator. *Mitigation: Interpret R as structured measurement rather than all healthcare contact. The study cannot determine the effect of intervening on visit schedules or ancillary care.*
- The target interventions set treatment monthly even when no structured measurement occurs. *Mitigation: Define the protocol as sustained pharmacy availability with monthly treatment assignment and no intervention on observation. Do not generalize to policies in which treatment can only start during a visit.*
- Complete outcome ascertainment removes a second informative-observation problem. *Mitigation: Use linked outcome ascertainment deliberately to isolate covariate observation. State that visit-dependent diagnosis and loss to follow-up remain untested.*
- Multiple-imputation Monte Carlo error can be mistaken for estimator variance. *Mitigation: Use 20 proper imputations with parameter and state-path draws, Rubin variance, fixed replicate-specific streams, and report the fraction of total variance due to between-imputation variation.*
- Second-order challenge results may mix observation bias with ordinary model misspecification. *Mitigation: Report the 12 core factorial scenarios separately from the 4 challenge scenarios. Require material failure in core informative scenarios before declaring the visit problem real.*
- An error in the exact recursive truth would contaminate every performance measure. *Mitigation: Validate four corner truths against independent simulations of 2000000 individuals per strategy and require agreement within both 3 Monte Carlo standard errors and 0.001.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The study contains 16000 replicate datasets, each with at most 96000 person-months. With shared visit-model fits, each replicate requires approximately 56 pooled logistic fits, two two-state likelihood optimizations, and exact finite-state recursions. This is about 896000 pooled glm fits and 32000 latent-likelihood fits. On 6 workers, the planned run is approximately 7 to 11 hours after a 20-replicate-per-scenario timing pilot. Exact truth takes seconds; four two-million-person truth checks add approximately 16 minutes using the supplied benchmark. There are zero ltmle or SuperLearner fits. The resumable harness should write one scenario-method batch at a time so a numerical failure does not require restarting the run.

**Also bears on.** MIS-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine 2019; Goldstein BA, Bhavsar NA, Phelan M, Pencina MJ. How and when informative visit processes can bias inference when using electronic health records data for clinical research. J Am Med Inform Assoc 2019; Wang et al. An operational target trial emulation framework for causal inference using electronic health records. npj Digital Medicine 2026; Multiply robust estimation of marginal structural models in observational studies subject to covariate-driven observation times. Biometrics 2024; Kalia et al. Estimation of marginal structural models under irregular visits and unmeasured confounder: calibrated inverse probability weights. BMC Medical Research Methodology 2023; Continuous-Time Causal Inference With Marked Point Process Weights. Statistics in Medicine 2025; gfoRmula: An R Package for Estimating the Effects of Sustained Treatment Strategies via the Parametric g-Formula. Patterns 2020

## 12. LRN-01: Failure awareness of flexible longitudinal estimators under practical and structural positivity violations

*Catalog entry: Flexible estimation is presented as a remedy for identification failure*

**Question.** Across 24 longitudinal target trial scenarios, what proportion of flexible estimates produce an apparently successful 95% interval that covers no more than one of three observationally indistinguishable causal risk differences, by how much do those true risk differences differ, and with what sensitivity and specificity does a prediction-level support diagnostic warn before the estimate is interpreted?

**Aims.** The primary aim is to estimate the paired false-certainty rate of a flexible longitudinal augmented inverse probability weighted estimator under structural nonpositivity. Secondary aims are to estimate the range of true 12-month risk differences compatible with the same observed-data law; compare bias, interval performance, and convergence with current-practice longitudinal IPW and a main-effects augmented estimator; and determine whether a support map using the estimator's complete conditioning feature set attains at least 0.90 sensitivity for material support violations and at least 0.90 specificity under strong overlap.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate six treatment decisions at months 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, and 10, followed by a binary outcome at month 12. For each person generate B1 from N(0,1), representing age with mean 60 and standard deviation 10 years; B2 from Bernoulli(0.50); B3 from Bernoulli(expit(-0.60 + 0.50*B1 + 0.40*B2)); L0 = 0.45*B1 + 0.55*B3 - 0.25*B2 + e0, where e0 is N(0,0.8^2); and M0 from Bernoulli(expit(-1.10 + 0.45*B3 + 0.50*L0)). Set A[-1]=0 and previous mean treatment to zero. At decision t, define etaA = -0.15 + 0.80*tanh(Lt) + 0.45*Mt + 0.35*B3 + 0.25*tanh(B1) + 1.10*(A[t-1]-0.5) - 0.30*(mean(A0,...,A[t-1])-0.5) + 0.10*t/5. For t=0, use the stated zero previous mean. Under nonlinear complexity add 0.35*sin(Lt) + 0.25*B1*tanh(Lt) - 0.20*I(Lt>0.75). Draw At from Bernoulli(pt), with pt defined by the support factor. For t<5, generate L[t+1] = 0.55*Lt + 0.20*B1 + 0.30*B3 + 0.25*Mt - 0.40*At + 0.15*(t+1)/5 + C*(0.20*sin(Lt) + 0.15*B3*I(Lt>0.5)) + e[t+1], where each innovation is N(0,0.75^2). Generate M[t+1] from Bernoulli(expit(-1.20 + 0.60*Mt + 0.40*Lt + 0.30*B3 - 0.35*At + 0.10*(t+1)/5 + C*(0.25*I(Lt>0.5) - 0.20*Lt^2/(1+Lt^2)))). Here C is zero for the main-effects law and one for the nonlinear law. All innovations and treatment uniforms are mutually independent conditional on recorded history.
- Outcome model: Let meanL, meanM, and meanA be the averages over the six visits. Generate Y from Bernoulli(expit(etaY)), where etaY is on the conditional log odds scale and equals -2.25 + 0.30*B1 + 0.20*B2 + 0.55*B3 + 0.60*L5 + 0.35*M5 + 0.20*meanL + 0.25*meanM - 0.75*meanA + 0.45*meanA*B3 + 0.25*meanA*I(B1>0) + C*(0.55*(L5^2/(1+L5^2)-0.5) + 0.35*sin(pi*meanL/2) + 0.35*M5*I(L5>0.5) - 0.30*meanA*tanh(L5)) + delta*Z. In structural scenarios define Rt=Lt+0.40*Mt+0.30*B3 and Z=I(any At=1 when Rt<-1.25)-I(any At=0 when Rt>1.25). Observed structural data always have Z=0. Consequently delta=-log(2), 0, and log(2) produce exactly the same observed-data distribution but different outcomes under forbidden counterfactual treatment histories.
- Treatment effect: The conditional direct contribution of sustained treatment versus no treatment is -0.75 on the log odds scale before effect modification. Treatment also lowers subsequent L by 0.40 and the log odds of subsequent M by 0.35, creating mediated effects and treatment-confounder feedback. The target is the resulting total marginal effect rather than the coefficient -0.75.
- Effect modification: B3 increases the sustained-treatment log odds contrast by 0.45 and B1>0 increases it by 0.25. Under nonlinear complexity there is an additional -0.30*tanh(L5) modifier. These modifiers are fixed across scenarios so that changes attributed to support are not changes in the treatment effect model.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 2 sample sizes by 2 complexity levels by 6 support or completion levels. Consistency, sequential exchangeability given the recorded history, no interference, correct temporal ordering, complete measurement, fixed visits, and absence of censoring or competing events are true by construction. The first three support regimes satisfy theoretical positivity. The structural regimes violate positivity for the sustained strategies. Within each sample-size and complexity cell, the three structural completions use the same seed and therefore the exact same observed dataset and fitted estimates. Only their counterfactual truths differ.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot assess unmeasured confounding, violations of consistency, interference, measurement error, censoring, competing risks, irregular observation, continuous or multiple treatments, grace periods, or transportability. No observed-data diagnostic can distinguish the three structural outcome completions or certify sequential exchangeability. Results apply directly to the specified series learner and support map, not to every neural network, BART implementation, or flexible estimator.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Sample size | n=1000, n=4000 | The two levels distinguish ordinary finite-sample sparsity from the empirically benchmarked 4000-person workload. Sample size does not change the superpopulation truth. |
| Observed-law complexity | C=0: main-effects treatment, covariate, and outcome laws, C=1: bounded nonlinearities and interactions in all three laws | The first level permits conventional modeling to work when support exists. The second gives flexible modeling a legitimate approximation-error advantage, preventing the study from treating flexibility as intrinsically harmful. |
| Support and structural completion | Strong overlap: pt=expit(0.5*etaA), delta=0, Moderate overlap: pt=expit(etaA), delta=0, Severe practical nonoverlap: pt=expit(2.5*etaA), delta=0, Structural protective completion: pt=0 when Rt<-1.25, pt=1 when Rt>1.25, otherwise pt=expit(etaA); delta=-log(2), Structural benign completion: the same structural treatment law; delta=0, Structural harmful completion: the same structural treatment law; delta=log(2) | The soft regimes span comfortable overlap, probabilities near common 0.025 diagnostic boundaries, and practically empty conditional treatment regions. The deterministic tails represent eligibility or contraindication rules. The three symmetric structural completions are not claimed to be applied effect sizes. They expose nonidentification without selecting only a completion under which extrapolation fails. Poor overlap defeating advanced adjustment is the applied phenomenon documented by the cited nonmutually exclusive assignment study. |

**Estimands.**

- **12-month sustained-strategy risk difference**: The superpopulation marginal risk difference E[Y under A0=...=A5=1] minus E[Y under A0=...=A5=0] among everyone eligible at month 0. The protocol has no grace period, censoring, treatment discontinuation, or competing event. This is a per-protocol contrast between sustained strategies, not a conditional regression coefficient or realized-sample estimand. *Truth: For each unique complexity and support mechanism, simulate 2000000 superpopulation trajectories under each strategy using common baseline variables and common innovations. Average expit(etaY) rather than drawing Y. Estimate Monte Carlo error from 100 batches of 20000 trajectories and extend in blocks of 1000000 if the batch standard error of the risk difference exceeds 0.0005. This error is at most 1 percent of the prespecified 0.05 material-difference threshold. Sample-size levels share one truth. Structural delta levels reuse identical intervention paths, so eight unique trajectory simulations produce all required truths.*
- **Observed-law nonidentification span**: Within each structural sample-size and complexity cell, the maximum minus minimum true 12-month risk difference across delta=-log(2), 0, and log(2). All three values correspond to the same observed-data law. *Truth: Compute all three outcome probabilities on the same 2000000 counterfactual trajectories. Common random numbers make the span's Monte Carlo error smaller than calculating three independent truths.*
- **Strategy-specific unsupported mass**: For each strategy and visit, the probability under that strategy's history distribution that the required action has true conditional probability zero. Also compute the corresponding mass below 0.025 for practical nonoverlap. Take the maximum across visits as the scenario summary. *Truth: Calculate the indicators directly during the large-sample intervention simulation. A structural violation is materially present when zero-probability mass is at least 0.05. A practical support problem is severe when mass below 0.025 is at least 0.05.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Truncated longitudinal IPW | Fit one logistic treatment model at each visit using 1, t/5, B1, B2, B3, tanh(B1), Lt, tanh(Lt), Mt, A[t-1], previous mean A, previous mean L, and previous mean M. For each sustained strategy, retain people following that strategy through month 10, calculate the product inverse probability weight, cap final weights at the strategy-specific 99th percentile, and estimate risk by the Hajek weighted mean. Subtract never-treated risk from always-treated risk. | Use a person-level stacked M-estimation sandwich containing all six logistic score equations and both capped Hajek mean equations. Recompute the cap under coefficient perturbations. Form A by centered numerical differentiation with step 1e-6*max(1,absolute parameter value), B from outer products of person-level stacked scores, and covariance A inverse B A inverse transpose divided by n. | yes | Any treatment GLM with converged=FALSE or nonfinite coefficients; no complete followers of either strategy; a zero weighted denominator; a nonfinite estimate or standard error; or a sandwich Jacobian with condition number above 1e12. Extreme weights and low effective sample size are warnings, not nonconvergence. |
| Main-effects longitudinal AIPW | Use the six full-sample treatment models from longitudinal IPW. For each strategy a, set Q6=Y. For t from 5 down to 0, fit a quasibinomial logit regression of Q[t+1] on the stated main history features, At, At*B3, At*I(B1>0), and At*tanh(Lt), then predict Q[t] after setting At=a. Truncate fitted action probabilities to [0.01,0.99] only inside the estimating equation. For each a calculate phi_i(a)=Q_i0(a)+sum over t of I(A_i0=...=A_it=a)/G_it(a) times (Q_i,t+1(a)-Q_it(a)), where G_it is the cumulative fitted probability of the regime. Estimate each risk by mean(phi_i(a)) and subtract risks. | Use the empirical variance of (phi_i(1)-estimated risk1)-(phi_i(0)-estimated risk0), divided by n. This is the longitudinal efficient-influence-function variance estimate for the risk difference. | no | Any treatment or Q regression with converged=FALSE, aliased required coefficients, nonfinite predictions, a nonfinite estimating-function contribution, or a nonfinite point estimate or standard error. Estimates outside the logical risk range are retained and flagged rather than silently clipped. |
| Cross-fitted flexible series AIPW with conventional diagnostics | Use two prespecified folds balanced on B3 and final Y. At visit t, the rich history map contains every recorded B, L0 through Lt, M0 through Mt, and A0 through A[t-1]; current values and previous means; natural cubic spline bases with 4 degrees of freedom for B1 and every recorded L; and products of the current L spline basis with B3, Mt, A[t-1], and previous mean A. Q regressions additionally contain At interactions with B3, I(B1>0), Mt, and the current L spline basis. Internal knots are the training-fold quartiles and boundary knots are the training-fold minima and maxima. Center and scale columns in the training fold, drop zero-variance columns, and remove exact aliases by pivoted QR. Fit fold-specific logistic treatment models and the same backward AIPW recursion as above. Combine validation-fold influence contributions and truncate action probabilities to [0.01,0.99] only inside the estimating equation. | Use the empirical variance of the cross-fitted risk-difference influence contributions divided by n. | no | Any fold-specific GLM with converged=FALSE, a rank-reduced model that cannot produce every required prediction, nonfinite coefficients or predictions, nonfinite influence contributions, or a nonfinite estimate or standard error. Complete or quasi-complete separation warnings are retained as conventional warnings even when glm reports convergence. |
| Cross-fitted flexible series AIPW with prediction-level support map | Use exactly the same point estimate as the flexible series AIPW method. The added component changes reporting, not the estimate. | Use exactly the same cross-fitted influence-function variance estimate. | no | The estimator has the same numerical nonconvergence definition as flexible series AIPW. A red support result is a substantive warning and is not mislabeled as numerical nonconvergence. |

**Performance.** Primary: Paired false-certainty rate for flexible series AIPW in each structural sample-size by complexity cell. A replicate is a false-certainty event when the estimator converges, its 95 percent interval width is at most 0.10, all conventional diagnostics are green, its heldout losses satisfy the stated 0.01 criterion, and the one interval covers no more than one of the three true risk differences attached to that identical observed dataset.. Secondary: Bias with Monte Carlo standard error for every method, scenario, and structural completion, Empirical standard error, Mean model standard error, Relative error in model standard error, Mean squared error, 95 percent coverage, Bias-eliminated coverage, Two-sided rejection rate for a zero risk difference, Convergence and warning rates, Median and 90th percentile interval width, Observed-law nonidentification span, Sensitivity for true zero-probability mass of at least 0.05, Sensitivity for true below-0.025 mass of at least 0.05, Specificity when true below-0.025 mass is at most 0.005, False red-warning rate under strong overlap, Paired reduction in false-certainty rate after adding the support map, Treatment and terminal-outcome heldout log loss, Effective sample size, maximum weight, and probability-truncation frequency. 1000 replicates. The primary measure is a proportion. Its worst-case Monte Carlo standard error occurs at probability 0.50 and equals sqrt(0.50*0.50/R). Requiring MCSE no greater than 0.016 gives R at least 0.25/0.016^2=976.5625, so use 1000 replicates. The worst-case MCSE is sqrt(0.25/1000)=0.01581. Report empirical paired MCSEs for differences between reporting policies.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require a structural nonidentification span of at least 0.05 and true zero-probability mass of at least 0.05. The practical failure-awareness problem is demonstrated if the conventional flexible workflow has a paired false-certainty rate of at least 0.50 in at least two of the four structural sample-size by complexity cells, including at least one n=4000 cell. The proposed diagnostic is considered successful if its structural sensitivity is at least 0.90, its strong-overlap specificity is at least 0.90, and it lowers paired false certainty by at least 0.40 without changing point estimates.
- Problem is not real if: Conditional on a nonidentification span of at least 0.05, the studied practical problem is not supported if the conventional flexible workflow already warns or fails in at least 0.90 of replicates, false certainty is at most 0.10 in every structural cell, and the prediction-level map improves warning sensitivity by less than 0.10 while maintaining at least 0.90 specificity.
- Uninformative if: Treat the study as uninformative for a binary catalog verdict if the structural truth span is below 0.05, zero-probability mass is below 0.05, flexible-method convergence under strong overlap at n=4000 is below 0.90, the map's false red-warning rate under strong overlap exceeds 0.10, achieved primary MCSE exceeds 0.02, or the false-certainty rate lies between 0.10 and 0.50 without meeting either directional rule. Do not retune delta, support thresholds, or the diagnostic after seeing these results.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- A DGM constructed to violate positivity can make extrapolation look harmful by construction. *Mitigation: Use three exactly observationally equivalent structural completions: one in which the smooth observed-support continuation is correct and two symmetric alternatives. Include strong-overlap scenarios in which flexibility can improve approximation. Freeze delta values before fitting and declare a truth span below 0.05 uninformative rather than increasing delta.*
- The structural completion proves logical nonidentification but cannot estimate how often such ambiguity occurs in applications. *Mitigation: Interpret the span as a demonstration of compatible truths, not a prevalence estimate. Report practical soft-overlap scenarios separately and avoid population-frequency claims.*
- Deterministic treatment tails may be easier to detect than realistic sparse support. *Mitigation: Include strong, moderate, and severe nonzero propensity regimes. Score the diagnostic against both exact zero probability and the prespecified 0.025 practical threshold.*
- The proposed diagnostic could appear successful because its definition resembles the DGM's support rule. *Mitigation: The diagnostic does not receive Rt, true propensity, structural labels, or DGM coefficients. It uses only the estimator's rich feature matrix, estimated treatment probabilities, and action-specific leverage. Thresholds are fixed before simulation.*
- Flexible series regression is not representative of every flexible learner. *Mitigation: State the scope explicitly. The series library supplies nonlinear approximation and confident smooth extrapolation using installed software, but the results do not rank neural networks, BART, or all machine-learning libraries.*
- Poor flexible-model performance could be caused by numerical instability rather than identification failure. *Mitigation: Separate nonconvergence, conventional warnings, support warnings, bias, and false certainty. Require at least 0.90 convergence in the strong-overlap n=4000 scenarios before drawing the main conclusion.*
- Weight truncation and approximate variance calculations could confound point-estimation and interval conclusions. *Mitigation: Prespecify all truncation. Evaluate model SE relative error and bias-eliminated coverage. Use a stacked sandwich for IPW and influence-function variance for both augmented estimators.*
- Monte Carlo error in the truth could be mistaken for estimator bias. *Mitigation: Use two million intervention trajectories, conditional outcome probabilities, common random numbers, batch standard errors, and an automatic extension rule until truth MCSE is at most 0.0005.*
- Reusing structural datasets creates dependence across reported scenarios. *Mitigation: This dependence is intentional and necessary to demonstrate observational equivalence. Analyze completion contrasts as paired quantities and calculate paired Monte Carlo standard errors.*
- Fixed bimonthly visits simplify the conditioning histories. *Mitigation: Record this as a design boundary. The feature dimension still grows with complete history, but the study says nothing about irregular visit processes or informative measurement times.*
- A support warning could be misinterpreted as proof that all identification assumptions hold when it is green. *Mitigation: Label green as no detected support failure under the stated feature map. Never label it identified. The protocol explicitly excludes diagnostics for unmeasured confounding, consistency, and interference.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The design creates 24000 scenario-replicate records. The three structural completions reuse fitted results, leaving 16000 distinct observed datasets. Each distinct dataset requires 6 shared main treatment GLMs, 12 main-effects Q regressions, and 36 rich two-fold fits consisting of 12 treatment and 24 Q regressions, for 54 fits per dataset and 864000 GLM fits total. The support-map reporting policy reuses these fits. There are zero ltmle fits, zero SuperLearner fits, and no requirement for lmtp, concrete, CICI, Stan, or cmdstanr. Eight unique two-million-person truth simulations should cost about 32 minutes at the supplied benchmark. With six outer workers, one BLAS thread per worker, and checkpointing through the existing harness, the replicate run is expected to take approximately 6 to 12 hours and remain within the overnight budget.

**Also bears on.** LRN-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Feuerriegel S, Frauen D, Melnychuk V, et al. Causal machine learning for predicting treatment outcomes. Nature Medicine. 2024;30:958-968.; Target trial emulation under nonmutually exclusive assignment. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2026;195(4):1045.; Hill JL, Su YS. Assessing lack of common support in causal inference using Bayesian nonparametrics: implications for evaluating the effect of breastfeeding on children's cognitive outcomes. Annals of Applied Statistics. 2013;7:1386-1420.; Evaluating bias in target trial emulation for heart failure across statistical and deep learning methods. Nature Communications. 2026.; Estimate time-varying exposure effects via ensemble learning-based marginal structural model. Biometrical Journal. 2026.
