## 13. OUT-01: Quantifying Estimand Error When Death Is Silently Censored

*Catalog entry: Censoring at death is applied as data cleaning rather than as an identifying assumption*

**Question.** Across 24 scenarios with approximately 5%, 20%, or 40% five-year competing mortality, by how many percentage points does the marginal 60-month treatment risk difference change when death is censored and excluded from the risk calculation rather than retained in a protocol-defined cumulative incidence, and how often does the resulting 95% confidence interval have coverage at or below 90% for the intended total effect while retaining nominal coverage for its actual hypothetical no-death target?

**Aims.** Estimate the true difference between the total-effect cumulative-incidence risk difference and the hypothetical no-death risk difference at 60 months; estimate the bias, coverage loss, and sign changes produced when a death-censored analysis is interpreted as estimating the total effect; verify whether the same analysis remains statistically well behaved for the no-death estimand it can identify under the deliberately favorable assumptions; and determine how these quantities change with competing mortality, treatment effects on death, treatment effects on the primary event, and shared prognostic causes.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains 4000 independently sampled, eligible, event-free individuals at time zero. Generate Z from N(0,1), representing age in decades above 65; M from Bernoulli(0.50); C from Bernoulli(expit(-0.40 + 0.35 Z + 0.25 M)); S = 0.30 Z + 0.50 C + epsilon, with epsilon from N(0,0.64); and the observed shared prognostic score Q from N(0,1). Assign observed baseline treatment A from Bernoulli(expit(-0.20 + 0.25 Z + 0.20 M + 0.50 C + 0.35 S + 0.20 Q)). Treatment then remains fixed through 60 months. All five variables are available to every estimator. This creates measured baseline confounding with practical overlap while preserving conditional exchangeability. Follow-up is exact and continuous, with administrative censoring only at 60 months.
- Outcome model: Generate a latent time to the first nonfatal primary event and a latent time to death using conditionally independent piecewise-exponential clocks. The observed time is their minimum or 60 months, and status is 1 for the primary event, 2 for death first, and 0 for administrative censoring. Primary-event baseline hazards per month are 0.0025 from 0 through 12 months, 0.0040 after 12 through 36 months, and 0.0060 after 36 through 60 months. Its cause-specific hazard is lambda_Y0(t) times exp(0.20 Z + 0.10 M + 0.45 C + 0.30 S + gamma Q + beta_YA A + beta_YAC A C). Death baseline hazards are selected by the mortality factor. Its cause-specific hazard is lambda_D0(t) times exp(0.45 Z + 0.15 M + 0.65 C + 0.35 S + gamma Q + beta_DA A). For each cause, draw an independent unit exponential threshold and invert its piecewise cumulative hazard. The reference individual's primary-event risk without competing death is 1 - exp(-0.27) = 0.237 over 60 months.
- Treatment effect: For the primary-event factor, the null level sets beta_YA = 0 and beta_YAC = 0. The heterogeneous protective level sets beta_YA = -0.510826 and beta_YAC = 0.405465, producing primary-event hazard ratios of 0.60 when C = 0 and 0.90 when C = 1. For the death-effect factor, the neutral level sets beta_DA = 0 and the protective level sets beta_DA = -0.510826, producing a death hazard ratio of 0.60. Treatment assignment is observational, but the counterfactual strategies intervene on A and hold it fixed.
- Effect modification: C modifies the treatment effect on the primary event only. Under the protective level, the cause-specific hazard ratio is 0.60 without C and 0.90 with C. There is no treatment by covariate interaction for death. Q has the same prognostic loading gamma on both causes and therefore induces marginal dependence between event and death, but Q is observed and included in both fitted models.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 by 2 by 2 by 2 equals 24 scenarios. Cause-specific proportional-hazards models are correctly specified; every treatment confounder and shared cause is measured; positivity holds structurally; latent event and death times are independent conditional on measured history; treatment is fixed; there is no loss to follow-up; and a mathematical intervention setting the death hazard to zero is defined. Consequently, the death-censored method should estimate the hypothetical no-death risk correctly. Any discrepancy from the total cumulative-incidence effect can therefore be attributed to the target question rather than poor fitting. Six scenarios in which both treatment-effect factors are null are exact controls where both causal risk differences equal zero.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot estimate how often applied emulations censor death silently. It cannot address treatment discontinuation, time-varying treatment, time-varying confounding, irregular or interval-censored observation, dependent censoring after conditioning, unmeasured common causes, recurrent outcomes, joint recurrent-terminal estimands, separable treatment components, multiple competing causes, model misspecification, or whether eliminating death is a scientifically meaningful intervention. It studies a baseline-assigned sustained strategy and the first primary event only.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Competing mortality burden | Low: death hazards 0.0005, 0.0008, and 0.0012 per month across the three intervals, Moderate: death hazards 0.0018, 0.0030, and 0.0045 per month, High: death hazards 0.0040, 0.0070, and 0.0100 per month | For the reference individual without a primary event, these imply five-year death risks of approximately 5.3%, 18.3%, and 36.6%. Covariate heterogeneity changes the marginal values, which will be reported from the truth calculation. The levels span low-risk prevention, chronic disease, and older or oncology populations without relying on one extreme setting. |
| Treatment effect on death | Neutral: beta_DA = 0, hazard ratio 1.00, Protective: beta_DA = -0.510826, hazard ratio 0.60 | The neutral level tests whether competing mortality alone can separate marginal absolute effects. The protective level represents a large but credible boundary at which survival-mediated changes in primary-event opportunity should be visible. |
| Treatment effect on the primary event | Null: beta_YA = 0 and beta_YAC = 0, Heterogeneous protective: hazard ratio 0.60 when C = 0 and 0.90 when C = 1 | The null level supplies exact causal null controls. The heterogeneous level prevents conclusions from depending on a homogeneous conditional effect and produces a clinically material alternative. |
| Strength of shared prognosis | None: gamma = 0, Strong and observed: gamma = 0.693147, hazard ratio 2.00 per standard deviation of Q for both causes | This changes marginal dependence between death and the primary event while preserving conditional identification because Q is measured. It tests whether a death-censored result remains misleading even under assumptions favorable to its hypothetical interpretation. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary 60-month total-effect cumulative-incidence risk difference**: In the target superpopulation, set A = 1 for everyone at eligibility and hold it through 60 months, then repeat with A = 0. Under each strategy, death follows its natural treatment-specific law and precludes the primary event. The estimand is Pr(T_Y under A = 1 is at most 60 and precedes T_D under A = 1) minus Pr(T_Y under A = 0 is at most 60 and precedes T_D under A = 0). The scale is a marginal absolute risk difference. The same contrast at 12 and 36 months is secondary. *Truth: Draw 2000000 independent superpopulation covariate profiles for each scenario and use common profiles for both strategies. Within each piecewise interval, calculate the conditional primary-event increment as survival at the interval start times lambda_Y divided by lambda_Y plus lambda_D times 1 - exp(-(lambda_Y + lambda_D) times interval length). Sum increments and average profile-specific risks. Estimate truth MCSE from 200 batches of 10000 profiles. The required MCSE is at most 0.0005, one twentieth of the smallest materiality threshold. If it is larger, add independent batches of 2000000 profiles until the requirement is met.*
- **Hypothetical no-death 60-month net-risk difference**: For the same superpopulation and sustained treatment strategies, intervene additionally to set the death hazard to zero throughout follow-up while leaving the primary-event structural hazard unchanged. The estimand is Pr(T_Y under A = 1 and death eliminated is at most 60) minus the corresponding probability under A = 0. It is a mathematical simulation benchmark, not an assertion that death can be eliminated clinically. *Truth: For each of the same 2000000 profiles, set lambda_D to zero and compute 1 - exp(-sum over intervals of lambda_Y times interval length) under each treatment. Average the paired profile-specific differences and apply the same batch-MCSE requirement.*
- **Estimand displacement**: The hypothetical no-death risk difference minus the total-effect cumulative-incidence risk difference at 60 months, expressed in percentage points. Its absolute value and whether it changes the sign of the treatment contrast are prespecified scenario-level outputs. *Truth: Subtract the two paired Monte Carlo truths. Calculate its MCSE directly from batch-level paired differences rather than adding two independent MCSEs.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Death-censored cause-specific Cox standardization | Fit a Cox model for the primary event using Surv(time, status = 1), so deaths enter as censoring times. Include A, Z, M, C, S, Q, and A by C; use an unspecified Breslow baseline hazard. For each individual, set A first to 1 and then to 0, predict the primary-event survival curve, calculate 1 minus survival at 12, 36, and 60 months, average over the realized baseline covariate distribution, and subtract risks. The calculation contains no death hazard. Evaluate the same estimate against the intended total-effect truth and against its actual hypothetical no-death truth. | Use the full subject-level influence-function decomposition for the Cox coefficients, Breslow baseline hazard, and empirical standardization distribution. Calculate the risk-difference influence value jointly from both treatment predictions and estimate variance as the empirical variance of those values divided by 4000. Use the iid prediction machinery in riskRegression 2026.3.11; retain the individual contributions for independent verification. | yes | Count failure if the Cox fit errors; reports an infinite coefficient; has a singular or non-finite covariance matrix; has an information-matrix condition number above 1e10; produces a non-finite cumulative baseline hazard, risk, standard error, or influence contribution; or has no primary events. A warning and its reason are retained even if the estimate remains usable. |
| Protocol-aligned multistate cause-specific Cox g-computation | Fit cause-specific Cox models for both the primary event and death. Both models include A, Z, M, C, S, Q, and A by C; the extra death interaction has true coefficient zero. For each individual and treatment strategy, combine the two predicted cause-specific cumulative hazards into the primary-event cumulative incidence by summing survival immediately before each event time times the primary-event hazard increment. Average individual cumulative incidences at 12, 36, and 60 months and subtract A = 0 from A = 1. Death remains a competing event and follows its treatment-specific law. | Use the joint subject-level influence-function decomposition supplied by the two cause-specific Cox fits and empirical standardization. Stack contributions from both causes so their within-subject covariance is retained. Estimate risk-difference variance as the empirical variance of the final subject-level influence values divided by 4000. | no | Count failure if either cause-specific model meets any Cox failure criterion; either cause has no events; the cumulative-incidence recursion produces a risk outside zero to one by more than 1e-10; the two cause probabilities sum above one by more than 1e-10; or any estimate, variance, or influence contribution is non-finite. |

**Performance.** Primary: Scenario-specific empirical coverage of the nominal 95% interval from the death-censored method when assessed against the intended total-effect 60-month risk difference.. Secondary: Bias against the total-effect truth for both methods, Bias of the death-censored method against the hypothetical no-death truth, Empirical standard error, Mean model standard error, Relative error in model standard error, Mean squared error, Coverage, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate, interpreted as type I error in the six exact-null scenarios and power elsewhere, Convergence proportion and warning frequency, True estimand displacement in percentage points, Proportion of replicates with the wrong treatment-effect sign, Factor-specific means and maxima of absolute displacement, Results at 12 and 36 months as secondary time-course checks. 4000 replicates. The coverage decision boundary is 0.90, and the target Monte Carlo standard error there is at most 0.005. For a binomial coverage indicator, R must satisfy sqrt(0.90 times 0.10 divided by R) at most 0.005, so R must be at least 3600. Using 4000 replicates gives MCSE 0.00474 at coverage 0.90 and 0.00345 at coverage 0.95. All other Monte Carlo standard errors are calculated by the existing ADEMP harness.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require calibration: the multistate method must have absolute bias at most 0.005, coverage from 0.93 through 0.97, relative model-SE error within 10%, and convergence at least 0.98 in at least 22 of 24 scenarios; the death-censored method must meet the same criteria against its hypothetical no-death truth. After calibration, classify the problem as real if at least 6 of the 18 active-effect scenarios have true absolute estimand displacement of at least 0.020, death-censored absolute bias against the total effect of at least 0.020, and total-effect coverage at or below 0.90. At least two qualifying scenarios must have moderate rather than high mortality, and at least one must have a neutral treatment effect on death.
- Problem is not real if: Classify the problem as not materially consequential within this design if both methods satisfy their own-target calibration criteria, every active-effect scenario has true absolute estimand displacement below 0.010 and death-censored absolute bias against the total effect below 0.010, total-effect coverage is at least 0.93 in at least 17 of 18 active scenarios, and no scenario has a sign reversal.
- Uninformative if: Classify the study as uninformative if results fall between the two rules or if calibration fails. Calibration failure includes convergence below 0.95 in any method-scenario cell, multistate absolute bias above 0.005 in more than two scenarios, death-censored failure against its own no-death target in more than two scenarios, type I rejection above 0.07 in more than one of the six exact-null scenarios, unresolved disagreement above 5% between influence-function and bootstrap standard errors, or truth MCSE above 0.0005.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- Different estimands are algebraically capable of differing, so large mortality and large treatment effects on death could make the desired finding inevitable. *Mitigation: Use the complete factorial grid, publish every truth before estimator results, include six exact causal-null scenarios, include low mortality and neutral death-effect cells, and require material discrepancies in moderate-mortality and neutral death-effect scenarios. The conclusion concerns magnitude and frequency over the declared grid, not the mere existence of two definitions.*
- The DGM and fitted Cox models are congenial, which can make the protocol-aligned method look unrealistically good. *Mitigation: State this as a deliberate mechanism experiment. Calibration of both methods to their own targets is required before interpreting differences. Do not claim robustness to misspecification, flexible nuisance estimation, or machine learning.*
- Conditional independence and complete measurement make the hypothetical no-death estimand unusually identifiable. *Mitigation: Treat this as a conservative choice favorable to death censoring. Label the no-death intervention as hypothetical and do not generalize its identification to settings with latent shared causes or ill-defined death prevention.*
- The observational treatment mechanism may create sparse support and make an estimand problem look like extrapolation. *Mitigation: Record treatment prevalence and covariate-specific fitted treatment probabilities in every scenario. Flag a replicate if either treatment arm has fewer than 500 individuals or if more than 1% of individuals have true treatment probabilities below 0.05 or above 0.95. Summarize flagged replicates separately.*
- A variance implementation error could be mistaken for coverage failure. *Mitigation: Validate influence-function standard errors against 1000-resample subject bootstraps in three fixed datasets, retain individual influence contributions, and use bias-eliminated coverage to separate interval-scale problems from estimand bias.*
- Monte Carlo error in the truth could be counted as estimator bias. *Mitigation: Use paired profile-level truth calculations with 2000000 profiles, batch MCSEs, a required MCSE of 0.0005, and adaptive doubling if that threshold is missed.*
- Exact event times and a fixed baseline treatment remove several difficulties present in longitudinal emulations. *Mitigation: Restrict the conclusion to baseline-assigned sustained strategies with exact first-event data. Explicitly classify treatment switching, recurrent events, irregular observation, and time-varying confounding as unanswered.*
- A five-year absolute difference of 0.020 may not be clinically important in every application. *Mitigation: Report continuous displacement values with Monte Carlo uncertainty in addition to the threshold classification. Present 0.010 and 0.020 reference lines and leave application-specific importance to the catalog reader.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The production grid contains 24 times 4000 = 96000 replicate datasets and two reused cause-specific Cox fits per replicate, totaling 192000 Cox fits. There is no nested bootstrap in production and no longitudinal TMLE or SuperLearner fit. With six workers, checkpointing through studies/_shared/R/harness.R after each 100 replicates, the replicate stage should take approximately 6 to 10 hours. The 24 truth calculations contain 48000000 covariate profiles but use vectorized closed-form piecewise-hazard calculations; allow approximately 1 to 2 additional hours. The three one-time variance validation bootstraps should take under one hour. The full run is expected to finish overnight with scenarios processed sequentially to control memory.

**Also bears on.** OUT-01 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods; Young JG, Stensrud MJ, Tchetgen Tchetgen EJ, Hernan MA. A causal framework for classical statistical estimands in failure-time settings with competing events; ICH E9(R1) addendum on estimands and sensitivity analysis in clinical trials; Lin V, McGrath S, Zhang Z, et al. gfoRmula: An R Package for Estimating the Effects of Sustained Treatment Strategies via the Parametric g-formula

## 14. DTA-02: When federated target trial estimates answer different questions: an ADEMP study of semantic drift, explicit transport, aggregate alarms, and local validation gates

*Catalog entry: Averaging site estimates in a federated emulation can conceal that the sites emulated different trials*

**Question.** Across six-site federated emulations in which two sites use one of five incompatible eligibility, exposure, or outcome mappings, how many percentage points does the estimated marginal five-year risk difference move relative to the intended trial, what is the probability of releasing an estimate more than 0.02 from the truth, and can local validation using 50, 100, or 200 records per predicted class reduce that probability to at most 0.05 while retaining at least 0.90 release probability when mappings are compatible?

**Aims.** Estimate the bias, unsafe-release probability, interval performance, and mapping-detection accuracy of conventional inverse-variance averaging, random-effects averaging, heterogeneity screening, explicit target-population transport, aggregate component diagnostics, and local validation gates. Determine whether conventional heterogeneity diagnostics can distinguish semantic mapping failure from genuine effect modification. Determine the minimum of 50, 100, or 200 locally reviewed records per predicted class that meets prespecified safety and availability criteria.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains six fixed site contexts with s_j equal to -1.25, -0.75, -0.25, 0.25, 0.75, and 1.25 and 3000 independent source records per site. Generate X1, an older-age indicator, from Bernoulli(expit(-0.20 + 0.45 s_j)); X2, a female-sex indicator, from Bernoulli(expit(0.10 - 0.15 s_j)); X3, a high-comorbidity indicator, from Bernoulli(expit(-0.80 + 0.70 X1 - 0.20 X2 + 0.25 s_j)); and U, an additional prognostic frailty indicator unavailable to the federation, from Bernoulli(expit(-1.00 + 0.40 X1 + 0.60 X3)). Generate a renal-function measure G = 75 - 10 X1 - 12 X3 - 10 U + 12 Z, where Z is standard normal. Intended eligibility is E = I(G >= 45). Treatment availability is Q from Bernoulli(expit(-0.35 + 0.35 s_j + 0.30 X1 - 0.20 X2 + 0.50 X3)). If Q = 0, treatment category D is 0, the comparator. If Q = 1, D is 1, 2, or 3 with probabilities 0.60, 0.20, and 0.20. The intended trial compares D = 1 with D = 0 and excludes D = 2 and D = 3. Categories 2 and 3 represent clinically distinct therapies that can share neighboring local codes with the intended treatment. Treatment depends only on X1, X2, X3, site, and not U, so conditional exchangeability holds within the eight X cells.
- Outcome model: The canonical narrow five-year event Y is Bernoulli with logit probability eta_Y = -2.10 + 0.45 X1 + 0.25 X2 + 0.75 X3 + 0.45 U + 0.10 s_j + tau_D. Set tau_0 = 0, tau_1 = -0.65 + 0.20 X3 + 0.30 U + gamma s_j, tau_2 = -0.10 + 0.10 X3 + 0.10 U, and tau_3 = 0.05. Generate an independent near-event W with logit probability -2.60 + 0.30 X1 + 0.15 X2 + 0.45 X3 + 0.35 U + 0.05 s_j - 0.05 I(D = 1) + 0.05 I(D = 3). There is complete five-year follow-up, no censoring, no competing event, and no missing data. The outcome is generated directly at five years rather than through monthly hazards.
- Treatment effect: The intended intervention effect is specified on the conditional log-odds scale by tau_1. With gamma = 0, odds ratios range from exp(-0.65) = 0.52 to exp(-0.15) = 0.86 across X3 and U. With gamma = 0.16, they range from exp(-0.85) = 0.43 to exp(0.05) = 1.05 across site context, X3, and U. Category D = 2 has a substantially weaker effect and category D = 3 has slight harm. Consequently, broadening the exposed definition from D = 1 to D in {1,2} defines a coherent but different intervention contrast.
- Effect modification: Effect modification is present through X3 and U in every scenario. The site-context factor gamma is either 0 or 0.16. Gamma = 0.16 adds genuine contextual heterogeneity that remains after standardizing measured covariates. Because U modifies treatment effect but does not affect treatment after conditioning on X, it is not an unmeasured confounder. It makes eligibility mappings that select different frailty distributions scientifically consequential.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 6 mapping conditions by 2 contextual-effect levels by 2 validation-accuracy levels equals 24 scenarios. Conditional exchangeability holds in every locally intended analysis because treatment is independent of U given the eight measured X cells. Positivity is moderate, treatment and outcome cell means are estimable without nuisance-model misspecification, the target covariate distribution is supplied exactly, sites are independent, aggregation is exact, and secure computation introduces no numerical or privacy noise. The compatible explicit-transport estimator should therefore be unbiased. The aggregate-equivalent outcome permutation is a negative-control mismatch that is semantically wrong but cannot change any estimator based on the released cell aggregates.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot evaluate irregular visits, longitudinal treatment changes, grace periods, censoring, competing risks, missing data, continuous or high-dimensional confounding, nuisance-model misspecification, differential-privacy noise, secure-aggregation failures, malicious sites, temporal coding drift, combinations of multiple simultaneous mapping errors, or generalization beyond six fixed site contexts. It cannot prove semantic equivalence from summaries. The permutation condition deliberately establishes that such proof is impossible when two definitions induce the same released aggregates. Results apply to detecting the specified incompatibilities, not to every coding-system or phenotype error.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Local trial-component mapping | Compatible: all sites use G >= 45, compare D = 1 with D = 0 while excluding D = 2 and D = 3, and use Y, Visible eligibility drift: two sites use G >= 60, Aggregate-stealth eligibility drift: within each X cell, two sites retain exactly the canonical number eligible but select records in descending order of U plus an independent Uniform(0,0.01) tie breaker, Visible exposure drift: two sites classify D = 1 or D = 2 as exposed, D = 0 as comparator, and only D = 3 as excluded, Visible outcome drift: two sites use the composite Y or W, Aggregate-equivalent outcome drift: two sites uniformly permute Y labels within every X by D analysis cell, preserving every cell count, event count, effect estimate, and cell-based variance while changing individual outcome identity | The levels represent a threshold disagreement, an undocumented proxy that preserves ordinary cohort summaries, neighboring treatment-code contamination, a narrow-versus-composite outcome disagreement, and an adversarial nonidentifiability control. The 45 and 60 thresholds and 25% contamination among locally exposed active therapies are moderate protocol changes rather than claims about frequencies in the cited studies. Exactly two sites are affected. To avoid tying failure to one site type, the affected pair cycles equally across replicates through sites {1,6}, {2,5}, and {3,4}. |
| Genuine site-context effect heterogeneity | gamma = 0, gamma = 0.16 per unit of s_j | These levels span no contextual interaction and a strong but plausible range from substantial benefit to approximately no benefit. They test whether heterogeneity alarms confuse genuine effect variation with mapping failure. |
| Accuracy of local reference adjudication | Perfect reference labels, Each binary reference label is independently flipped with probability 0.05; each three-class exposure label is replaced with either other class with total probability 0.05 | The levels separate an ideal local validation system from one with modest chart-review or phenotype-adjudication error. The 5% value is a sensitivity analysis, not an estimate attributed to prior work. |

**Estimands.**

- **Intended common-target, equal-site five-year risk difference**: The protocol is eligibility G >= 45; baseline assignment to D = 1 versus D = 0; exclusion of D = 2 and D = 3; narrow outcome Y; complete follow-up to five years. Let q_x be the canonical eligible X distribution obtained by mixing the six source-site superpopulations and conditioning on E = 1. The target is Psi = (1/6) sum over sites j and eight cells x of q_x times {Pr(Y under D = 1 | J = j, X = x, E = 1) - Pr(Y under D = 0 | J = j, X = x, E = 1)}. This is a marginal risk difference for a superpopulation with the six named site contexts weighted equally and the same measured-covariate distribution q_x in every context. The site roster is fixed rather than sampled from a population of possible sites. *Truth: Compute q_x and Psi exactly by enumerating six sites, eight X cells, and two U values. Multiply the sequential Bernoulli probabilities by the normal-tail probability Pr(G >= 45 | X,U,J), normalize to obtain q_x and Pr(U | X,E = 1,J), and sum the expit outcome probabilities under D = 1 and D = 0. Exact enumeration also gives probability-limit local estimands for the threshold, broad-exposure, and composite-outcome mappings. For aggregate-stealth eligibility, its limiting U selection probabilities are analytic: if the canonical eligible fraction k_x is no larger than Pr(U = 1 | X), select a fraction k_x divided by Pr(U = 1 | X) of U = 1 records; otherwise select all U = 1 records and the required fraction of U = 0 records. The permutation condition has exactly the compatible cell estimand by construction. No brute-force truth sample is required.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Naive fixed-effect averaging of local trial estimates | Within each site's locally mapped cohort, estimate mu_ajx as the event mean for local arm a in each of the eight X cells and p_jx as the local empirical X-cell proportion. Compute psi_j = sum_x p_jx(mu_1jx - mu_0jx). Pool sites using inverse within-site variance weights: psi_FE = sum_j w_j psi_j divided by sum_j w_j, where w_j = 1/V_j. | Use the empirical influence function phi_i = A_i/e_x(Y_i - mu_1x) - (1-A_i)/(1-e_x)(Y_i - mu_0x) + mu_1x - mu_0x - psi_j, with e_x equal to the local treated proportion in cell x. Set V_j to sample variance(phi_i)/n_j. Treat estimated inverse-variance weights as fixed and set Var(psi_FE) = 1/sum_j w_j. | yes | Non-convergence occurs if any locally represented X cell has fewer than five records in either analysis arm, any site variance is nonfinite or nonpositive, or fewer than all six site estimates are available. |
| DerSimonian-Laird random-effects averaging | Start from the six local estimates and variances above. Compute Q = sum_j w_j(psi_j - psi_FE)^2, C = sum_j w_j - sum_j w_j^2/sum_j w_j, tau_squared = max(0,(Q - 5)/C), random-effects weights w_REj = 1/(V_j + tau_squared), and psi_RE = sum_j w_REj psi_j divided by sum_j w_REj. | Use Var(psi_RE) = 1/sum_j w_REj with the DerSimonian-Laird moment estimate of tau_squared. | yes | Non-convergence follows the local estimator rules and also includes C <= 0, a nonfinite tau_squared, or a nonpositive pooled variance. |
| Conventional Q and I-squared gate | Use the naive fixed-effect estimate only when Cochran Q has chi-square p >= 0.10 with 5 degrees of freedom and I-squared = max(0,(Q - 5)/Q) is at most 0.50. Otherwise withhold the network estimate. | When released, use the fixed-effect variance 1/sum_j w_j. | yes | Computational non-convergence follows the fixed-effect method. Rejection by Q or I-squared is recorded as diagnostic abstention, not non-convergence. |
| Ungated explicit-target federated standardization | Treat the externally supplied eight-cell target distribution q_x as fixed. At site j compute psi_jq = sum_x q_x(mu_1jx - mu_0jx) from local arm-specific cell means. Estimate the intended network contrast as psi_q = sum_j psi_jq/6. Sites return the 8 by 2 counts and event totals, psi_jq, and its variance or equivalent influence-curve second moment. | For site j use V_jq = sum_x q_x^2(s_1jx^2/n_1jx + s_0jx^2/n_0jx), where s_ajx^2 is the unbiased binary-outcome sample variance. Use Var(psi_q) = sum_j V_jq/36. The target table is treated as fixed, so its uncertainty is not included. | no | Non-convergence occurs if a target-positive X cell has fewer than five local records in either arm, any required cell statistic is nonfinite, or the network variance is nonpositive. |
| Aggregate component-homogeneity gate plus explicit transport | Release the explicit-target estimate only if three aggregate tests pass. Sum Pearson homogeneity statistics across the eight X strata for mapped eligibility among source records, giving 40 degrees of freedom; across eight X strata for the three local exposure classes exposed, comparator, and excluded, giving 80 degrees of freedom; and across eight X by two local treatment strata for observed outcome risk, giving 80 degrees of freedom. Apply Holm adjustment to the three p-values and pass only if every adjusted p-value is at least 0.05. | When released, use sum_j V_jq/36 from explicit-target standardization. | no | Computational non-convergence follows explicit-target standardization. A sparse contingency table that invalidates a Pearson calculation causes diagnostic abstention. A statistically significant component test is abstention rather than non-convergence. |
| Local trial-component validation gate with 50 reviewed records per predicted class | Each site validates eligibility as two classes, exposure as three classes, and outcome as two classes. Sample 50 records without replacement from every locally predicted class and compare the local label with the reference label. For each class calculate its discordance proportion and one-sided 95% Wilson upper bound using z = 1.644854. A class passes only when the upper bound is at most 0.15. Release the all-six-site explicit-target estimate only if every class at every site passes. | When released, use sum_j V_jq/36. Validation uncertainty enters the release decision but is not added to the conditional effect variance. | no | Effect-estimator non-convergence follows explicit transport. Fewer than 50 available records in a predicted class, a failed validation bound, or a missing component report causes protocol abstention and is not counted as numerical non-convergence. |
| Local trial-component validation gate with 100 reviewed records per predicted class | Apply the same seven-class-per-site validation rule with m = 100 and release the all-six-site explicit-target estimate only if every one-sided 95% Wilson upper bound is at most 0.15. | When released, use sum_j V_jq/36 without adding validation-selection variance. | no | Use the same rules as the 50-record gate, with fewer than 100 available records in a predicted class treated as protocol abstention. |
| Local trial-component validation gate with 200 reviewed records per predicted class | Apply the same seven-class-per-site validation rule with m = 200 and release the all-six-site explicit-target estimate only if every one-sided 95% Wilson upper bound is at most 0.15. | When released, use sum_j V_jq/36 without adding validation-selection variance. | no | Use the same rules as the smaller gates, with fewer than 200 available records in a predicted class treated as protocol abstention. |
| Site-exclusion validation gate with 200 reviewed records per predicted class | Apply the 200-record validation rule separately to each site. Exclude failing sites and average psi_jq equally across passing sites when at least four sites pass. Report the resulting estimand explicitly as the common-q average over passing site contexts, not as the original six-site target. | For K passing sites use Var = sum of V_jq over passing sites divided by K^2. | no | Abstain if fewer than four sites pass or if any included site fails the explicit-transport numerical criteria. Validation exclusion is not numerical non-convergence. |

**Performance.** Primary: Unsafe-release probability: Pr(an estimate is released and its absolute error for the intended six-site risk difference exceeds 0.02). Always-release methods have release probability one. Because always abstaining can score zero, unsafe-release probability must be interpreted jointly with compatible-network release probability.. Secondary: Bias and absolute bias for the intended risk difference, Empirical standard error, Mean model-based standard error, Relative error in model-based standard error, Mean squared error, 95% coverage, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for the null risk difference of zero, Numerical convergence, Network release and abstention probabilities, Site-level and network-level mapping-detection sensitivity and specificity, False abstention under compatible semantics with gamma = 0 and gamma = 0.16, Conditional bias and coverage among released estimates, Paired mapping-induced change from the compatible condition using common random numbers, For site exclusion, error and coverage against both the original six-site target and the declared passing-site target, Verification that aggregate-equivalent outcome relabeling changes the paired estimate by no more than Monte Carlo noise. 2500 replicates. Unsafe release is binary. Its worst-case Monte Carlo standard error is sqrt[0.5(1-0.5)/R]. Requiring this to be at most 0.01 gives R >= 0.25/0.0001 = 2500 replicates per scenario. Thus the full study uses 24 by 2500 = 60000 scenario replicates. Mapping levels within the same gamma and validation-error block use common random numbers, and validation sample sizes are nested, reducing Monte Carlo error for paired contrasts. Report every performance estimate with its harness-computed Monte Carlo standard error.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: First require the compatible explicit-target estimator to have absolute bias at most 0.005, coverage between 0.925 and 0.975, and numerical non-convergence below 0.05. Conditional on that validity check, classify the scoped problem as real if at least two of the four effect-altering mismatches, visible eligibility, aggregate-stealth eligibility, broad exposure, and composite outcome, produce absolute bias of at least 0.02 or an unsafe-release probability of at least 0.20 for ungated explicit transport, and if either the Q gate or aggregate gate has mapping sensitivity below 0.80 or compatible-network release below 0.80 under gamma = 0.16. Separately classify a local-validation design as operationally adequate only if, under both reference-label error levels, its unsafe-release probability is at most 0.05 in every effect-altering mapping scenario, its release probability is at least 0.90 in every compatible scenario, and its site-level sensitivity and specificity are each at least 0.90.
- Problem is not real if: After the compatible validity check passes, classify the scoped problem as not consequential if every effect-altering mapping has absolute bias below 0.01, the upper 95% Monte Carlo confidence limit for unsafe release is below 0.05 in every such scenario, and conventional Q or aggregate diagnostics achieve both sensitivity and specificity of at least 0.90. The aggregate-equivalent outcome permutation is excluded from this harm criterion because it is deliberately effect-neutral.
- Uninformative if: Classify the study as uninformative if the compatible explicit-target estimator fails its validity check, numerical non-convergence exceeds 0.10 in any scenario, the exact probability-limit calculations show that all four intended harmful mappings move the affected-site estimand by less than 0.01, the permutation condition changes released cell estimates by more than 0.002 in paired Monte Carlo means, or results fall between the real and not-real thresholds. Do not reinterpret intermediate results after inspecting them.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM could be constructed to make semantic mapping failure look harmful. *Mitigation: Freeze all coefficients and thresholds before running replicates; compare every mismatch with a paired compatible condition; include genuine effect heterogeneity; include an aggregate-stealth eligibility error; and include an outcome-label permutation that is semantically wrong but exactly effect-neutral. Report mapping types separately rather than averaging them into one favorable result.*
- A local reference label is treated as a semantic gold standard even though real records may remain ambiguous. *Mitigation: Cross perfect adjudication with independent 5% reference-label error and report the validation burden and false-abstention rate. Interpret successful validation as an upper bound achievable with a documented local adjudication process, not as proof that every network has such a standard.*
- The validation gate can achieve low unsafe-release probability by almost always abstaining. *Mitigation: Make compatible-network release probability and site-level specificity co-primary decision constraints. A gate is not adequate unless release is at least 0.90 under compatible mappings.*
- Testing many component-class bounds can cause network-level false rejection. *Mitigation: Use the prespecified 0.15 one-sided Wilson bound, evaluate 50, 100, and 200 records per class with nested samples, and report both class-level and whole-network specificity. Do not tune the cutoff after results.*
- Exact discrete-cell adjustment favors the proposed transported estimator. *Mitigation: Make this a deliberate isolation design: exchangeability and measured-covariate adjustment are correct so observed bias can be attributed to target weighting or semantics. State explicitly that the study does not evaluate high-dimensional nuisance learning or model misspecification.*
- The aggregate-equivalent permutation is adversarial rather than a literal coding mechanism. *Mitigation: Label it an identifiability-boundary negative control. Use the threshold, broad-code, and composite mappings for applied performance conclusions.*
- Fixed site differences can make aggregate homogeneity tests reject even when semantics agree. *Mitigation: This is intentional and directly measures the diagnostic's specificity. Include both gamma levels and report compatible false-abstention rather than treating every detected difference as mapping failure.*
- Excluding failed sites silently changes the target population. *Mitigation: Name the passing-site estimand, require at least four passing sites, and evaluate it against both its own truth and the original six-site truth. Never label its interval as an interval for the original target.*
- Only two sites are incompatible and only one component fails at a time. *Mitigation: Rotate the affected pair across low, middle, and high site contexts. Restrict conclusions to one-component failures affecting two of six sites; multiple simultaneous failures require a later factorial study.*
- Exact aggregation omits privacy perturbation and implementation failures. *Mitigation: Treat pooled-equivalent federated computation as already addressed. State that secure-aggregation code, privacy leakage, differential-privacy noise, and malicious-site behavior require separate studies.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The design generates 60000 replicate datasets, each with 6 by 3000 = 18000 source records, for 1.08 billion transient baseline records and 360000 local site analyses. All methods reuse the same eight-cell tabulations. There are zero glm fits and zero ltmle or SuperLearner fits: 24 scenarios by 2500 replicates by 0 expensive fits. Truth and mapping probability limits use exact enumeration, so there is no two-million-person truth run per scenario. Using six workers and resumable batches, the expected run is approximately 3 to 8 hours and should remain below an overnight 12-hour budget. Implementation uses the existing harness plus data.table, future, and furrr. TrialEmulation, gfoRmula, ltmle, lmtp, concrete, CICI, Stan, and cmdstanr are unnecessary.

**Also bears on.** DTA-02 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019; Li et al. Federated target trial emulation using distributed observational data for treatment effect estimation. npj Digital Medicine. 2025; Manke-Reimers M, et al. When, why and how are estimated effects transported between populations? A scoping review. European Journal of Epidemiology. 2025; Federated target trial emulation for time-to-event outcomes via POLARIS. 2026; Privacy-preserving distributed algorithms in action: ten principles for high-quality multi-site clinical studies. JAMIA. 2026

## 15. ELG-01: Latent eligibility in target trial emulation: a validation-anchored ADEMP simulation

*Catalog entry: An unrecorded eligibility criterion is indistinguishable from a criterion that was met*

**Question.** Across 24 scenarios, how large is the bias in the 60-month marginal risk difference among truly eligible people when 20% or 50% of eligibility measurements are unrecorded, the conditional odds ratio for recording ineligible versus eligible people is 1, 2, or 4, expected validation sample size is 100 or 400, and treatment effects are homogeneous or modified by comorbidity? How much do validation-anchored multiple imputation, two-phase weighting, doubly robust correction, and partial-identification bounds improve on complete-case analysis and methods requiring eligibility-MAR?

**Aims.** Estimate signed and absolute bias, 95% interval coverage, and eligible-population error for each method. Quantify whether failure grows from an eligibility-recording odds ratio of 1 to 4 and from 20% to 50% unrecorded eligibility. Determine whether a probability validation sample of expected size 100 or 400 reduces absolute risk-difference bias below 0.005 and restores coverage to 0.93 through 0.97. The odds-ratio-1 scenarios are implementation controls for already developed MAR methods, not an attempt to redesign that solved part of the problem.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Generate n = 4000 independent people per replicate. Draw Z uniformly on [-1,1] and define age as 65 + 15Z years. Draw male sex S from Bernoulli(0.50). Draw specialist access H from Bernoulli(expit(0.10 - 0.50Z + 0.40S)). Draw comorbidity C from Bernoulli(expit(-1.00 + 0.80Z + 0.50S - 0.70H)). Draw recorded treatment intent I from Bernoulli(expit(-0.50 + 0.50Z + 0.90C - 0.60H + 0.30S)). Draw a standard logistic error epsilon independently and define the latent eligibility score L = 0.85 - 0.65Z - 1.10C + 0.25S + 0.50H + epsilon. Define the underlying eGFR-like laboratory value G = 45 + 5L and true eligibility E = 1(G >= 45). Thus P(E = 1 | Z,S,C,H,I) = expit(0.85 - 0.65Z - 1.10C + 0.25S + 0.50H); I has a true coefficient of zero in this model. Treatment is initiated once at baseline with P(A = 1 | Z,S,C,H,I) = expit(-0.30 + 0.35Z + 0.65C + 0.75I - 0.40H + 0.20S). This gives conditional exchangeability and practical positivity while retaining observational treatment allocation. Generate clinical recording R from Bernoulli(expit(alpha + 0.50Z + 0.80C + 0.70I - 0.60H + gamma(1-E))). For each unrecorded-rate level m and gamma, alpha is the unique root of E[P(R = 1 | X,E)] = 1-m. Compute and freeze the six numeric roots before production by uniroot on [-10,10] using a 10,000,000-person calibration draw with seed 81001; require calibration error below 0.0001. Clinical records reveal G and E only when R = 1. For R = 0, draw forced validation V independently with probability 0.125, 0.500, 0.050, or 0.200 for the combinations (m = 0.20,nv = 100), (m = 0.20,nv = 400), (m = 0.50,nv = 100), and (m = 0.50,nv = 400), respectively. Validation reveals G and E. Define Q = 1 when R = 1 or V = 1, and define the known phase-two observation probability rho = 1 for R = 1 and the stated validation probability for R = 0.
- Outcome model: The protocol has one baseline eligibility assessment, immediate initiation of A = 1 versus A = 0, no switching, complete follow-up, and a binary event by 60 months. Define p0 = 0.20 + 0.04Z + 0.08C - 0.03H + 0.03I + 0.02S. These coefficients place untreated risk between 0.13 and 0.37. Draw one UY uniformly on [0,1], define Y0 = 1(UY <= p0), define Y1 = 1(UY <= p0 + delta(C)), and observe Y = AY1 + (1-A)Y0. The model is additive on the 60-month risk scale. Eligibility and G have no residual prognostic effect after the fully recorded covariates; this isolates errors caused by recovering the eligible population.
- Treatment effect: Under homogeneous effects, delta(C) = -0.06 for everyone. Under heterogeneous effects, delta(C) = -0.04 when C = 0 and -0.12 when C = 1. All generated risks remain between 0 and 1. The homogeneous level is a negative control because selection cannot change a constant causal risk difference.
- Effect modification: C is the only treatment-effect modifier and is fully recorded. The heterogeneous level creates an 0.08 risk-difference contrast between comorbidity groups. This is a deliberate stress level that can make incorrect recovery of the eligible comorbidity distribution clinically material. The homogeneous level and the eligibility-MAR level prevent the DGM from guaranteeing an apparent problem.
- Deliberately true: The design makes consistency, no interference, conditional treatment exchangeability, positivity, a correctly specified treatment propensity model, an exact deterministic eligibility threshold, and known ignorable validation sampling true. At recording odds ratio 1, clinical recording is MAR given recorded covariates. At odds ratios 2 and 4, recording is MNAR only through true eligibility. The validation-anchored eligibility and recording models are correctly specified. The outcome effect depends only on recorded covariates. These choices isolate latent eligibility rather than rewarding methods for also solving treatment confounding, measurement error, censoring, or arbitrary nuisance-model misspecification.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot establish performance with external or nonprobability validation data, unknown validation inclusion probabilities, lack of validation transportability, measurement error around the eligibility threshold, irregular visits, time-varying eligibility, repeated sequential trials, treatment switching, censoring, competing events, an outcome-dependent recording decision beyond recorded history, unrecorded effect modifiers, unmeasured treatment confounding, severe treatment nonpositivity, or misspecification of both the eligibility and recording models. It evaluates a single baseline criterion and a 60-month binary endpoint, not a longitudinal survival estimator.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Marginal proportion with unrecorded clinical eligibility | 0.20, 0.50 | Twenty percent represents moderate incomplete eligibility ascertainment; 50% represents the severe EHR setting in which complete-case restriction can discard half the source population. These levels span the missingness regimes considered in the direct missing-eligibility literature without claiming that either is universal. |
| Conditional recording odds ratio for ineligible versus eligible people | 1, 2, 4 | Set gamma to 0, log(2), or log(4). Odds ratio 1 is eligibility-MAR conditional on recorded history. Odds ratios 2 and 4 represent moderate and strong latent-eligibility dependence. This association is not estimable without additional information, so the levels are explicitly sensitivity values rather than claimed empirical constants. |
| Expected number of forced validation assessments among R = 0 | 100, 400 | These are feasible chart-review or stored-sample validation budgets. Bernoulli validation makes the realized count variable while preserving known inclusion probabilities and straightforward two-phase inference. |
| Treatment-effect structure on the risk-difference scale | homogeneous: delta = -0.06, heterogeneous: delta = -0.04 for C = 0 and -0.12 for C = 1 | The homogeneous level is a negative control. The heterogeneous level makes erroneous recovery of the eligible comorbidity distribution consequential without introducing an unrecorded effect modifier. |

**Estimands.**

- **60-month marginal causal risk difference among truly eligible people**: For the superpopulation generated by the protocol, estimate RD60 = P(Y1 = 1 | E = 1) - P(Y0 = 1 | E = 1), comparing immediate baseline initiation with no initiation, no subsequent switching, and complete 60-month follow-up. This is marginal over the true eligible distribution, not conditional on covariates and not restricted to people whose eligibility was recorded. *Truth: For homogeneous effects, RD60 is exactly -0.060000. For heterogeneous effects, RD60 = -0.04 - 0.08P(C = 1 | E = 1). Compute P(C = 1 | E = 1), both arm-specific risks, and RD60 once using 10,000,000 independent covariate and eligibility draws with seed 20260731. Average the conditional risks p0 and p0 + delta(C), rather than simulating outcomes. Reuse these two truths across all observation and validation scenarios because those protocol components do not alter the causal estimand. The worst-case Monte Carlo SE for eligible prevalence is below 0.00016; the Monte Carlo SE for heterogeneous RD60 is below 0.000013.*
- **True eligible-population proportion**: PE = P(E = 1) in the same superpopulation. Each method must report its implied eligible fraction and implied eligible count out of 4000 alongside its treatment-effect estimate. *Truth: Compute PE from the same 10,000,000-person truth draw as the primary estimand by averaging E. For partial identification, also compute whether the estimated prevalence interval contains PE.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Oracle full-eligibility benchmark | Fit e(X) = P(A = 1 | Z,S,C,H,I) by logistic regression with the exact linear formula. For a in {0,1}, set za = 1(A=a)/(a e(X) + (1-a)(1-e(X))) and solve sum[E za (Y-mua)] = 0. Report mu1-mu0 and mean(E). | Use the empirical stacked M-estimation sandwich containing the treatment propensity score equations and the two weighted risk equations. Compute A as the central finite-difference Jacobian with step 1e-6 times max(1,absolute parameter value), B as the mean outer product of person-level scores, and covariance A inverse B A inverse-transpose divided by n. | no | The treatment model fails to converge, has complete separation or any absolute coefficient above 20, either arm equation has effective sample size below 10, or the sandwich is singular or nonfinite. |
| Complete-case eligibility analysis | Use the same treatment propensity model and weighted risk equations as the oracle, but replace E with R E. The implied eligible fraction is mean(R E), which explicitly records that the method analyzes recorded eligible people rather than all truly eligible people. | Use the same stacked empirical sandwich with treatment propensity and risk equations; treat R and E as observed membership indicators. | yes | The propensity model fails, either recorded-eligible treatment arm has effective sample size below 10, or the covariance or estimate is nonfinite. |
| Eligibility-MAR multiple imputation of the continuous laboratory value | Among R = 1 records, fit G on Z,S,C,H,I by ordinary least squares. For each of M = 20 imputations, draw coefficients from a multivariate normal distribution using the HC2 covariance, sample centered fitted residuals with replacement, impute G for R = 0, derive E as 1(G >= 45), and estimate the two propensity-weighted risks using the completed eligibility indicator. Pool the risk difference and eligible fraction across imputations. | Within each imputation use the stacked sandwich for treatment propensity and weighted arm risks. Use Rubin total variance T = mean(U) + (1 + 1/20)B, where U is within-imputation variance and B is between-imputation variance. | no | The laboratory model is rank deficient, its HC2 covariance is not positive semidefinite, any completed arm has effective sample size below 10, fewer than 20 completed estimates are finite, or the pooled variance is nonfinite. |
| Eligibility-MAR multiple imputation of the eligibility indicator | Among R = 1 records, fit logistic regression E on Z,S,C,H,I. For each of M = 20 imputations, draw coefficients from the model's robust covariance, draw missing E from Bernoulli probabilities, and estimate the propensity-weighted arm risks and eligible fraction in the completed data. Pool across imputations. | Use the stacked propensity and arm-risk sandwich within imputations and Rubin total variance across imputations. | no | The eligibility model fails, separates, has an absolute coefficient above 20, yields nonfinite probabilities, any completed arm has effective sample size below 10, or fewer than 20 completed estimates are finite. |
| Estimated eligibility-MAR inverse probability of ascertainment weighting | Fit s(X) = P(R = 1 | X) using logistic regression with splines::ns(Z,4), S,C,H,I, all pairwise interactions among S,C,H,I, and interactions between ns(Z,4) and each binary covariate. Among R = 1,E = 1 records, use membership weight 1/s(X) in the common propensity-weighted arm-risk equations. Estimate eligible prevalence as mean(R E/s(X)). | Use one stacked empirical sandwich containing the treatment propensity score, ascertainment-model score, eligible-prevalence equation, and both arm-risk equations. This includes uncertainty from estimating s(X). | no | Either logistic model fails or separates, any predicted probability is below 1e-8, either arm has effective sample size below 10, or the stacked covariance is singular or nonfinite. |
| Oracle marginal-ascertainment weighting diagnostic | Compute the true marginal s(X) from the DGM as pE(X)r1(X) + [1-pE(X)]r0(X), where re(X) is the known recording probability at E=e. Use membership weight R E/s(X) in the common treatment-weighted risk equations. | Treat true s(X) as fixed and use the stacked sandwich for the estimated treatment propensity and arm-risk equations. | no | Either arm has effective sample size below 10 or an estimate or covariance is nonfinite. |
| Validation-anchored joint-model multiple imputation of eligibility | Give each Q = 1 record phase-two weight d = 1 when R = 1 and d = 1/rho when R = 0,V = 1. Fit weighted logistic models pE(X) = P(E = 1 | Z,S,C,H,I) and pR(E,X) = P(R = 1 | Z,S,C,H,I,E). For each unvalidated R = 0 record, calculate q = pE(X)[1-pR(1,X)] divided by pE(X)[1-pR(1,X)] + [1-pE(X)][1-pR(0,X)]. Draw missing E from Bernoulli(q) in M = 20 completed data sets and estimate the common propensity-weighted risks and eligible fraction. | Build a joint robust sandwich for both phase-two weighted logistic score systems. Draw their coefficients jointly from this covariance for each imputation. Use the completed-data stacked sandwich within imputations and Rubin total variance across imputations. | no | Either weighted model fails, separates, or has an absolute coefficient above 20; the joint covariance is not positive semidefinite; posterior eligibility probabilities are nonfinite; an arm has effective sample size below 10; or fewer than 20 imputations are valid. |
| Known-design two-phase inverse probability weighting | Define membership weight w = Q E/rho, with rho = 1 for R = 1 and the known validation probability for R = 0. Use w in the common propensity-weighted arm-risk equations. Estimate eligible prevalence by mean(w). | Use a stacked empirical sandwich containing the treatment propensity score, eligible-prevalence equation, and both arm-risk equations. Treat the Bernoulli validation probabilities as known design quantities. | no | No validated eligible person appears in a necessary arm, an arm has effective sample size below 10, or the estimate or sandwich covariance is singular or nonfinite. |
| Doubly robust augmented two-phase correction | Obtain q(X,R) from the validation-anchored pE and pR models. For arm a define za = 1(A=a)/P(A=a|X), numerator contribution Na = q za Y + Q/rho (E-q) za Y, and denominator contribution Da = q za + Q/rho (E-q) za. Estimate mua = sum(Na)/sum(Da) and RD60 = mu1-mu0. Estimate eligible prevalence from mean[q + Q/rho(E-q)]. The estimator is consistent if either q is correct or rho is correct, in addition to the treatment assumptions. | Use the full stacked empirical sandwich for treatment propensity, phase-two weighted pE and pR scores, eligible prevalence, and both augmented arm-risk equations. | no | A nuisance model fails or separates, posterior q is nonfinite, an augmented denominator is at most 1e-8, or the stacked covariance is singular or nonfinite. |
| No-assumption partial-identification outer bounds | Ignore validation information. For each arm a calculate treatment-reweighted probabilities c_a = mean[za R E Y], d_a = mean[za R E], u1_a = mean[za(1-R)Y], and u0_a = mean[za(1-R)(1-Y)]. Set the risk bounds to L_a = c_a/(d_a+u0_a) and U_a = (c_a+u1_a)/(d_a+u1_a). Report the risk-difference outer bound [L_1-U_0,U_1-L_0]. Report eligible-prevalence bounds [mean(RE),mean(RE)+mean(1-R)]. | Use the stacked treatment-propensity sandwich and delta-method gradients for both bound endpoints. These are conservative outer bounds because they do not impose cross-arm restrictions on hidden eligibility. | no | A required denominator is zero, the estimated lower endpoint exceeds the upper endpoint by more than 1e-10, or an endpoint covariance is singular or nonfinite. |

**Performance.** Primary: Signed bias of the estimated 60-month marginal risk difference, with absolute bias used in the decision rule. For partial identification, the corresponding primary quantity is whether the identified set contains the true risk difference and its width.. Secondary: Empirical standard error, Mean model-based standard error, Relative error in model-based standard error, Mean squared error, 95% interval coverage, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for RD60 = 0, Convergence proportion, Bias and relative bias in the implied eligible-population proportion, Mean absolute error in implied eligible count out of 4000, Effective sample size and maximum analysis weight, Partial-identification set coverage and mean width, Difference in absolute bias and MSE versus complete-case analysis, Difference in absolute bias and MSE versus the oracle benchmark. 2000 replicates. The least precise design-based method uses an expected validation sample of 100. With approximately 70 eligible validation records and 35 per treatment arm, the binary-outcome variance bound gives an approximate replicate SD of sqrt(0.25/35 + 0.25/35) = 0.1195, rounded to 0.12. To target Monte Carlo SE 0.003 for bias requires (0.12/0.003)^2 = 1600 replicates. Inflate by 25% for occasional numerical failures and variance above the approximation, giving 2000. At SD 0.12, MCSE is 0.12/sqrt(2000) = 0.00268. At 95% coverage, the binomial MCSE is sqrt(0.95*0.05/2000) = 0.00487. All Monte Carlo SEs come from the existing ADEMP harness.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Use the heterogeneous-effect, expected-validation-400 scenarios for the primary decision. The latent problem is material if all of the following hold: in the 50%-unrecorded, recording-OR-4 scenario, oracle marginal-ascertainment weighting has absolute RD bias at least 0.015 and the 95% Monte Carlo lower confidence bound for absolute bias exceeds 0.010; bias has the same direction and is larger at OR 4 than OR 2 and at 50% than 20% unrecorded eligibility; at least two of the three feasible MAR methods have 95% coverage whose upper Monte Carlo confidence limit is below 0.90 in at least two of the four MNAR combinations; and the doubly robust validation estimator at expected validation size 400 has absolute bias at most 0.005, a 95% Monte Carlo upper confidence bound below 0.010, coverage from 0.93 through 0.97, and convergence of at least 0.98. Homogeneous-effect scenarios must have absolute bias below 0.005 for the oracle, complete-case, and design-based validation estimators.
- Problem is not real if: In the heterogeneous, 50%-unrecorded, recording-OR-4 scenario, the oracle marginal-ascertainment weighting diagnostic and all three feasible MAR methods each have absolute bias below 0.005, a 95% Monte Carlo upper confidence bound below 0.010, coverage from 0.93 through 0.97, and no monotone increase in bias from OR 1 to OR 4. This would show that latent recording dependence of the stipulated magnitude does not materially move the target risk difference under this DGM.
- Uninformative if: Results are uninformative if they fall between the numerical rules; if the bias MCSE exceeds 0.003; if any decision-critical method converges in fewer than 95% of replicates; if homogeneous-effect or OR-1 controls show absolute bias above 0.010 for the oracle or validation design estimators; if the validation estimator remains biased above 0.010 despite its correct design probabilities; or if conclusions depend on excluding failed replicates. Conditional-on-convergence results must be accompanied by worst-case summaries that assign failed risk differences the values -1 and 1.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM could be constructed to make latent eligibility appear harmful. *Mitigation: Cross the MNAR mechanism with two negative controls. OR 1 removes latent dependence; homogeneous treatment effects make selection irrelevant to the marginal risk difference. Require near-zero bias in both controls, a graded OR and missingness response, persistence under oracle marginal recording weights, and reversal under known-design validation correction.*
- The validation-anchored models are favored because their logistic forms match the DGM. *Mitigation: Include model-free two-phase weighting and a doubly robust estimator. Repeat the doubly robust estimator with an intercept-only eligibility regression. State explicitly that joint-model MI performance does not establish robustness to misspecification.*
- Internal probability validation is more favorable than external or convenience validation. *Mitigation: Use Bernoulli validation with known probabilities and label conclusions as applying to internal probability validation only. Do not claim transportability to external validation sources or chart reviews with unknown selection.*
- Failure of an estimated MAR recording model could be mistaken for failure of the MAR identifying assumption. *Mitigation: Use a flexible estimated recording model and an additional diagnostic supplied with the exact marginal P(R = 1 | X). Bias in the latter is structural because even the true marginal recording probability cannot restore conditional independence from E.*
- Treatment confounding or propensity misspecification could obscure eligibility effects. *Mitigation: Generate treatment from the exact fitted logistic formula, retain broad propensity support, use the same treatment estimator in every method, and include treatment-model uncertainty in every variance estimate. Conclusions are explicitly conditional on no unmeasured treatment confounding.*
- The effect modifier is fully observed and the laboratory value has no residual prognostic role, which simplifies the problem. *Mitigation: Declare this as an isolation design. The study answers whether latent membership alone distorts a marginal effect through population composition; it does not claim performance when the unrecorded laboratory value is also an unmeasured confounder or effect modifier.*
- Multiple-imputation Monte Carlo noise could affect rankings. *Mitigation: Use 20 imputations with parameter uncertainty. On a prespecified 10% subset of replicates, repeat with 50 imputations; flag the comparison if any method's scenario-level bias changes by more than 0.002 or coverage by more than 0.01.*
- Deleting failed replicates can make unstable methods look accurate. *Mitigation: The harness records every failure. Report convergence first, conditional performance second, and worst-case failure bounds. A decision-critical method with convergence below 95% makes the result uninformative.*
- Brute-force truth evaluation can dominate runtime and introduce unnoticed simulation error. *Mitigation: Observation and validation do not alter the estimand, so compute only two truths, one per effect structure. Integrate conditional risks over one shared 10,000,000-person draw rather than simulating outcomes separately for all 24 scenarios.*
- The partial-identification interval may appear reassuring solely because it is extremely wide. *Mitigation: Report truth inclusion together with mean width. Treat a mean RD-set width above 0.20 as practically uninformative even if coverage is nominal.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The production run contains 24*2000 = 48,000 replicates. Reusing nuisance fits across methods requires six fitted models per replicate: one treatment logistic model, one laboratory lm, one direct-eligibility logistic model, one flexible recording logistic model, and two validation-weighted logistic models. This is 288,000 glm or lm fits, plus 3*20*48,000 = 2,880,000 inexpensive completed-data calculations. No ltmle, longitudinal TMLE, SuperLearner, lmtp, concrete, CICI, Stan, or cmdstanr is used. On six workers, the expected run is 6 to 12 hours after a 100-replicate timing pilot. Calibration and the two shared 10,000,000-person truth evaluations should take under 30 minutes. The complete run therefore fits the overnight budget and the existing seeded, resumable harness.

**Also bears on.** ELG-01 (answers)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019; Tompsett D, et al. Target Trial Emulation and Bias Through Missing Eligibility Data: An Application to a Study of Palivizumab for the Prevention of Hospitalization Due to Infant Respiratory Illness. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2023; Benz L, et al. Adjusting for Selection Bias Due to Missing Eligibility Criteria in Emulated Target Trials. American Journal of Epidemiology. 2025; Shen, et al. Missingness in Eligibility Criteria for Target Trial Emulation in EHR With Survival Outcomes. Statistics in Medicine. 2026; Wang, et al. An Operational Target Trial Emulation Framework for Causal Inference Using Electronic Health Records. npj Digital Medicine. 2026; Robins JM, Rotnitzky A, Zhao LP. Estimation of Regression Coefficients When Some Regressors Are Not Always Observed. Journal of the American Statistical Association. 1994; Imbens GW, Manski CF. Confidence Intervals for Partially Identified Parameters. Econometrica. 2004
