## 16. GMT-03: When nominally large target trial emulations lose valid inference: a factorial study of time-varying effective sample size, rare events, and estimated longitudinal weights

*Catalog entry: Large databases yield small effective samples after eligibility, comparator restriction and weighting*

**Question.** Across a 24-scenario factorial design beginning with 1,000,000 source records, expected postrestriction samples of 1,000 or 4,000, primary and competing five-year risks near 1% or 10%, and three levels of treatment overlap, in which scenarios does the usual 95% Wald interval for the marginal 60-month risk difference cover less than 90%; how large is the coverage loss; and does a prespecified warning rule based on a minimum arm-specific risk-set Kish effective sample size below 100 or a cause-specific effective event count below 20 identify failing scenarios with at least 80% sensitivity and 80% specificity?

**Aims.** Estimate, for every scenario, coverage of the conventional estimated-weight sequential-IPW interval and its change from the matched no-confounding scenario. Report the first overlap level at which coverage falls below 90%, together with the corresponding monthly risk-set effective sample sizes, cause-specific effective event counts, retained patient count, and 1,000,000-record source count. Separate failures caused by estimated-weight variance, weight-tail skewness, event rarity, and numerical nonconvergence by comparing fixed-weight, nuisance-aware, effective-sample t, and oracle-weight inference. Evaluate overall and subgroup risk differences. The diagnostic result is numerical: sensitivity and specificity of the fixed 100-patient or 20-effective-event warning rule for classifying scenario-level coverage below 90%.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Use expit(x)=1/(1+exp(-x)). Each replicate represents a source database containing exactly 1,000,000 people. Draw the retained active-comparator new-user cohort size as n ~ Binomial(1000000, p_R), then generate only those n retained people. This shortcut is distributionally exact because retention is deliberately independent of all postrestriction variables. For each retained person generate subgroup G ~ Bernoulli(0.25), continuous baseline X ~ Uniform(-1,1), and L_0 ~ Bernoulli(expit(-0.2 + 0.6X + 0.8G)). For event-free people at months t=1,...,59, generate L_t ~ Bernoulli(expit(-0.7 + 1.3L_(t-1) + 0.5X + 0.6G - 0.5A_(t-1))). L_t is therefore a treatment-affected time-varying confounder. Visits occur at fixed monthly times. Follow-up stops at the first primary event, competing event, or completion of month 60. There is no loss to follow-up other than the artificial censoring imposed by strategy deviation.
- Outcome model: After assigning A_t at each month t=0,...,59, draw one multinomial outcome among primary event Y_t, competing event D_t, and no event. Let eta_Y = alpha_Y + 0.35X + 0.55G + 0.80L_t - 0.287682A_t + 0.287682A_tG and eta_D = alpha_D + 0.45X + 0.80G + 0.60L_t. Set P(Y_t=1)=exp(eta_Y)/(1+exp(eta_Y)+exp(eta_D)), P(D_t=1)=exp(eta_D)/(1+exp(eta_Y)+exp(eta_D)), and assign the remaining probability to no event. Both events are absorbing. The two alpha_Y values are -9.35 and -6.95, intended to produce approximately 1% and 10% five-year primary-event risks under the comparator. The two alpha_D values are -9.40 and -7.00, intended to produce approximately 1% and 10% five-year competing-event risks. The large-sample truth calculation, rather than these approximate labels, supplies the analysis truth.
- Treatment effect: Observed baseline treatment follows A_0 ~ Bernoulli(expit(kappa(-0.75 + 0.8X + 0.6G + 0.8L_0))). For event-free people at t>=1, A_t ~ Bernoulli(expit(4.0(2A_(t-1)-1) + kappa(-0.75 + 0.7X + 0.6G + 0.9L_t))). The coefficient 4.0 creates realistic treatment persistence while permitting deviations. The overlap multiplier kappa controls measured confounding and weight-tail severity. The target strategies set every A_t to 1 or every A_t to 0 from baseline through the first event or month 60. On the monthly multinomial-logit scale, the direct primary-event treatment coefficient is log(0.75)=-0.287682 for G=0 and zero for G=1. Treatment also affects later outcomes indirectly by reducing later L_t. There is no direct A_t term in the competing-event equation.
- Effect modification: G defines a prespecified 25% subgroup. The direct monthly primary-event treatment odds ratio is 0.75 for G=0 and 1.00 for G=1 because the A_t by G coefficient is 0.287682. The marginal subgroup effect need not be exactly null in G=1 because treatment changes later L_t and survival. Its true value is therefore enumerated rather than assumed.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 2 retention levels by 3 overlap levels by 2 primary-event levels by 2 competing-event levels. Sequential exchangeability, consistency, and nonzero treatment probabilities are true. Both fitted treatment models have exactly the correct logistic form. Retention is independent and cannot introduce selection bias. There is no exposure or outcome measurement error and no natural loss to follow-up. Severe kappa values deliberately create heavy cumulative weights, but evidence for inferential failure will require matched comparisons with kappa=0; observing that the deliberately heavy weights have a low Kish effective sample size is not itself a positive finding.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot evaluate irregular visits, delayed data capture, dependent eligibility selection, unmeasured confounding, treatment-model misspecification, machine-learning nuisance fits, natural censoring, measurement error, site clustering, cross-site borrowing, structural positivity violations, weight truncation, modified treatment policies, Bayesian shrinkage, longitudinal TMLE, or inference for an unknown assignment mechanism. Its thresholds apply to this sequential-IPW estimator, protocol, horizon, and DGM family; they are not universal effective-sample thresholds for every estimator.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Eligibility and active-comparator retention | p_R=0.001; expected retained n=1000 from 1000000, p_R=0.004; expected retained n=4000 from 1000000 | These levels represent retaining 0.1% or 0.4% of a nominally large claims or EHR database after eligibility, new-user, and active-comparator restrictions. They create materially different independent-patient counts without changing the target population. |
| Treatment-overlap multiplier | kappa=0, kappa=1.25, kappa=2.25 | Kappa=0 is a negative-control treatment process with baseline probability 0.5 and history-independent persistence. Kappa=1.25 produces moderate history-dependent overlap. Kappa=2.25 permits baseline probabilities of approximately 0.03 to 0.96 and minimum monthly adherence probabilities near 0.68, producing severe but nonstructural near-positivity over sustained 60-month strategies. |
| Primary-event frequency | alpha_Y=-9.35; intended five-year risk near 1%, alpha_Y=-6.95; intended five-year risk near 10% | One percent represents a rare safety or effectiveness endpoint. Ten percent provides a common-event comparator while retaining the same confounding and effect structure. |
| Competing-event frequency | alpha_D=-9.40; intended five-year risk near 1%, alpha_D=-7.00; intended five-year risk near 10% | Varying the competing event independently distinguishes failure caused by few primary events from failure caused by depletion of the primary-event risk set through competing events. |

**Estimands.**

- **Primary marginal 60-month primary-event risk difference**: RD_Y(60)=P(Y under sustained A=1 occurs by month 60 before D)-P(Y under sustained A=0 occurs by month 60 before D), marginal over the postrestriction superpopulation. The protocol fixes eligibility at baseline, compares sustained use of the two active comparators beginning at baseline, treats D as a competing event rather than censoring, and follows people for 60 months. The scale is an absolute cumulative-incidence difference. *Truth: For each of the four unique alpha_Y by alpha_D combinations, simulate 2000000 postrestriction people under each sustained strategy. Use identical baseline draws and paired random-number streams across strategies, then estimate the risk difference and its Monte Carlo SE from the individual paired potential-outcome differences. Truth is shared across retention and kappa levels because neither changes the interventional distribution. Two million gives roughly 500000 G=1 people and makes truth error small relative to sampling SE. Add independent batches of 2000000 only if the stored Monte Carlo SE exceeds 0.0005 overall.*
- **Prespecified subgroup primary-event risk differences**: RD_Y,G=g(60)=P(Y under sustained A=1 by month 60 before D | G=g)-P(Y under sustained A=0 by month 60 before D | G=g), for g=0 and g=1. These are marginal within each superpopulation subgroup, not conditional regression coefficients. *Truth: Use the same paired 2000000-person truth simulations and calculate paired risk differences within G=0 and G=1. Add another independent 2000000-person batch if either subgroup truth Monte Carlo SE exceeds 0.001.*
- **Secondary horizon-specific primary-event risk differences**: The same marginal sustained-strategy primary-event risk difference at 12 and 36 months. These quantify how time-varying weight concentration and risk-set depletion accumulate before the primary 60-month horizon. *Truth: Record month-12 and month-36 cumulative incidences from the same large truth simulations.*
- **Competing-event risk differences**: RD_D(t)=P(D under sustained A=1 occurs by t before Y)-P(D under sustained A=0 occurs by t before Y), marginal in the postrestriction superpopulation at t=12, 36, and 60 months. *Truth: Record competing-event indicators and paired strategy differences in the same large truth simulations.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Estimated sequential-IPW with fixed-weight Wald inference | Fit a baseline logistic model A_0 on 1, X, G, and L_0 and one pooled postbaseline logistic model A_t on 1, A_(t-1), X, G, and L_t using all observed event-free person-months. For strategy g, let O_ig indicate that person i follows A_s=g through the first primary event, competing event, or month 60. Let pi_ig be the product of fitted probabilities of the observed compatible treatment decisions through that terminal time and q_ig=O_ig/pi_ig. For cause indicator Z_i, estimate mu_g=sum(q_ig Z_i)/sum(q_ig) and RD=mu_1-mu_0. Compute products on the log scale. Do not truncate weights. | Treat fitted weights as fixed. Define bar_q_g=n^(-1)sum(q_ig), phi_ig=q_ig(Z_i-mu_g)/bar_q_g, and phi_i=phi_i1-phi_i0. Estimate Var(RD) by [n/(n-2)] times sum((phi_i-mean(phi))^2)/[n(n-1)]. This is an individual-cluster HC1 sandwich and retains covariance between the two strategy contributions. | yes | Declare nonconvergence if either logistic fit has glm$converged false, is rank deficient, or has a nonfinite coefficient; any fitted treatment probability is outside [1e-8,1-1e-8]; any cumulative weight exceeds 1e12; either arm has a zero or nonfinite weight denominator; or the estimate or SE is nonfinite. A replicate with zero cause-specific events remains converged when the denominator exists; its zero estimate or degenerate interval must not be discarded. |
| Estimated sequential-IPW with nuisance-aware stacked sandwich inference | Use the same fitted probabilities and Hajek risk-difference estimator as the status quo method. | Stack, at the independent-patient level, the four baseline propensity-score equations, the five pooled longitudinal propensity-score equations summed over that patient's months, and the two risk equations q_ig(Z_i-mu_g)=0. With d=11 parameters, calculate A=-n^(-1)sum(dU_i/dtheta), B=n^(-1)sum(U_i U_i transpose), and V=[n/(n-11)]A^(-1)BA^(-T)/n. Use analytic logistic-score derivatives and d log(q_ig)/d gamma equal to the negative sum of compatible treatment log-likelihood score derivatives. Obtain the RD variance with contrast vector mu_1-mu_0. | no | Use all status quo nonconvergence conditions and additionally declare failure when the stacked bread has reciprocal condition number below 1e-12, the sandwich is not positive semidefinite within numerical tolerance 1e-10, or the RD variance is negative or nonfinite. Zero event counts are retained unless they make the matrix singular. |
| Estimated sequential-IPW with effective-cell t inference | Use the same Hajek estimator and estimated weights as the nuisance-aware method. | Use the same nuisance-aware stacked-sandwich variance. For each strategy g and binary endpoint value z, calculate ESS_gz=[sum(q_ig I(Z_i=z))]^2/sum(q_ig^2 I(Z_i=z)). Set nu=floor(min over g and z of ESS_gz)-1. | no | Use the nuisance-aware conditions. Also declare this interval unavailable when any endpoint cell has ESS_gz<=1 or nu<1. Record that failure separately rather than removing the replicate from other methods. |
| Oracle-probability sequential-IPW benchmark | Replace fitted treatment probabilities with the exact DGM probabilities while retaining the same Hajek estimator, artificial-censoring rule, horizons, and outcomes. | Use the fixed-weight individual-cluster HC1 variance formula from the status quo method. Because the probabilities are known, no nuisance-estimation score is included. | no | Declare failure only for a zero or nonfinite arm denominator, a cumulative weight above 1e12, or a nonfinite estimate or SE. Retain zero-event replicates. |

**Performance.** Primary: Conditional empirical coverage of the nominal 95% interval for the overall 60-month primary-event risk difference from estimated sequential-IPW with fixed-weight Wald inference. Coverage is calculated among converged replicates, while convergence and the unconditional probability of producing a covering interval are reported alongside it.. Secondary: Bias, empirical SE, mean model SE, relative error in model SE, MSE, conditional coverage, unconditional successful coverage, bias-eliminated coverage, rejection of H0: RD=0, and convergence, each with the existing ADEMP Monte Carlo SE., The same measures for nuisance-aware, effective-cell t, and oracle inference at 12, 36, and 60 months., Overall, G=0, and G=1 results for primary and competing-event risk differences., Within each scenario, the coverage difference between kappa=0 and each larger kappa at fixed retention and event intercepts., At every month and strategy: unweighted compatible risk-set count, cumulative event count, sum of weights, sum of squared weights, Kish ESS, ESS divided by retained n, maximum normalized weight, and the 99th-to-50th weight percentile ratio., At each horizon and strategy: cause-specific effective event count, effective non-event count, and minimum monthly arm-specific risk-set ESS., For each retention and event-frequency combination, the first tested kappa at which status quo coverage is below 90%; report the crossing as a bracket between adjacent kappa levels rather than inventing a continuous universal threshold., Scenario-level sensitivity and specificity of a prespecified warning flag. Flag a scenario when its replicate-median minimum monthly arm-specific risk-set ESS is below 100 or its replicate-median minimum cause-specific effective event count across strategies is below 20. Define a failing scenario as status quo coverage below 90%.. 4000 replicates. Target a Monte Carlo SE no greater than 0.005 for coverage at the decision boundary p=0.90. This requires m>=0.90(0.10)/(0.005^2)=3600 converged replicates. Allowing 5% nonconvergence requires 3600/0.95=3789.5 generated replicates, so use 4000 per scenario. With 3800 converged replicates, the coverage MCSE is 0.00487 at p=0.90 and 0.00354 at p=0.95.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: Declare the finite-information problem real within this DGM family if at least two scenarios, including at least one with expected postrestriction n=4000 and at least one containing a rare primary or rare competing event, have status quo coverage <=0.90, a 95% Wilson upper confidence limit below 0.925, convergence >=0.95, and either replicate-median minimum risk-set ESS below 25% of retained n or cause-specific effective event count below 20. Alternatively, declare an operational failure if convergence falls below 0.90 at kappa=2.25 while the matched kappa=0 scenario has convergence >=0.98 in at least two strata. Attribute failure specifically to the weight tail only if coverage drops by at least 0.05 from matched kappa=0 to kappa=1.25 or 2.25 in at least two strata whose kappa=0 coverage is >=0.93. Declare the fixed 100-or-20 reporting rule useful only if both sensitivity and specificity for scenario-level coverage failure are >=0.80.
- Problem is not real if: Declare no material failure within scope if all 24 status quo scenarios have convergence >=0.95 and coverage between 0.93 and 0.97, every matched kappa=0 to kappa=2.25 coverage decrease is below 0.02, and every matched convergence decrease is below 0.02. Failure of the 100-or-20 diagnostic by itself does not establish that the inferential problem is absent.
- Uninformative if: Call attribution uninformative if fewer than 3600 replicates converge in a cell without a matched weight-related numerical-failure signal; either stored truth has Monte Carlo SE above 0.0005 overall or 0.001 within subgroup; median minimum risk-set ESS differs by less than 20% between kappa=0 and kappa=2.25 in every matched stratum; or kappa=0 coverage is already below 0.90 in all matched strata, leaving no adequate negative control for a weight-tail comparison. Coverage from 0.90 through 0.93, a material failure confined to one scenario, or sensitivity or specificity from 0.60 through 0.79 is classified as inconclusive rather than forced into either conclusion.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The DGM deliberately creates severe weights, so simply demonstrating a small ESS would be circular. *Mitigation: Use kappa=0 as a negative control, require matched coverage or convergence changes, include moderate and severe dose levels, and base the conclusion on interval behavior rather than on whether the manipulated weight distribution became extreme.*
- Rare events alone can produce poor Wald behavior and could be mistaken for a weight-tail effect. *Mitigation: Cross primary-event and competing-event rarity independently with every overlap and retention level. Report matched rare-to-common and kappa=0-to-severe contrasts.*
- Finite-sample bias of the point estimator can masquerade as a variance-estimation failure. *Mitigation: Report ordinary and bias-eliminated coverage, estimator bias, oracle-weight performance, and model-SE versus empirical-SE comparisons.*
- Estimated weights alter variance, so a fixed-weight sandwich cannot identify whether the problem is weight concentration or ignored nuisance estimation. *Mitigation: Compare the conventional fixed-weight sandwich with the stacked nuisance-aware sandwich and the known-probability oracle benchmark using the same point-estimation structure.*
- A weight-only ESS is not the information size for every causal estimator. *Mitigation: Restrict all conclusions to the specified sequential-IPW estimator. Report independent-patient counts, risk-set counts, event counts, multiple weight diagnostics, and influence-based SE behavior alongside Kish ESS.*
- A post hoc ESS cutoff could be selected because it happens to classify these 24 scenarios. *Mitigation: Make 100 risk-set equivalents or 20 effective events the sole confirmatory diagnostic rule. Treat thresholds inferred from adjacent kappa cells as descriptive brackets and report all scenario-level points.*
- Correctly specified parametric treatment models make the simulation more favorable than applied analyses. *Mitigation: State this limitation prominently. The design isolates finite-information behavior under correct nuisance specification; failure here is strong evidence, while success cannot establish robustness to misspecification or flexible nuisance learning.*
- Independent retention understates changes in covariate composition caused by real eligibility and active-comparator restrictions. *Mitigation: Use retention only to manipulate the independent-patient count and make no selection-bias claim. Label every estimand as belonging to the postrestriction superpopulation.*
- Fixed monthly visits can conceal irregular-observation and visit-process bias. *Mitigation: Generate and analyze exactly monthly visits and explicitly exclude irregular observation from the scope.*
- Dropping zero-event or failed replicates would make coverage look better. *Mitigation: Retain zero-event replicates for methods that return an interval, report method-specific nonconvergence, and report both conditional coverage and the unconditional probability of producing a covering interval.*
- Numerical overflow could be confused with a statistical finding. *Mitigation: Accumulate probabilities and weights on the log scale, use the fixed 1e12 numerical-failure threshold, and separately report overflow, separation, singular-bread, and insufficient-effective-cell failures.*
- Large-sample Monte Carlo error in the truth can distort coverage in the largest retained cohorts. *Mitigation: Use paired strategy simulations, store truth Monte Carlo SEs, reuse only mathematically identical truths, and extend truth batches until the prespecified precision thresholds are met.*
- A 24-cell grid cannot establish a universal point where all asymptotic inference fails. *Mitigation: Report scenario-specific crossing brackets and a fixed diagnostic rule. Do not extrapolate beyond the simulated event frequencies, treatment process, follow-up horizon, or estimator.*

**Runtime as estimated.** The main experiment contains 24*4000=96000 replicates. Each replicate requires two treatment-probability glm fits, for approximately 192000 glm fits total; all four inference methods reuse those fits except the oracle benchmark. With 60 monthly cycles, six workers, chunked checkpointing, and no resampling or ensemble fitting inside a replicate, the projected run is approximately 6 to 12 hours. Truth requires only four distinct outcome-intercept combinations, not 24 scenario-specific enumerations. At the supplied estimate of four minutes per 2000000-person truth enumeration, the initial truth bill is about 16 minutes. Aggregation, diagnostic summaries, and figures should take less than one additional hour.

**Also bears on.** GMT-03 (answers-part)

**Cited, unverified.** Morris TP, White IR, Crowther MJ. Using simulation studies to evaluate statistical methods. Statistics in Medicine. 2019;38:2074-2102.; Robins JM, Hernan MA, Brumback B. Marginal structural models and causal inference in epidemiology. Epidemiology. 2000;11(5):550-560.; Austin PC, Stuart EA. Moving towards best practice when using inverse probability of treatment weighting using the propensity score to estimate causal treatment effects in observational studies. Statistics in Medicine. 2015;34(28):3661-3679.; Impact of near-positivity violations on IPTW-estimated marginal structural survival models with time-dependent covariates. Biometrical Journal. 2025.; Zhang, Bujkiewicz and Jackson. Effective sample size estimators for weighted analyses. BMC Medical Research Methodology. 2024.

## 17. ELG-02: Consequences and detection of healthcare-use collider eligibility in target trial emulation

*Catalog entry: Eligibility criteria built from healthcare-use variables can induce selection through colliders*

**Question.** Across 24 prespecified data-generating scenarios, by how many risk-difference points does restricting eligibility to a prebaseline healthcare-use variable bias the estimated 5-year effect of treatment when that variable is a collider, and can a temporally constrained advisory graph screen attain at least 80% sensitivity, at most 10% false-positive probability, and at least 50% reduction in absolute bias when a clinically authorized warning is acted upon?

**Aims.** Estimate the signed and absolute bias caused by conditioning on healthcare-use eligibility; distinguish collider bias from harmless conditioning on a treatment-side or outcome-side utilization predictor; measure warning sensitivity, specificity, and false-positive probability; quantify bias after warning-authorized removal of the criterion; quantify the resulting change in target population and true treatment effect; and determine whether conventional 95% confidence intervals reveal or conceal the bias.

**Data-generating mechanism.**

- Covariates: Each replicate contains an analysis cohort of 4,000 people and an independent protocol-design cohort of 4,000 people generated from the same superpopulation. For each person generate X1 ~ Bernoulli(0.50), X2 ~ Bernoulli(0.40), latent treatment propensity P ~ Bernoulli(0.50), and latent incipient disease U ~ Bernoulli(0.50), mutually independently. Generate recorded proxies Pstar and Ustar by independently recording each latent variable correctly with probability q and reversing it with probability 1-q. The criterion H denotes a qualifying visit or recorded laboratory value at month -1. Generate H ~ Bernoulli(pH), where pH = p + dP(P-0.5) + dU(U-0.5). This construction gives marginal Pr(H=1)=p exactly. At the most extreme setting, p=0.30 and dP=dU=0.25, the four probabilities for P,U equal to 00, 10, 01, and 11 are 0.05, 0.30, 0.30, and 0.55. Treatment is assigned at month 0 as A ~ Bernoulli(expit(-1.20 + 0.60X1 + 0.40X2 + 1.40P)); propensities range from 0.231 to 0.769. H therefore precedes A and cannot be caused by the treatment being emulated. X1 and X2 are measured confounders. P affects treatment but not outcome; U affects outcome but not treatment. P and U become associated after conditioning on H only in the collider scenarios.
- Outcome model: Let VY ~ Uniform(0,1), independent of all other variables. Define the potential 5-year cumulative-incidence outcome Y(a) = I[VY < 0.12 + 0.06X1 + 0.04X2 + 0.18U - 0.08a + 0.08aU], and observe Y=Y(A). All risks lie between 0.04 and 0.40. There is no censoring, competing event, treatment switching, missing outcome, or loss to follow-up.
- Treatment effect: On the risk-difference scale, treatment reduces 5-year risk by 0.08 when U=0 and has zero effect when U=1. The unrestricted superpopulation risk difference is therefore -0.040. Treatment assignment is observational, but the treatment strategies being contrasted are deterministic initiation and no initiation at month 0.
- Effect modification: U is the sole treatment-effect modifier. Among people with H=1, Pr(U=1|H=1)=0.50 for the treatment-side-only structure and 0.50+d/(4p) for the outcome-side-only and collider structures. Consequently, the exact H-eligible risk difference is -0.040 for treatment-side-only scenarios. For outcome-side-only and collider scenarios it is -0.03333, -0.02333, -0.03667, or -0.03167 for the respective combinations (p,d)=(0.30,0.10), (0.30,0.25), (0.60,0.10), and (0.60,0.25). This makes the estimand cost of removing H explicit rather than hiding it inside estimation error.
- Deliberately true: The design is fully factorial: 3 causal roles by 2 association strengths by 2 eligibility prevalences by 2 proxy accuracies. It deliberately makes P and U independent in the source population, makes X1 and X2 the only ordinary confounders, and makes H a collider in exactly 8 scenarios. Thus any additional treatment-outcome association after restricting on H has a known source. The 16 noncollider scenarios test whether the auditor merely reacts to healthcare-use variables. Effect modification deliberately makes removal of H change the true effect in outcome-side-only and collider scenarios.
- Cannot detect: The study cannot estimate how often collider eligibility occurs in real EHR or claims studies. It cannot assess irregular observation, post-treatment eligibility, descendants of colliders, recurrent treatment, time-varying confounding, adherence, censoring, competing events, differential missingness, expert agreement, or whether removing a clinically necessary criterion is acceptable. Complete outcome observation outside H=1 means it also cannot represent databases in which excluded people disappear from follow-up.

| factor | levels | why |
|---|---|---|
| Causal role of H | Treatment-side-only healthcare use: dP=d and dU=0, Outcome-side-only healthcare use: dP=0 and dU=d, Collider healthcare use: dP=d and dU=d | The two one-parent structures are negative controls. They make healthcare-use status insufficient by itself for a warning. The collider structure creates the path A <- P -> H <- U -> Y after restriction. |
| Eligibility association strength | Modest: d=0.10 risk-difference points in Pr(H=1) per binary parent, Strong: d=0.25 risk-difference points in Pr(H=1) per binary parent | These levels represent a weak administrative contribution and a strong contribution from treatment propensity or early disease. They are design-calibration values, not claims about a particular disease. |
| Eligibility prevalence | Restrictive criterion: Pr(H=1)=0.30, Broad criterion: Pr(H=1)=0.60 | The levels distinguish a criterion retaining a minority of the source population from one retaining a majority. They also vary the strength of Berkson dependence induced by conditioning while retaining adequate cell counts. |
| Recorded-proxy accuracy | Moderate information: sensitivity=specificity=0.70 for Pstar and Ustar, High information: sensitivity=specificity=0.90 for Pstar and Ustar | The levels test whether rich recorded covariates attenuate rather than eliminate selection through incompletely measured determinants. |

**Estimands.**

- **Original-protocol marginal 5-year risk difference**: Psi_H = E[Y(1)-Y(0) | H=1] for the superpopulation satisfying the month -1 healthcare-use criterion, comparing initiation at month 0 with no initiation and following everyone to month 60. *Truth: Compute exactly by enumerating the 16 combinations of X1, X2, P, and U, weighting each by its Bernoulli mass and Pr(H=1|P,U), and summing the two specified potential-outcome risks. No brute-force truth simulation is required. The exact values are listed in the effect-modification specification.*
- **Revised-protocol marginal 5-year risk difference**: Psi_all = E[Y(1)-Y(0)] in the unrestricted source superpopulation after the administrative H criterion is removed. The treatment strategies and 60-month horizon remain unchanged. *Truth: Exact enumeration over X1, X2, P, and U gives Psi_all=-0.040 in every scenario.*
- **Eligibility-induced estimand shift**: Delta_target = Psi_all-Psi_H. This is a protocol-population contrast, not estimator bias. *Truth: Subtract the exact enumerated values. Delta_target is zero in treatment-side-only scenarios and ranges from -0.00333 to -0.01667 in outcome-side-only and collider scenarios.*
- **Audit-policy estimand**: For an independently obtained warning D, Psi_D = D Psi_all + (1-D) Psi_H. Each replicate is evaluated against the truth corresponding to the protocol selected before its analysis cohort is examined. *Truth: Use the two exact truths and the replicate-specific external design-sample decision. Confidence intervals are conditional on the frozen protocol decision; warning uncertainty is evaluated separately through sensitivity and false-positive probability.*

**Methods.**

| method | point estimator | variance | status quo | non-convergence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Naive eligible-cohort contrast | Among H=1, subtract the observed mean of Y for A=0 from the observed mean for A=1. | Welch variance s1^2/n1+s0^2/n0. | no | Failure if the eligible cohort contains no treated or no untreated person, or if the estimate or standard error is nonfinite. |
| Criterion retained with conventional rich adjustment | Restrict to H=1. Fit the saturated linear probability model Y ~ A * X1 * X2 * Pstar * Ustar. Predict each eligible person's outcome under A=1 and A=0 and average the individual prediction differences. The saturation removes functional-form choices within the observed binary data while leaving residual confounding from imperfect proxies. | Use the empirical influence function for standardized ordinary least squares. If z_i is the fitted design row, z_i(a) is its intervention row, M is mean(z_i z_i transpose), and d_a is mean(z_i(a)), then IF_i(a)=z_i(a) transpose beta_hat-Psi_hat_a+d_a transpose M inverse z_i times (Y_i-z_i transpose beta_hat). The risk-difference influence function is IF_i(1)-IF_i(0), and the standard error is its sample standard deviation divided by the square root of the eligible sample size. | yes | Failure if the design matrix is rank deficient, any required observed stratum lacks one treatment arm, M cannot be inverted, or the estimate or standard error is nonfinite. |
| Temporally constrained advisory graph screen with authorized removal | Run the warning screen on the independent design cohort. If it warns and the criterion has been predeclared an administrative criterion that domain review permits removing, estimate Psi_all in the complete analysis cohort using the same saturated standardization model. Otherwise estimate Psi_H using the retained-criterion method. | For the four graph-screen logistic models use HC0 sandwich covariance, computed as inverse(X transpose W X) times X transpose diag((y-mu_hat)^2) X times inverse(X transpose W X). For the selected effect estimator use the standardized ordinary-least-squares influence function specified above. | no | Failure if any required logistic fit has separation, a singular information matrix, a nonfinite coefficient or sandwich standard error, or if the selected effect model meets its rank or finiteness failure rule. Failed screens are not silently converted into warnings or nonwarnings. |
| Blanket healthcare-use removal | Always remove H and estimate Psi_all in all 4,000 analysis-cohort members using saturated standardization over X1, X2, Pstar, and Ustar. | The standardized ordinary-least-squares influence function specified for the retained-criterion method. | no | Failure under the same rank, treatment-support, matrix-inversion, or finiteness rules as saturated standardization. |
| Oracle graph action | Remove H only when the scenario's true structure is collider; otherwise retain it. Reuse the corresponding saturated full-cohort or eligible-cohort estimate. | The standardized ordinary-least-squares influence function for the selected cohort. | no | The corresponding saturated estimator's failure rule. |
| Oracle same-target cause adjustment | Retain H=1 and fit the correctly specified model Y ~ X1+X2+P+U+A+A:U using the latent variables. Standardize over the H=1 population. This estimates the original Psi_H without changing eligibility. | The standardized ordinary-least-squares influence function using the oracle model design matrix. | no | Failure if the design matrix is rank deficient, M cannot be inverted, or the estimate or standard error is nonfinite. |

**Performance.** Primary: Signed bias of the conventional rich-adjustment estimate of Psi_H, reported in risk-difference points separately for every scenario; absolute bias is used for the prespecified decision threshold.. Secondary: Empirical standard error, Mean influence-function or Welch model standard error, Relative error in model standard error, Mean squared error, 95% interval coverage, Bias-eliminated coverage, Rejection rate for the null risk difference of zero, Convergence and usable-replicate proportion, Collider-warning sensitivity across the 8 collider scenarios, False-positive probability and specificity across the 16 noncollider scenarios, Positive predictive value of a warning for scenario-level absolute bias of at least 0.010, Absolute-bias reduction from the advisory action relative to retaining H, Oracle-versus-observed adjustment gap, Probability of changing the protocol, Exact eligibility-induced estimand shift. 2000 replicates. The smallest expected analyzed cohort has 4000 times 0.30=1200 people. Treatment propensity is bounded between 0.231 and 0.769, and Bernoulli outcome variance is at most 0.25. A conservative standard-deviation calculation for a treatment contrast is sqrt[(0.25/1200) times (1/0.231+1/0.231)]=0.0425, rounded upward to 0.043. To target a Monte Carlo standard error of at most 0.001 for bias requires R at least (0.043/0.001)^2=1849; this is rounded to 2000. The corresponding MCSE for nominal 95% coverage is sqrt(0.95 times 0.05/2000)=0.00487. All ADEMP measures and their MCSEs will be produced by the existing shared harness.

**Decision rule, written before the results exist.**

- Problem is real if: The collider-selection mechanism is declared consequential if at least 4 of the 8 collider scenarios have conventional rich-adjustment absolute bias of at least 0.010, the 95% Monte Carlo interval for signed bias lies wholly outside [-0.010,0.010], and the upper Monte Carlo confidence limit for coverage is below 0.90; no more than 2 of the 16 noncollider scenarios may meet both failure criteria; and oracle same-target adjustment must reduce absolute bias below 0.005 with coverage between 0.93 and 0.97. The prototype is declared operationally successful only if the lower 95% Wilson bound for sensitivity is at least 0.80, the upper 95% Wilson bound for false-positive probability is at most 0.10, and median within-target absolute bias across collider scenarios is reduced by at least 50% when authorized warnings are acted upon.
- Problem is not real if: The simulated mechanism is declared practically negligible over the prespecified range if at least 7 of the 8 collider scenarios have a 95% Monte Carlo interval for bias wholly inside [-0.010,0.010], coverage between 0.93 and 0.97, and less than 0.005 absolute-bias improvement from oracle same-target adjustment.
- Uninformative if: The result is uninformative for catalog resolution if neither numerical pattern holds; if more than 5% of intended replicates fail for any central method; if comparable failures occur in more than 10% of noncollider scenarios, indicating an estimator artifact; if the oracle same-target analysis does not recover bias below 0.005; or if the structural mechanism is consequential but the warning screen misses its sensitivity, false-positive, or bias-reduction threshold. A large Delta_target is not called estimation success or failure; it is reported as the clinical cost requiring domain judgment.

**Threats the design names against itself.**

- The most serious threat is circularity: the collider scenarios are constructed to contain a collider, so the simulation is guaranteed to generate some opened-path association. *Mitigation: Do not use the study to estimate real-world prevalence or prove that a particular criterion is a collider. Require a clinically meaningful 0.010 bias threshold, include 16 healthcare-use negative-control scenarios, vary association strength and eligibility prevalence, and interpret results as a conditional response surface. Empirical calibration and external validation remain necessary.*
- Removing H changes the target population and can appear to reduce error merely because the truth was silently changed. *Mitigation: Define Psi_H, Psi_all, and Delta_target before analysis. Score every estimate against the truth for its selected protocol. Also report oracle same-target adjustment, which retains H and therefore separates bias correction from estimand replacement.*
- An automated warning could become an automatic exclusion or could search for the eligibility choice producing the largest apparent treatment effect. *Mitigation: Use an independent design cohort, freeze the warning thresholds, mask the treatment-effect coefficient, and predeclare H removable on administrative grounds. The simulation evaluates a clinically authorized action; it does not authorize an algorithm to remove real criteria.*
- The auditor receives domain labels identifying treatment-side and outcome-side constructs, which may be wrong or unavailable in practice. *Mitigation: Call the result advisory, vary proxy accuracy, report an oracle ceiling, and do not claim causal certification. A later study must vary graph misspecification and domain-review disagreement.*
- A favorable result could arise from an outcome model selected to match the DGM. *Mitigation: Use a saturated observed-data model for all nonoracle methods, include noncollider controls, and use the correctly specified latent-variable model only as a labeled oracle benchmark. Publish cell counts, support diagnostics, and results from every scenario.*
- Sparse eligible strata can create apparent bias or unstable intervals. *Mitigation: Use N=4000, keep marginal eligibility at 0.30 or 0.60, bound treatment probabilities between 0.231 and 0.769, prohibit hidden extrapolation, classify rank or treatment-support failures as nonconvergence, and make a failure rate above 5% an uninformative result.*
- Nondifferential binary proxies are much simpler than real EHR measurement. *Mitigation: Use two accuracy levels and avoid extrapolating beyond them. The released results must label proxy accuracy on every plot and table. Differential measurement, missing-not-at-random recording, and continuous proxies require a separate extension.*
- Fixed timing and complete follow-up remove several mechanisms that coexist with healthcare-use selection. *Mitigation: State the narrow time order explicitly and make no claim about irregular visits, postbaseline eligibility, immortal time, censoring, competing risks, or longitudinal treatment.*
- Post hoc aggregation across 24 scenarios could turn isolated failures into a general claim. *Mitigation: Use the prespecified counts of affected collider and noncollider scenarios, numerical bias and coverage thresholds, and Monte Carlo confidence limits. Publish all scenario-level results regardless of direction.*

**Runtime as estimated.** There are 24 times 2000=48,000 replicates. Reusing estimates across policies requires four small logistic graph-screen fits and three small ordinary-least-squares effect fits per replicate, for 336,000 fitted models. The exact 16-cell truth enumeration is negligible and avoids the two-million-person truth bill. Relative to the supplied machine benchmark, the expected run is about 30 to 60 minutes on 6 workers; allowing two hours for saturated-model checks, serialization, and summaries is conservative and remains well within the overnight budget. No ltmle, SuperLearner, lmtp, concrete, CICI, Stan, or cmdstanr fit is needed.

**Also bears on.** ELG-02 (answers-part)
